  findings: The experiment demonstrates that damages rules analogous to civil litigation (compensatory, class action, treble damages) can deter free riding and promote cooperation in a repeated public good game. The baseline (no punishment) shows rapid decay of contributions and group payoffs. Compensatory damages (limited to individual harm) slow but do not prevent decay. Class action damages (based on group harm) and treble damages, when sufficiently likely or severe, stabilize contributions and maintain higher group payoffs. The effect is not due to the theoretical threat of punishment, but to participants' actual experience of being punished. The option for the punisher to forfeit rather than retain damages (i.e., to punish without personal gain) is rarely used and does not affect outcomes. Thus, the deterrent effect of damages is robust to whether the punisher benefits personally. The findings suggest that for punishment to sustain cooperation and group welfare, it must be sufficiently severe and/or likely, and that actual experience with sanctions is more important than their mere possibility.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (damages) in a repeated public good game increases group efficiency (payoff) relative to the no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is severe (treble damages) or based on group harm (class action) and is likely to be imposed. The effect is not simply on average contribution, but on the stability of cooperation and group payoffs over time. For prediction tasks, the presence and design of punishment (severity, scope, probability) are key moderators of efficiency gains. Control efficiency (baseline group payoff) is improved by adding punishment, but the magnitude depends on the punishment regime. The paper also shows that the actual experience of being punished is a stronger driver than the theoretical threat, suggesting that models should account for realized, not just expected, punishment. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with repeated interaction, group feedback, and endogenous punishment.

- source: Dal Bó, E., & Dal Bó, P. (2014). Do the right thing: The effects of moral suasion on cooperation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a series of lab experiments using a two-player linear public goods game, the authors show that (1) moral suasion (exposure to a moral message) increases contributions and group payoffs, but the effect is short-lived when punishment is not available; (2) when a punishment stage is added (where players can pay to reduce their partner's payoff), the effect of moral suasion becomes persistent, with higher and sustained contributions and higher group payoffs; (3) punishment alone increases contributions compared to no-punishment, but does not prevent a decline over time; (4) the combination of punishment and moral suasion produces the highest and most persistent cooperation and payoffs; (5) the mechanisms include both changes in expectations about others and changes in preferences. The paper does not report efficiency as a ratio, but does report group payoffs and their changes, which are closely related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player linear public goods game with random rematching, adding a punishment stage (with a cost to the punisher and a larger cost to the punished) increases group payoffs and sustains higher cooperation, especially when combined with moral suasion. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive, and the effect is amplified when moral suasion is present. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, etc.) are well specified, making the results directly relevant for predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs with and without punishment. The paper is less informative about settings with more players, field contexts, or different punishment technologies, but is highly informative for standard lab PGGs.

- source: Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. (2016). Partner choice versus punishment in human Prisoner's Dilemmas. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four studies, the paper finds that (1) punishers are rated as more trustworthy and group focused than nonpunishers; (2) after repeated PGG rounds, punishers are trusted more and receive more money in trust games, but this effect is absent after a single round; (3) justified punishment (punishing free riders) leads to increased trust and monetary benefits, while unjustified punishment leads to reduced trust and earnings; (4) the reputational and monetary benefits of justified punishment can offset or even exceed the cost of punishing, especially in repeated interactions. The paper does not directly report group efficiency ratios, but does show that punishment can increase individual and group monetary outcomes via increased trust and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated PGGs with punishment, justified punishment can increase the monetary benefits to punishers via enhanced trust and subsequent transfers, partially or fully offsetting the cost of punishment. The effect is contingent on repeated interaction (number of rounds) and the justification for punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in repeated PGGs (with visible identities and reputation) is likely to increase group efficiency and individual payoffs, especially when punishment is justified and recognized as such. In single-round games, these benefits do not materialize. The paper does not provide direct group efficiency ratios, but the monetary outcomes and trust game results are closely related and informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Muthukrishna, M., Francois, P., Pourahmadi, S., & Henrich, J. (2017). Corrupting cooperation and how anti-corruption strategies may backfire. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that allowing bribery in an institutional punishment public goods game (IPGG) substantially reduces public good provisioning (by about 25%). In the standard IPGG (control), stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) increase contributions and public good provisioning. However, when bribery is possible (bribery game, BG), stronger leaders actually decrease public good provisioning, as they use their power to extract bribes rather than enforce cooperation. Anti-corruption strategies (partial/full transparency, leader investment) can increase contributions in some contexts (strong leaders or high economic potential), but may have no effect or even reduce contributions further in contexts with weak leaders and poor economic potential. The main outcome is mean contribution to the public good, which is a close proxy for efficiency. The results show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be reversed or undermined by corruption opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the presence of corruption opportunities (e.g., bribery) can reverse the usual positive effect of institutional punishment on efficiency. The effect of punishment depends critically on whether leaders can be corrupted, the strength of the leader's punitive power, and the economic potential (mpcr). In standard IPGGs, stronger punishment increases efficiency, but in bribery-enabled games, stronger punishment can decrease efficiency. Anti-corruption interventions (transparency, leader investment) can help, but only under certain structural conditions. When using this paper for prediction, it is important to consider whether the game design allows for corruption/bribery, the strength of punishment, and the economic context. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported directly, but the relative changes are clear: punishment increases efficiency in standard IPGG, but decreases it when bribery is possible.

- source: Hetzer, M., & Sornette, D. (2013). The Co-Evolution of Fairness Preferences and Costly Punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulation model, based on the structure of standard public goods games with punishment (n=4, mpcr=0.4, punishment cost: 1 MU spent removes 3 MUs from target), shows that when agents evolve under disadvantageous inequity aversion, they develop a stable, empirically-matched propensity to punish defectors (k≈0.25). This level of punishment is sufficient to sustain high cooperation and maximize group fitness/wealth. The model finds that disadvantageous inequity aversion is evolutionarily stable and dominates other preference types, leading to higher average wealth, lower volatility, and longer agent lifetimes. While the model does not report efficiency in the strict sense (group payoff as a fraction of maximum possible), it provides strong evidence that punishment under disadvantageous inequity aversion increases group welfare and evolutionary success compared to self-regarding or non-punishing populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in a standard public goods game with punishment (n=4, mpcr=0.4, punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment leads to higher group welfare and evolutionary success, provided agents have disadvantageous inequity aversion. The model is calibrated to match empirical punishment levels and shows that these levels are sufficient to sustain high cooperation and maximize group fitness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency (group payoff/welfare) relative to the control. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative efficiency ratios, so the evidence is most useful for qualitative or directional prediction, especially in settings matching the model's design parameters.

- source: Abdallah, S., Sayed, R., Rahwan, I., LeVeck, B. L., Cebrian, M., Rutherford, A., & Fowler, J. H. (2014). Corruption drives the emergence of civil society. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with both peer and centralized punishment, the presence of corruption undermines the effectiveness of strong centralized punishment. When centralized punishment is severe and corruption is possible, corruptors exploit the system, leading to lower cooperation and more defectors. Weaker centralized punishment allows peer-punishers to maintain cooperation even in the presence of corruption. Introducing a hybrid strategy (paying both the pool and peer-punishing) can maintain high cooperation across all levels of centralized punishment. The model suggests that a mix of centralized and decentralized enforcement is necessary for robust cooperation, especially in the face of corruption.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency (or cooperation) in public goods games depends critically on the structure of punishment (peer, pool, hybrid), the possibility of corruption, and the severity of centralized punishment. In environments where only centralized punishment is available and corruption is possible, increasing punishment severity can reduce efficiency by enabling corruptors to exploit the system. Allowing peer or hybrid punishment can mitigate this effect and restore cooperation. For prediction, the presence of corruption, the type of punishment available, and the severity of punishment are key moderators. The findings suggest that simply enabling (strong) punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the institutional context and the possibility of corruption must be considered.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Vasconcelos, V. V., & Santos, F. C. (2014). Climate change governance, cooperation and self-organization. *Physics of Life Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing local (group-level) sanctioning institutions (punishment) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss significantly increases the fraction of groups that successfully achieve the public good, especially under low risk perception and high contribution thresholds. Global (population-wide) sanctioning institutions provide only marginal improvement. Smaller group sizes and higher risk perception also promote higher group achievement. Threshold uncertainty (uncertainty about the required contribution level) can sharply reduce group achievement. The model suggests that decentralized, polycentric punishment is more effective than centralized punishment for promoting cooperation and group success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (specifically, local/group-level sanctioning institutions) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss will increase group achievement (a close proxy for efficiency), especially when group size is small, risk perception is high, and contribution thresholds are stringent. The effect of punishment is much stronger when implemented locally rather than globally. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local punishment should be expected to yield a more positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes than global punishment or no punishment, particularly in small groups and under high risk. However, the main outcome is group achievement, not exact efficiency, so predictions should be interpreted accordingly.

- source: Noussair, C. N., & van Soest, D. P. (2014). Economic Experiments and Environmental Policy. *Annual Review of Resource Economics, Vol 6*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: chat, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews laboratory experimental evidence on public goods games and related social dilemmas, focusing on mechanisms to promote cooperation and efficiency. It finds that allowing peer punishment and/or rewards generally increases cooperation and can improve group payoffs, but the effect on efficiency is not universal and depends on implementation details (e.g., possibility of counter-punishment, cultural context). Communication between players robustly increases efficiency. Top-down interventions (such as fines or minimum contributions) can crowd out voluntary cooperation, sometimes reducing overall welfare. In market-based resource allocation, auction and trading mechanisms can achieve high efficiency, but are affected by strategic behavior, market power, and the presence or absence of endowment effects. The review highlights that the design of the game and institutions (e.g., whether players can undo others' contributions, whether the game is framed as giving or taking) strongly moderates the effect of punishment and other interventions on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides broad, high-level evidence that punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency in public goods games, but the effect is highly context-dependent. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when (a) group composition and identity are stable, (b) counter-punishment is not possible, and (c) the cultural context supports prosocial punishment. However, the review also warns that punishment can fail to improve efficiency or even reduce it if retaliation is possible or if the strategic environment allows for undoing others' contributions. Communication is a more robust positive moderator of efficiency. The review does not provide quantitative effect sizes but offers qualitative guidance on when punishment is likely to be effective. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be assumed to always increase efficiency; moderators such as communication, game framing, and the possibility of counter-punishment must be considered.

- source: Parks, C. D., Joireman, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Cooperation, Trust, and Antagonism: How Public Goods Are Promoted. *Psychological Science in the Public Interest*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward mechanisms are effective at promoting cooperation in public goods games and similar social dilemmas, with meta-analytic evidence indicating that their effects on cooperation are roughly equal and of medium size. The presence of punishment opportunities leads to higher contributions to public goods over time, and thus to higher group efficiency and collective welfare. The effectiveness of punishment is moderated by factors such as trust (more effective in high-trust societies), group size (less effective in large groups), and the iterated nature of the game (more effective in repeated interactions). The review also notes potential downsides of punishment, such as undermining intrinsic motivation or evoking retaliation, but concludes that, overall, structural solutions like punishment are robust tools for increasing cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper should inform prediction by reinforcing that, across a wide range of public goods game designs, enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms generally increases group efficiency and collective welfare compared to control conditions without such mechanisms. The effect is robust across many studies and is supported by meta-analytic evidence. However, the magnitude of the effect may depend on moderators such as group size, trust, and the iterated nature of the game. The review provides indirect evidence for the predictive value of design dimensions such as punishment cost, identifiability, communication, and group size. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially in high-trust, small-group, iterated settings.

- source: Milinski, M. (2016). Reputation, a universal currency for human social interactions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that punishment in public goods games can maintain high levels of cooperation, but often at a net cost due to the expenses of punishing and being punished. Reputation mechanisms, such as indirect reciprocity, can also sustain cooperation and do so more efficiently by avoiding these costs. When both punishment and reputation mechanisms are available, punishment is used less frequently and more selectively, and overall group efficiency is increased. The review also notes that reward mechanisms are generally less effective than punishment in sustaining cooperation. The findings are based on a synthesis of multiple experimental studies, with particular emphasis on the interaction between punishment and reputation in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games generally increases cooperation and can improve group efficiency, especially when combined with reputation mechanisms. However, punishment alone may not always increase efficiency due to its costs. The review provides indirect evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the presence of reputation systems and the ability to observe or communicate about others' actions. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and is maximized when reputation mechanisms are also present. The review does not provide new quantitative estimates but synthesizes qualitative and comparative findings from the literature.

- source: Peysakhovich, A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Habits of Virtue: Creating Norms of Cooperation and Defection in the Laboratory. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Exposure to environments that support cooperation (via repeated PDs with high continuation probability and favorable payoffs) leads to higher prosociality in subsequent one-shot games, including the public goods game, trust game, dictator game, and ultimatum game. In a second experiment, exposure to cooperative environments increases third-party punishment of selfishness and reduces antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment. These effects are strongest among subjects who rely on intuitive (heuristic) decision-making. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is not assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional environments can shape prosocial and punishment behaviors in public-goods-game-like settings, and that these behaviors spill over into new contexts. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency. The design details (e.g., group size, punishment cost/tech, MPCR) are reported and can inform mapping to other studies, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Gürerk, Irlenbusch, B., & Rockenbach, B. (2014). On cooperation in open communities. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that when subjects can self-select into a punishment community (VF-PUN), average contributions are much higher than in a reward community (VF-REW) or in exogenously assigned punishment communities (EX-PUN, FIX-PUN). The effect is attributed to self-selection rather than slow growth of the community. The presence of a punishment option is critical for high cooperation, and adding reward does not further improve outcomes. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; all main results are in terms of contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that self-selection into punishment-enabled public goods games leads to higher cooperation (contribution) rates than exogenous assignment or reward-based institutions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher contributions likely imply higher efficiency, but actual efficiency or group payoff is not reported. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, so the paper is useful for mapping design to expected cooperation, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: dos Santos, M., & Wedekind, C. (2015). Reputation based on punishment rather than generosity allows for evolution of cooperation in sizable groups. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper shows that in simulated public goods games, a reputation system based on punitive actions (punishment scoring) allows for the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation frequencies in large groups, even when errors in reputation assessment are present. In contrast, reputation based on generosity (contributions) or rewards is much less effective: generosity-based reputation rarely leads to cooperation in large groups, and reward-based systems are unstable due to invasion by non-rewarders. The model assumes binary contribution and punishment choices, with payoffs used for evolutionary selection, but does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that punishment-based reputation mechanisms are more effective than generosity- or reward-based reputation for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, especially as group size increases and errors in reputation assessment become more likely. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be interpreted as supporting the likely positive effect of punishment on efficiency in similar game designs, especially in large groups and error-prone environments. The model assumes all-or-nothing contribution and punishment choices, no communication, and full observability of actions (except for errors in reputation memory). The results are most informative for predicting the effect of punishment in large, anonymous, repeated PGGs with reputation tracking, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency.

- source: Paál, T., & Bereczkei, T. (2015). Punishment as a Means of Competition: Implications for Strong Reciprocity Theory. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 10-round, 4-player PGG with a competitive, rank-based payoff and costly punishment enabled from round 6 onward, average contributions declined steadily and did not increase after punishment was introduced. Punishment was not targeted at low contributors but at those with higher accumulated profits, indicating strategic rather than norm-enforcing use. There was no association between contributions and punishment received or imposed. The results suggest that in competitive environments, punishment can become a tool for rivalry rather than for sustaining cooperation, and does not reverse the decline in cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a PGG with a competitive, tournament-style payoff structure and full information about others' profits, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. Instead, punishment is used for strategic competition, and cooperation continues to decline. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent: in competitive, rank-based settings, punishment may not improve and may even undermine group efficiency. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so this inference is based on contribution behavior and the described use of punishment.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Brandts, J., Nardin, L. G., Sabater-Mir, J., & Conte, R. (2014). The Norm-Signaling Effects of Group Punishment: Combining Agent-Based Simulation and Laboratory Experiments. *Social Science Computer Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when punishment is inflicted by the entire group, human subjects are more likely to cooperate (i.e., contribute to the public good) than when punishment is uncoordinated, even though the material cost to defectors is held constant. The authors attribute this to the norm-signaling function of group punishment, which is hypothesized to activate both instrumental and normative motivations for compliance. Agent-based simulations support the idea that norm salience, as modeled in the EMIL-I-A architecture, can explain the observed human behavior. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (contribution rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of punishment (specifically, whether it is coordinated by the group or uncoordinated) can significantly affect cooperation rates in public goods games, even when the material incentives are held constant. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that group punishment may lead to higher efficiency than uncoordinated punishment, but this is inferred from contribution rates rather than directly measured. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of group punishment structure and norm-signaling on cooperation, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency when combined with other sources.

- source: Pfattheicher, S., Keller, J., & Knezevic, G. (2017). Sadism, the Intuitive System, and Antisocial Punishment in the Public Goods Game. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The studies show that activating the intuitive system increases antisocial punishment among individuals with high sadism, while inhibiting intuition reduces it. This effect is specific to antisocial punishment and not to punishment of defectors or to contribution behavior. The presence of punishment in the game enables antisocial punishment, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological moderators of antisocial punishment but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and cognitive moderators of antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, but it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is only indirectly informative: it confirms that antisocial punishment can occur in standard PGGs with punishment, and that its frequency is affected by individual differences and cognitive processing mode. However, it does not quantify the impact of punishment on efficiency or provide control vs. treatment efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper can help confirm the mapping of design features, but not the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Kubena, A. A., Houdek, P., Lindová, J., Príplatová, L., & Flegr, J. (2014). Justine Effect: Punishment of the Unduly Self-Sacrificing Cooperative Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a 12-player PGG, the introduction of punishment leads to a U-shaped relationship between contribution and punishment received: both the lowest and highest contributors are punished, with the most severe punishment falling on the highest contributors (the 'Justine Effect'). Antisocial punishment is primarily carried out by free riders and low contributors. While the paper reports that average contributions nearly double when punishment is available, it does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, nor does it compare these outcomes between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The focus is on the behavioral dynamics of punishment, not on payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral dynamics of punishment in large-group PGGs, especially regarding antisocial punishment and its targets. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, which are central to the downstream prediction task. The finding that punishment increases average contributions suggests a possible positive effect on efficiency, but the presence of antisocial punishment (which reduces the payoffs of high contributors) could offset these gains. Without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the paper's value for predicting treatment efficiency is limited to indirect inference based on contribution patterns and punishment behavior.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Nettle, D., & McElreath, R. (2015). Interactions between personality and institutions in cooperative behaviour in humans. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the opportunity for decentralized punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions to the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect is especially pronounced for individuals high in extraversion, who tend to free-ride in the absence of punishment but increase their contributions when punishment is possible. Agreeableness is associated with higher initial contributions regardless of institution, while neuroticism is associated with lower contributions in both conditions. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in contributions with punishment suggests a likely increase in efficiency, subject to the cost of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions, particularly among certain personality types (notably extraverts). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be quantified from this paper alone. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the punishment mechanism is standard (costly, decentralized, 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio). The findings support the general prediction that punishment increases cooperation, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes for the downstream task.

- source: Wu, J. J., Li, C., Zhang, B. Y., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2014). The role of institutional incentives and the exemplar in promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Institutional punishment increases average group contributions above control and reward-only treatments, and the combination of reward and punishment (IRP) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The effect is robust across three incentive schemes (fixed, increasing, decreasing). The study finds that individuals react more strongly to punishment than to reward, especially among high contributors. The presence of 'cooperating-individuals' (exemplars) in a group is associated with higher average contributions, as conformists tend to follow their lead. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff, but the design and behavioral findings are highly relevant for understanding how institutional punishment affects cooperation in repeated PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional punishment (and especially combined reward and punishment) increases average group contributions in repeated PGGs with four players and 50 rounds. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the contribution results suggest that punishment is likely to improve efficiency relative to control, especially when combined with reward. The experimental design and manipulation of incentive schemes are informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG settings, but direct quantitative prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone due to the lack of payoff/efficiency reporting.

- source: Weng, Q., & Carlsson, F. (2015). Cooperation in teams: The role of identity, punishment, and endowment distribution. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) strong identity (via human knot) increases contribution rates in heterogeneous teams without punishment, but not in homogeneous teams; (2) strong identity (via online chat) increases contribution rates in homogeneous teams; (3) punishment increases contribution rates when punishment is effective (1:3), even under strong identity, but not when punishment is less effective (1:2) and identity is strong; (4) strong identity does not further enhance contributions or punishment when punishment is available; (5) punishment of deviations from the team average does not depend on the target's endowment. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (as measured by contribution rate) depends on the effectiveness of punishment and the strength of identity-building. When punishment is effective (punishment impact per unit cost is high), it increases contributions even under strong identity. When punishment is less effective and identity is strong, punishment does not increase contributions. However, the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, for similar public good games, enabling effective punishment is likely to increase efficiency (by increasing contributions), but the effect may be moderated by identity-building and punishment effectiveness. The absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Vollan, B., Landmann, A., Zhou, Y. X., Hu, B. L., & Herrmann-Pillath, C. (2017). Cooperation and authoritarian values: An experimental study in China. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent punishment for under-contribution, exogenously imposed punishment rules (authoritarian/top-down) significantly increase average contributions compared to no law or endogenously chosen (democratic) punishment rules. This effect is strongest among participants (especially workers) with high deference to authority, and weaker or absent among those with low deference to authority (especially students). The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but since the game is linear and contributions are the main outcome, higher contributions imply higher efficiency. There is no evidence of a democracy premium in this Chinese sample; instead, authoritarian implementation is more effective at increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear PGG with a mild, non-deterrent punishment, enabling punishment via exogenous (authoritarian) imposition increases average contributions (and thus efficiency) compared to no punishment. The effect is context-dependent: in this Chinese sample, exogenous punishment is more effective than democratic (endogenous) punishment, especially among those with high deference to authority. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on cultural context and the method of rule implementation (exogenous vs. endogenous). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Punishment, and Cooperation Across 18 Societies: A Meta-Analysis. *Perspectives on Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase cooperation in public goods games, with a moderate average effect (d=0.77, 95% CI [0.63, 0.91]), but with substantial cross-societal variation. The effect of punishment is significantly stronger in high-trust societies. Methodological moderators show that punishment is more effective in partner-matching protocols (fixed groups) and with more iterations (rounds), but not significantly affected by group size. Other societal variables (GDP, democracy, market competition, religious participation, norms of civic cooperation) have weaker or non-significant effects after controlling for trust. The outcome measured is behavioral cooperation/contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the standardized Fehr & Gächter public goods game, enabling peer punishment increases cooperation rates, especially in high-trust societies. However, the outcome is contribution/cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone. The findings suggest that, for lab PGGs with 3-7 players, 6-50 rounds, and standard costly peer punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MUs), punishment increases cooperation, with larger effects in partner-matching and longer games. The effect on efficiency is likely positive if higher cooperation translates to higher group payoffs, but this is not directly measured. The paper is highly informative for predicting behavioral cooperation under different game designs and societal contexts, but less so for direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bell, A., Zhang, W., & Nou, K. (2016). Pesticide use and cooperative management of natural enemy habitat in a framed field experiment. *Agricultural Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment uses a one-shot public goods game with three treatments: (1) Base (no punishment), (2) UCPun (conditional decision maker can be punished by an unconditional decision maker), and (3) CPun (conditional decision maker can punish an unconditional decision maker). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), but does lead to more sensitive punishment behavior. Responsibility effects (increased cooperation when given punishment power) are stronger than substitution effects. The overall effect of punishment on cooperation is positive, but the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment mechanisms affect conditional cooperation in a one-shot public goods game with four players, MPCR 0.4, and costless punishment. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that enabling punishment increases the responsiveness of cooperators to others' contributions and may increase overall cooperation, but also increases free riding. For prediction tasks, the results imply that punishment is likely to increase cooperation-related behaviors, but the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous due to the rise in free riders. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to quantitatively predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency, but the behavioral mechanisms identified are informative for modeling.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2016). Exploring an Effective Incentive System on a Groupware. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of public-goods-like meta-sanction games, systems with first-order punishment and appropriate second-order sanctions (such as punishing non-punishers or rewarding punishers) can achieve and robustly sustain high cooperation rates. In contrast, systems that allow first-order rewards without second-order incentives for rewarding behavior can actually impede the formation of cooperative regimes. The cooperation rate is highly sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio of sanctions and the discount factor for higher-order sanctions. The results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the structure of the punishment and reward system (including the presence or absence of second-order incentives) strongly affects the cooperation rate in public-goods-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (with appropriate cost/benefit ratios and possibly second-order sanctions) can increase cooperation rates, but the translation to efficiency is not quantified. The results also warn that adding rewards without second-order incentives can reduce cooperation, which may imply lower efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Baldassarri, D. (2015). Cooperative Networks: Altruism, Group Solidarity, Reciprocity, and Sanctioning in Ugandan Producer Organizations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both communication and the threat of sanctioning increase contributions in a repeated PGG compared to baseline. Communication (reciprocity) has a stronger effect than punishment. However, only cooperation in the communication condition predicts real-life cooperative behaviors in the farmer organizations. The threat of sanctioning increases contributions in the lab, but this does not translate to real-world cooperation among council members. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in small, cohesive groups with repeated interaction, communication (reciprocity) is a more effective and relevant mechanism for promoting cooperation than punishment. While punishment increases contributions in the lab PGG, it does not predict real-world cooperative behavior in this context. The absence of efficiency or payoff data means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it suggests that in similar settings, punishment may not translate into real-world cooperative gains. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that communication/reciprocity is a key moderator, and that punishment's effect may be context-dependent and less robust in small, high-trust groups.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Lun, J. (2015). Societal threat and cultural variation in the strength of social norms: An evolutionary basis. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the PGG with punishment, higher societal threat leads to higher rates of cooperation and a greater proportion of norm-enforcing punishers. Under low threat, cooperation and norm enforcement are weaker. The presence of punishment is necessary to achieve high cooperation under threat; spatial structure alone is insufficient. The model also shows that norm strength (adherence and enforcement) increases rapidly in response to increased threat and declines gradually when threat is removed. While payoffs are modeled, the main reported outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement in PGG-like environments, especially under higher threat. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment enabled/disabled, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The model parameters (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) are specified, and the model is robust to parameter changes, but the main outcomes are behavioral. The paper supports the qualitative prediction that enabling punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement, which is likely to increase efficiency, but does not provide direct efficiency data for prediction tasks.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Rong, Z. H. (2015). Mutual punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study uses agent-based simulations of a spatial public goods game on a square lattice, where each player participates in five overlapping groups. The key manipulation is the introduction of a symmetric, mutual punishment: whenever two neighbors have different strategies, both pay a punishment fine. The main result is that higher punishment fines lead to higher steady-state cooperation rates, and for sufficiently high fines, the population becomes fully cooperative. The mechanism is robust to changes in system size and noise. The paper provides a formula for the payoff difference between cooperators and defectors at the interface between clusters, showing that this difference increases with the punishment fine, favoring the spread of cooperation. However, the paper does not report group-level efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mutual punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially as the punishment fine increases. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not efficiency. The model is theoretical and does not include communication, reward, or other institutional features. The punishment is symmetric and always applied when strategies differ, which is a specific variant of punishment not always present in experimental or real-world settings.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2015). Group Cooperation without Group Selection: Modest Punishment Can Recruit Much Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulations demonstrate that when punishment is possible and can recruit labor (i.e., induce free riders to cooperate in the future), even modest levels of punishment are sufficient to sustain high levels of cooperation in public goods games. The model relaxes several assumptions of prior models, allowing for probabilistic punishment, continuous cooperation probabilities, individual recognition, and group mixing. The results show that both punishment and cooperation can evolve by individual selection, even in large groups, and that second-order free riders do not prevent the evolution of cooperation. However, the outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation and punishment rates), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in public goods games can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is probabilistic and can recruit labor. The findings suggest that game designs allowing for individual recognition and repeated interactions (mixing ecologies) will see higher cooperation supported by lower punishment rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. Its value is in supporting the mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar empirical designs, but this is not directly quantified here.

- source: Stagnaro, M. N., Arechar, A. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). From good institutions to generous citizens: Top-down incentives to cooperate promote subsequent prosociality but not norm enforcement. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG (with 3 players, 10 rounds, and varying inspection probabilities) leads to a substantial increase in contribution rates compared to a no-punishment control. This institutional exposure also increases subsequent prosociality in a Dictator Game, but does not directly increase punishment behavior in a Third-Party Punishment Game. The effect on punishment is only indirect, mediated by increased prosociality. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG increases contribution rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly. The design dimensions (player count, number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the manipulation of punishment is clear. However, for the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper only provides indirect support via contribution rates. It does not allow direct estimation of efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that even minimal punishment is sufficient to substantially increase cooperation, and that further increases in punishment probability do not yield additional gains in contribution. The absence of direct efficiency data is a limitation for prediction.

- source: Shimao, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2013). Strict or Graduated Punishment? Effect of Punishment Strictness on the Evolution of Cooperation in Continuous Public Goods Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effectiveness of punishment strictness in promoting cooperation depends on the population structure. In spatially structured populations (lattice), strict punishment (high strictness parameter) leads to higher evolved cooperation levels than graduated punishment. In random-matching (well-mixed) populations, graduated punishment (low strictness parameter) is more effective at increasing cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameters for public goods efficiency and punishment effectiveness. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the average cooperation level as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) is moderated by the population structure: strict punishment is more effective in spatially structured settings, while graduated punishment is more effective in well-mixed settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension corresponding to population structure (e.g., fixed groups vs. random rematching) is a key moderator of punishment's effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as informative about contribution/cooperation rates, which are closely related but not identical to efficiency.

- source: Kaiping, G. A., Jacobs, G. S., Cox, S. J., & Sluckin, T. J. (2014). Nonequivalence of updating rules in evolutionary games under high mutation rates. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with punishment, the choice of updating rule (BP, DP, LD) can dramatically change the long-run frequencies of defectors, cooperators, and punishers. For the same parameter values, the birth-death process leads to a population dominated by defectors, while the death-birth process results in a majority of cooperative strategies (cooperators and punishers). Link dynamics yields intermediate results. These differences arise even in well-mixed populations and are especially pronounced when mutation rates are not negligible and when more than two strategies interact. The paper does not report group efficiency or payoff, but the shift from defector to cooperator/punisher dominance implies a likely increase in group welfare under some updating rules.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the updating rule (a feature of evolutionary dynamics, not typically manipulated in lab experiments) can be a major moderator of the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by implication, on efficiency. In public goods games with punishment, the same game design (including punishment parameters) can yield very different long-run outcomes depending on the updating rule: punishment may be ineffective (defector-dominated) under one rule and highly effective (cooperator/punisher-dominated) under another. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as indicating that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the evolutionary dynamics assumed, especially in theoretical or agent-based models. For empirical prediction, this suggests caution in generalizing across studies with different evolutionary assumptions.

- source: Chen, X. J., Sasaki, T., & Perc, M. (2015). Evolution of public cooperation in a monitored society with implicated punishment and within-group enforcement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of implicated punishment (where all group members are fined if a defector is detected) can transform the public goods game into a coordination game, making both full cooperation and full defection stable equilibria. Increasing the monitoring probability, the implicated punishment fine, or the within-group enforcement probability all enlarge the basin of attraction for full cooperation, thus promoting higher cooperation rates. Group size has a nonlinear effect: intermediate group sizes are least conducive to cooperation, while both small and large group sizes favor cooperation. The addition of within-group enforcement (peer punishment) further relaxes the conditions for cooperation to thrive. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but higher cooperation rates in standard PGGs are typically associated with higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that implicated punishment and within-group enforcement can substantially increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when monitoring probability and punishment fines are sufficiently high. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the close relationship between cooperation rate and efficiency in standard PGGs suggests that these mechanisms would also increase efficiency. The nonlinear effect of group size is important: intermediate group sizes may be less effective for promoting cooperation than either small or large groups. For prediction tasks, the presence of implicated punishment and within-group enforcement, as well as the values of monitoring probability, punishment fine, and group size, should be considered as key moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2017). Stochastic evolutionary voluntary public goods game with punishment in a Quasi-birth-and-death process. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment and non-participation strategies into a stochastic evolutionary public goods game changes the evolutionary dynamics and the likelihood of the system reaching cooperative, defecting, or non-participation equilibria. Higher punishment probability (alpha) and lower punishment cost (gamma) both increase the parameter regions where the system is likely to reach cooperative/punishing (C+P) equilibria. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it reports the probability of the system selecting each type of equilibrium as a function of the parameters. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under a wide range of conditions, but the effect is moderated by the cost and probability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the likelihood of cooperative/punishing equilibria increases with higher punishment probability and lower punishment cost, and that these effects are sensitive to the marginal per-capita return (r/N) and the fixed income for non-participants. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its findings must be mapped from the probability of cooperative equilibria to expected efficiency. The results suggest that, in parameter regions where the C+P equilibrium is likely, efficiency would be higher than in regions dominated by defection or non-participation. Thus, the paper supports the idea that punishment can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cong, R., Zhao, Q. C., Li, K., & Wang, L. (2017). Individual mobility promotes punishment in evolutionary public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the optimal mobility (migration tendency) of punishers depends on the cost of punishment. For high punishment costs, a medium tendency to migrate (rather than always punishing or always running) maximizes the prevalence of punishers. For low punishment costs, always punishing is optimal. The presence of cooperators (second-order free-riders) changes the dynamics: in harsh environments (low enhancement factor), punishers benefit from moderate migration, but in more favorable environments, cooperators can dominate, and the advantage of punishment diminishes. The model shows that mobility can help punishers survive, but only under certain cost and environmental conditions. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher prevalence of punishers and cooperators is interpreted as beneficial for cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for how punishment, its cost, and the mobility of punishers affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, especially when the cost is not too high and punishers have a moderate migration tendency. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when punishment is not too costly and when the game environment allows for spatial structure and mobility. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Perc, M., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Szolnoki, A., Floría, L. M., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Evolutionary dynamics of group interactions on structured populations: a review. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The review summarizes that structured populations (lattices, networks, bipartite graphs) and coevolutionary rules can strongly affect the evolution of cooperation in public goods games. Punishment and reward mechanisms, as well as strategic complexity (e.g., conditional cooperation, pool/peer punishment, rewarding), can promote cooperation and solve second-order free-rider problems in structured settings. The review highlights that the effectiveness and stability of punishment and reward depend on the interplay between game parameters (e.g., group size, benefit functions, network structure) and evolutionary dynamics. However, the main outcomes discussed are cooperation rates and the survival/extinction of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides high-level theoretical and simulation-based context for how punishment and other mechanisms affect cooperation in public goods games on structured populations. It is useful for understanding which game design dimensions (e.g., group size, network structure, strategic complexity) are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report control vs. punishment-enabled efficiency rates. For the downstream prediction task, this review can inform which dimensions are likely to be important moderators, but cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Human cooperation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and experimental literature on the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in humans, with a focus on public goods games and related social dilemmas. It finds that: (1) Repetition and targeted reciprocity (including punishment and reward) can promote cooperation in public goods games; (2) Both punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but reward is generally more efficient (i.e., produces higher group payoffs) than punishment, which can destroy value and provoke retaliation or antisocial punishment; (3) The effectiveness of punishment and reward depends on their implementation and the broader social context; (4) Cooperation in one-shot anonymous games is explained as an overgeneralization of strategies learned in repeated or reputation-based settings. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and qualitative evidence that adding punishment to public goods games can increase cooperation, but may not always increase efficiency or group payoff, especially if punishment is costly, provokes retaliation, or is used antisocially. Reward mechanisms are generally more efficient than punishment. The review does not provide quantitative data or direct predictions about efficiency changes, so it should be used for qualitative guidance: expect that punishment can promote cooperation, but its effect on efficiency is context-dependent and may be negative if punishment is misapplied. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the design of punishment (cost, effectiveness, possibility of antisocial use) and the presence of reward options are important moderators.

- source: Baumard, N., André, J. B., & Sperber, D. (2013). A mutualistic approach to morality: The evolution of fairness by partner choice. *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation and punishment behaviors in economic games, including public goods games, are guided by evolved fairness norms shaped by partner choice. It reviews evidence that people punish both low and high contributors in PGGs, often to restore fairness rather than to maximize group efficiency. The mutualistic approach predicts that individuals seek proportionality between contributions and rewards, and that punishment is used to rebalance unfair distributions. The paper notes that in real-world small-scale societies, exclusion and partner switching are more common than costly punishment. It also highlights that punishment in PGGs is sometimes antisocial (targeting high contributors) and is strongly shaped by local fairness norms. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and interpretive context for understanding why and how punishment may affect cooperation and fairness in PGG-like environments, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as fair and proportional, and that exclusion or partner choice may be more effective than costly punishment in sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., fairness norms, entitlement, partner choice), but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Vincent, A. (2017). Punishment and status in collective action: How status hierarchies foster optimal punishment use. *Sociology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that status hierarchies can endogenously coordinate punishment in public goods games, leading to more optimal (i.e., less costly and more effective) use of punishment. High status actors are more likely to be expected to punish when punishment is seen as group-oriented, and their punishment is more likely to promote subsequent cooperation and reduce the need for further punishment. The author suggests that these dynamics can help groups realize public goods more efficiently, but this is a theoretical claim rather than an empirically demonstrated effect. The paper also discusses why typical experimental designs may fail to observe these effects, due to their suppression of status emergence and communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical arguments and literature synthesis suggesting that the presence of status hierarchies and the assignment of punishment responsibility to high status actors could improve the efficiency of punishment in public goods games by reducing unnecessary or counterproductive punishment and fostering cooperation. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that game designs allowing status differentiation, communication, or leadership roles may see more efficient punishment use, but this is not quantified. The paper is less useful for direct prediction of efficiency from game design dimensions in the absence of empirical data.

- source: Feinberg, M., Willer, R., & Schultz, M. (2014). Gossip and Ostracism Promote Cooperation in Groups. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with random rematching, the ability to gossip about others' contributions and to ostracize (exclude) group members based on this information leads to a substantial increase in cooperation and, after several rounds, to the highest group and individual earnings. Gossip alone also increases cooperation and earnings compared to a basic game with no reputation or exclusion, but the effect is less pronounced and decays over time. Ostracism is used primarily against low contributors, and those who are ostracized tend to increase their contributions upon returning. The efficiency gains from gossip and ostracism are not immediate, as exclusion temporarily reduces group size and the public good multiplier, but by the final rounds, these groups outperform the others in earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a punishment-like mechanism (ostracism) in addition to reputation/gossip in a public goods game increases efficiency (group and individual earnings) compared to both a basic game and a gossip-only game. The effect is robust despite a temporary efficiency loss due to exclusion. The design dimensions—such as group size (4), number of rounds (6 per game), no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR of 0.5 (or 0.375 when group size drops to 3), and anonymous exclusion—are all specified, making this study highly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment (ostracism) in similar lab PGGs.
