The results suggest that, even when punishment is implemented as exclusion rather than direct costly punishment, and even when exclusion temporarily reduces group size and multiplier, the long-run effect on efficiency is positive if reputation information is available.

- source: dos Santos, M. (2015). The evolution of anti-social rewarding and its countermeasures in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with pool reward funds, allowing defectors to form their own reward funds (anti-social rewarding) causes a breakdown of cooperation and group efficiency, even when mechanisms to exclude non-rewarders (second-order sanctions) are present. Theoretical analysis shows that anti-social rewarding leads to stable equilibria with low or no cooperation, unless additional countermeasures are implemented. These include making pro-social reward funds more productive than anti-social ones, or making reward funds contingent on the actual production of the public good. Only under these conditions, and with sufficiently strong exclusion of non-rewarders, can high cooperation and efficiency be restored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of anti-social rewarding mechanisms in public goods games with institutional rewards can dramatically reduce the efficiency gains from positive incentives, even when second-order punishment or exclusion is available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling anti-social rewarding (or failing to prevent it) will likely result in much lower efficiency in the punishment/reward-enabled condition compared to control, unless the game design includes mechanisms that make pro-social rewards more effective or contingent on public good production. The findings are directly relevant for settings with institutional rewards, but less so for standard punishment-only designs. The results highlight the importance of considering the possibility of anti-social incentives and the structure of reward and punishment technologies when predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, K., Cong, R., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2015). Social exclusion in finite populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social exclusion mechanisms (peer and pool) can allow excluders to dominate over defectors in finite-population public goods games, depending on exclusion cost, group size, and exclusion effectiveness. Peer exclusion is more effective at low costs, while pool exclusion is more robust at high costs and larger group sizes. Both mechanisms can sustain cooperation and prevent the dominance of defectors, which would otherwise reduce group payoff. The analysis is based on evolutionary game theory, using payoff functions and fixation probabilities, rather than direct simulation of group efficiency. The results suggest that enabling exclusion mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to no-punishment baselines, especially under the right parameter conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support that enabling exclusion-based punishment in public goods games can increase the prevalence of cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, especially when exclusion is effective and/or costs are not too high. The results are parameterized by group size, exclusion cost, and exclusion probability, and show that pool exclusion is more robust in large groups or at high costs. While the paper does not report efficiency as a direct outcome, the dominance of excluders and cooperators over defectors implies higher group payoff. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when exclusion is effective, costs are moderate, and group size is not too large for peer exclusion (but pool exclusion can handle larger groups). However, the lack of direct efficiency reporting and the theoretical nature of the results mean that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Yokoyama, A. (2014). The Effect of Ostracism and Optional Participation on the Evolution of Cooperation in the Voluntary Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in voluntary public goods games, allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) candidates with low reputation (defectors) leads to the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation rates. Optional participation (where individuals can choose whether to join a group based on group reputation) does not sustain cooperation. The strictest exclusion rule (requiring unanimous acceptance for membership) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The model is evolutionary and simulation-based, and outcomes are reported as the proportion of cooperators, not as group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that costless ostracism (exclusion of defectors) is a highly effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, more so than optional participation alone. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the implication is that group efficiency would be higher when ostracism is enabled, since the share of cooperators is much higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costless and takes the form of exclusion, rather than costly point-based punishment. The results are less directly applicable to predicting the quantitative effect of standard costly punishment on efficiency, but they support the qualitative prediction that effective exclusion or ostracism can greatly increase group efficiency if measured.

- source: Gangadharan, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Normative conflict and the limits of self-governance in heterogeneous populations. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Allowing communication and rewards in a public goods game leads to near-maximal efficiency in homogeneous groups, but only partial efficiency in heterogeneous groups where returns from the public good differ. In homogeneous groups, communication increases group earnings to 94% of the maximum, while in heterogeneous groups, the increase is only 80% of the maximum. The main reason is that most heterogeneous groups prioritize equality or compromise between equality and efficiency, rather than maximizing group earnings. Rewards are not systematically used to offset inequality. The loss in efficiency in heterogeneous groups is due both to lower contributions and to under-use of rewards. The study demonstrates that normative conflict can limit the effectiveness of self-governance mechanisms in promoting efficiency in heterogeneous populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling communication and rewards in public goods games depend critically on group homogeneity. In homogeneous groups, such mechanisms can achieve near-maximal efficiency. In heterogeneous groups, even with communication and rewards, efficiency gains are much smaller due to normative conflict between equality and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity in returns (mpcr differences) is a strong moderator that limits the efficiency benefit of enabling communication and rewards. The absence of punishment means the results are most directly applicable to reward-based or communication-based interventions, but the findings on normative conflict are likely relevant for predicting the limits of punishment as well, especially in heterogeneous settings.

- source: Ozono, H., Kamijo, Y., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Institutionalize Reciprocity to Overcome the Public Goods Provision Problem. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated four-person public goods game, embedding a reciprocal strategy as an institution (institutionalized reciprocity, IR) leads to significantly higher group efficiency (total profit) than a standard control game. The effect is strongest when the institution enforces a strict upper bound on contributions (S-IR), which enables reciprocators to coordinate against free riders and prevents excessive cooperation. In S-IR, group profits are significantly higher than in both the control and a less restrictive IR variant (NL-IR). The IR mechanism operates as a collective punishment system: if any player fails to meet the target, the group's future benefit is reduced for all. The study does not implement standard peer punishment, but the IR mechanism provides a collective sanction that is effective at increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutionalized, rule-based collective punishment (via reduction of future group benefit after a deviation) can substantially increase efficiency in repeated public goods games, especially when the institution strictly limits contributions to a target. While the study does not test standard peer punishment, it shows that collective, institutionally enforced sanctions can outperform standard repeated games without punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that introducing a credible, enforced institutional sanction (even if not peer punishment) can raise efficiency well above control, particularly when the rules are clear and binding. The results are most directly applicable to games with four players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions (in increments), and full feedback on others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2015). Collective action problem in heterogeneous groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that group effort and efficiency in public goods games and group contests depend strongly on group size, within-group heterogeneity, and the nonlinearity of cost and benefit functions. In linear models, group effort typically decreases with group size and increases with heterogeneity, while in nonlinear models, these patterns can reverse. The largest contributors are those with the highest stake or lowest cost, but under some conditions, these individuals may end up with lower net payoffs than free-riders. The review also summarizes experimental findings, noting that punishment can increase contributions but may not always increase total earnings or efficiency, especially in heterogeneous groups. Theoretical models predict that group selection, between-group competition, and synergicity can all increase group effort and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and synthetic evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group heterogeneity, cost/benefit structure, and group selection affect efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency, as punishment is only discussed in the context of prior literature and experiments. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is mainly useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the moderating role of group structure, but not for estimating the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2015). Antisocial pool rewarding does not deter public cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the introduction of both antisocial and prosocial pool rewarding does not deter the evolution of cooperation or reduce group efficiency. Instead, prosocial rewarding continues to promote cooperation, and the aggregation required for rewards to be effective leads to higher group payoff and efficiency. The critical threshold for cooperation to dominate is lowered as the effectiveness of rewarding increases, even when defectors can also reward each other. In well-mixed populations, antisocial rewarding can prevent cooperation unless cooperators are initially predominant, but in structured populations, local aggregation allows cooperation to thrive. The results are robust to increased randomness in the interaction network.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public goods games with pool rewarding, the presence of antisocial rewarding does not reduce the efficiency gains from prosocial rewarding in structured populations. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling reward mechanisms (even if both cooperators and defectors can use them) will generally increase efficiency compared to control, provided the population structure allows for aggregation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings with spatial or network structure, group size of 5, all-or-nothing contributions, and no punishment. The results are less applicable to well-mixed populations or to games with punishment rather than reward.

- source: Stewart, A. J., Parsons, T. L., & Plotkin, J. B. (2016). Evolutionary consequences of behavioral diversity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in iterated public goods games, increasing the number of available contribution levels (behavioral diversity) can either increase or decrease group efficiency, depending on the ratio of public benefit to individual cost (mpcr). With low mpcr, more choices allow for more robust cooperative strategies and higher mean payoffs. With high mpcr, the abundance of suboptimal robust strategies causes populations to get stuck at lower efficiency. The ability to punish (by reducing one's own contribution in response to defection) is necessary for two-choice strategies to be robust, but explicit costly punishment is not modeled. The paper provides analytic conditions for when simple strategies can resist invasion by more complex ones, and uses evolutionary simulations to show the impact of choice set size on mean payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the number of contribution options (all_or_nothing, player_count, mpcr, etc.), but it does not model explicit costly punishment as a separate mechanism. Instead, 'punishment' is operationalized as the ability to reduce one's own contribution in response to others' defection. The findings suggest that increasing the number of contribution options (behavioral diversity) can either help or hurt efficiency, depending on mpcr. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that more behavioral options do not always increase efficiency, and that the effect depends on the underlying game parameters. However, because explicit costly punishment is not present, the results are only adjacent to the core prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhang, C. Y., & Cao, M. (2015). How insurance affects altruistic provision in threshold public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing insurance for contributors in threshold public goods games increases the provision of the public good and can improve the efficiency (group payoff) of the system. The effect is strongest when the insurance compensation is high and the required threshold for provision is high. The model shows that insurance can shift the evolutionary dynamics to favor contributors over defectors, depending on parameter values. The allocation rule for surplus contributions (parameter omega) also moderates the effect. The paper does not analyze punishment or sanctions, but provides a theoretical basis for how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can promote cooperation and efficiency in threshold public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that risk-mitigation mechanisms (insurance) for contributors can increase efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the risk of loss is high and insurance compensation is generous. While it does not study punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in games where contributors face risk and can be protected from loss. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, adding insurance or similar mechanisms can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The model is most applicable to threshold public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, no communication, and no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of the threshold, insurance compensation, and allocation rules for surplus contributions.

- source: Moreira, J. A., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2013). Evolution of collective action in adaptive social structures. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can adapt their social ties—specifically, break links with defectors more quickly than with cooperators—cooperation can be sustained at higher levels than in well-mixed or static networks. The evolutionary dynamics shift from defection dominance to a scenario with stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, depending on the group size, enhancement factor, and network adaptation rates. Stochastic effects (e.g., mutation, exploration) further help populations escape defection traps and maintain cooperation. However, the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how endogenous partner selection and adaptive network structure can promote cooperation in PGGs, which may indirectly inform predictions about efficiency in environments where such mechanisms are present. However, it does not address punishment or sanctions, nor does it provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that adaptive partner selection can increase cooperation rates, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other interventions.

- source: Gneezy, U., Leibbrandt, A., & List, J. A. (2016). ODE TO THE SEA: WORKPLACE ORGANIZATIONS AND NORMS OF COOPERATION. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Fishermen in group-based (sea) societies exhibit higher trust, trustworthiness, pro-sociality, and cooperation in economic games—including the public goods game—than those in individual-based (lake) societies. These differences are robust to controls for selection and other confounds. The study provides evidence that workplace organization (group vs. individual work) is a key driver of cooperative norms. However, the public goods game does not include punishment or reward options, and the main outcomes are behavioral (contributions, trust, etc.), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the effect of group-based workplace organization on cooperation and contribution behavior in public goods games, but it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It does not manipulate or include punishment, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in public goods games. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR) are reported and can be extracted, but the absence of punishment means this paper is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency with punishment enabled.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Hendriks, A., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2016). Think global, act local: Preserving the global commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when participants can observe their neighbors' contributions to the public good and can condition their pairwise cooperation (in a PD) on this information, contributions to the public good are sustained at high levels, even in very large groups (up to 1,000 participants). In the control condition, where such information is not available, contributions decline over time. The mechanism is not direct punishment but rather the withholding of cooperation in a separate pairwise game, which acts as a social sanction. The effect is robust to group size and does not require participants to know the overall level of public good provision. The main outcome is sustained contribution, not measured efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for targeted social sanctions (withholding cooperation in a pairwise PD) based on observed public good contributions can sustain high contribution rates in large groups. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to punishment (withholding cooperation as a sanction) but does not involve costly punishment or explicit payoff reduction. The findings suggest that enabling targeted, observable social sanctions—even if not costly—can have a strong positive effect on contribution rates, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar designs, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Zhang, H. R., & Perc, M. (2016). Evolution of conditional cooperation under multilevel selection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with continuous contribution and multilevel selection, the presence of conditional strategies (modeled as piecewise linear response functions) enables the emergence and stability of high cooperation levels. The dominant strategy is robust to initial conditions and parameter changes. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on average contribution and strategy prevalence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional cooperation strategies can sustain high contribution levels in public goods games under multilevel selection, even without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related dimensions. Its findings are most relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of explicit sanctioning mechanisms, and for interpreting the role of conditional strategies as an alternative to punishment. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2017). Alliance formation with exclusion in the spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of an excluder strategy (which monitors and excludes defectors at a cost) can lead to the elimination of defectors when excluders form alliances with other cooperative strategies (cooperators or punishers). This effect is robust even when the cost of exclusion is extremely high, provided the population structure allows for the formation of sufficiently large clusters of excluders and cooperators/punishers. The main outcomes are the extinction probabilities of defectors and the conditions for the survival of excluders, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusion-based sanctions (a form of punishment) can be highly effective in structured populations, especially when combined with other cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain spatial and strategic conditions, sanctioning mechanisms can eliminate defection, which would likely increase efficiency, but this is not quantified. The findings are most relevant for understanding the qualitative impact of exclusion and alliances in spatially structured games, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Zhou, T. W., Ding, S., Fan, W. J., & Wang, H. (2016). An improved public goods game model with reputation effect on the spatial lattices. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that embedding a reputation effect into the utility function of agents in a spatial PGG model leads to a substantial increase in the stationary fraction of cooperators, especially as the reputation factor increases. The critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge is reduced, and the region of parameter space supporting full cooperation is expanded. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are based on Monte Carlo simulations and are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. For the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency effects of punishment—this paper is only weakly informative. It suggests that non-monetary social mechanisms (like reputation) can increase cooperation, but does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects efficiency, nor does it report group payoff or welfare. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the reputation mechanism, but not for punishment or efficiency.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2017). Publishing the donation list incompletely promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list incompletely (only for those who contribute above a threshold) leads to higher total contributions and more equal contributions among players in a public goods game on a network. There is an optimal threshold for publication that maximizes these effects. The per capita number of neighbors and the amount of historical data players consider also moderate the effect, but the main result is robust: partial transparency outperforms full transparency or secrecy. The model is agent-based and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on social reputation and face loss as motivators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that social information mechanisms (such as partial publication of contribution lists) can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not study monetary punishment or its effect on efficiency. The findings are relevant for predicting the effect of information disclosure or reputation-based interventions, not for predicting the effect of adding costly punishment. The sum of contributions is a proxy for group welfare, but efficiency (as defined by payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported. Use this paper as informative for the effect of social/reputational incentives, not for monetary punishment treatments.

- source: Yang, Z. H., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2013). Effects of adaptive dynamical linking in networked games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that adaptive dynamical linking—where agents lengthen or shorten social ties based on whether their partners cooperate or defect—can dramatically increase the prevalence of cooperation in both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods game. The effect is stronger when agents adjust their links more rapidly (higher adjustment rate, Δ), and under weak selection. The mechanism does not involve any explicit punishment, reward, or reputation system. The outcome measured is the frequency of full cooperation (all-cooperator absorbing state) in repeated simulations. There is no direct measurement or discussion of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as a demonstration that network adaptation (partner switching) can promote cooperation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (partner choice) that can promote cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Aimone, J. A., Iannaccone, L. R., Makowsky, M. D., & Rubin, J. (2013). Endogenous Group Formation via Unproductive Costs. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing subjects to self-select into groups with lower private returns (sacrifice) leads to higher contributions and, crucially, higher group and individual payoffs in high-sacrifice groups compared to no-sacrifice groups. The mechanism works by screening out free-riders and encouraging conditional cooperators to join and contribute more. Infiltration by free-riders is limited, so high-sacrifice groups do not suffer reduced payoffs. When group assignment is random (control), the effect is much weaker. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but provides strong evidence that endogenous sorting via unproductive costs can increase efficiency in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games with endogenous group formation and self-imposed unproductive costs, but it does not inform about punishment or sanction mechanisms. If a game design allows for endogenous sorting into groups with different private returns (sacrifice), expect higher efficiency in high-sacrifice groups due to screening and substitution effects. However, the findings do not transfer to settings where punishment is the treatment of interest, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. The control (random assignment) condition shows that endogenous sorting is key to the efficiency gains.

- source: Bouma, J., & Ansink, E. (2013). The role of legitimacy perceptions in self-restricted resource use: A framed field experiment. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that label framing (contextualizing the game as protected area management) does not significantly reduce extractions (increase efficiency) on average. However, for participants who trust enforcement officers or feel their community can influence park management, framing leads to lower extractions (higher efficiency). The effect is not significant for the sample as a whole, but is significant for these subgroups. The explanatory power of legitimacy indicators is low, and the sample size is small. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms are present. However, it does show that contextual framing and legitimacy perceptions can moderate extraction (and thus efficiency) in PGG-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that institutional context and perceived legitimacy may interact with other design features to affect efficiency, but it does not inform the effect of adding punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in field or framed settings.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2016). The public goods game with a new form of shared reward. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a shared reward, proportional to the number of cooperators, can enable cooperation to invade and become stable in a population of defectors if the reward parameter exceeds a critical threshold. This threshold depends negatively on the population size and the public goods multiplier, and positively on the group size. Larger populations and higher public goods multipliers make it easier for cooperation to be favored, while larger group sizes make it harder. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, and thus does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how reward mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoff. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but may be useful for understanding the comparative role of reward-based interventions.

- source: Henrich, J., Chudek, M., & Boyd, R. (2015). The Big Man Mechanism: how prestige fosters cooperation and creates prosocial leaders. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that prestige-based leadership, via cultural transmission, can generate and sustain cooperation in public goods game environments, leading to higher group efficiency and payoffs. The effect depends on the probability that followers copy the leader (prestige effect, p), the stickiness of acquired behaviors (s), and group size (n). Cooperation is more likely to evolve and persist in small groups, with high prestige effects and high stickiness. The model predicts that prestigious leaders will be more prosocial in small groups, but this effect diminishes in large groups. The paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in its models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games without punishment, the presence of prestige-biased cultural transmission and prosocial leaders can increase group efficiency, especially in small groups and when followers are likely to copy and retain the leader's cooperative behavior. However, because the models do not include punishment or sanctions, the findings do not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in games with prestige-based leadership and social learning, not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment. If a game design includes prestige or leading-by-example mechanisms, this paper suggests that efficiency may already be elevated, and the incremental effect of punishment may be smaller or interact with these mechanisms.

- source: van Soest, D., Stoop, J., & Vyrastekova, J. (2016). Toward a delineation of the circumstances in which cooperation can be sustained in environmental and resource problems. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game (VCM), average net group contributions are positive and significant, leading to substantial group payoffs and efficiency. In contrast, when the action space is extended to allow negative contributions (claims/taking from the public fund), average net group contributions are not significantly different from zero, and group payoffs are much lower. This holds even for subjects who initially display prosocial behavior. The ability to undo others' contributions destroys cooperation and efficiency, and the effect is not explained by framing or subject pool differences. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the action space—specifically, whether players can undo others' contributions—has a dramatic effect on efficiency in public goods games. In standard VCMs (with only non-negative contributions), efficiency is much higher than in Claim Games (where negative contributions are allowed). However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, establish a strong baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is high in standard VCMs and very low in Claim Games. For prediction tasks, this paper helps set expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency under different action space designs, but does not inform the effect of punishment.

- source: Meloni, S., Xia, C. Y., & Moreno, Y. (2017). Heterogeneous resource allocation can change social hierarchy in public goods games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when players in a networked PGG can allocate their contributions unevenly—favoring games where they previously earned higher payoffs—this leads to increased cooperation, a more heterogeneous distribution of investments, and a shift in the structure of wealth production. Specifically, small clusters of cooperators become highly productive, and the overall wealth distribution approaches the Pareto law (80% of wealth produced by 20% of nodes) for sufficiently strong payoff-driven allocation. The study also shows that the classical PGG (equal allocation) does not reproduce these features. The model is robust to different evolutionary update rules and network structures. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in PGGs or their impact on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in resource allocation affects efficiency and wealth distribution in PGGs, but it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task—estimating the efficiency impact of punishment given game design dimensions—this paper is not directly informative, as punishment is not present in the model.

- source: Fosgaard, T. R., & Piovesan, M. (2015). Nudge for (the Public) Good: How Defaults Can Affect Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that while most subjects do not simply accept the default contribution values, the presence of a default (either free rider or perfect conditional cooperator) significantly influences their stated cooperation strategies and actual contributions in a subsequent repeated public goods game. Specifically, exposure to a cooperative default increases later contributions, while a free rider default decreases them. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. It does, however, show that non-binding defaults can shift contribution behavior, which may indirectly inform models of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Cone, J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Time Pressure Increases Cooperation in Competitively Framed Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time pressure increases cooperation (measured as contribution to the public good) in a competitively framed public goods game, but not in the standard cooperative frame. The effect is robust across two datasets (the authors' and a prior study). The results support the Social Heuristics Hypothesis, suggesting that intuitive responses favor cooperation, especially in novel or less familiar contexts. However, the study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation (such as time pressure and framing), but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified, but the absence of punishment and lack of payoff-based outcomes mean this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Vermeer, A. B. L., Heerema, R. L., & Sanfey, A. G. (2016). Third-Party Cooperation: How Reducing Material Involvement Enhances Contributions to the Public Good. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that reducing personal material involvement in a public goods game increases cooperation rates: participants contribute more when making decisions for a third party than for themselves. This effect is further enhanced when the group consists of human players rather than computer players, suggesting that social motives drive third-party cooperation. The main outcome is the proportion of tokens contributed, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how personal involvement and social context affect cooperation rates, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. The design and results may be contextually informative for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Bigoni, M., Bortolotti, S., Casari, M., Gambetta, D., & Pancotto, F. (2016). Amoral Familism, Social Capital, or Trust? The Behavioural Foundations of the Italian North-South Divide. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds a persistent and significant North-South divide in cooperation in both the public good game and the trust game, with higher cooperation in the North. This gap is not explained by differences in risk aversion, concerns for equality or efficiency, or standard proxies for social capital. The experimental design uses a standard PGG (N=4, 8 rounds, MPCR=0.5, no punishment or reward, no communication, continuous contribution choices from a discrete set, full feedback on others' contributions and earnings). The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, trust frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that regional differences in social norms and expectations, rather than incentives or individual preferences, underlie the observed cooperation gap.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on baseline cooperation rates in a standard PGG with no punishment or reward, using a representative sample and a well-described design. It is directly informative for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar PGGs, especially in the Italian context. However, it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatment, so it provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency change when punishment is enabled, except as a control benchmark. The detailed reporting of game parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, feedback, no chat, etc.) makes it useful for mapping design dimensions, but not for the core prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2017). Social learning and the demise of costly cooperation in humans. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that providing social information about other groups' behaviors or payoffs does not increase cooperation in repeated public goods games; in most cases, it leads to a significant decline in cooperation. The decline is strongest when individuals can observe the behavior and payoffs of the most successful individuals, supporting the idea of payoff-biased or prestige-biased social learning. There is no evidence for conformity bias (copying common behaviors) or group-prestige bias (copying successful groups). The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning and information about others' behavior/payoff affect cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as not providing evidence about punishment effects, but may be informative about baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment.

- source: Lamba, S. (2014). Social learning in cooperative dilemmas. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals do not show a clear tendency to conform to the modal contribution or to copy the highest earner's contribution in public goods games. There is significant individual and village-level variation in social learning strategies, and these are influenced by individual and contextual factors. Overall, contributions decreased in the second round, and there is no evidence that social learning increases cooperation. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present in the experimental design. The findings are relevant for understanding baseline behavior in the absence of punishment, especially regarding the role of social learning, but do not inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help characterize control conditions (no punishment, no reward, no communication), but does not provide treatment effect estimates or moderators for punishment.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2016). Conditional cooperation and confusion in public-goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public-goods game (4 players, 1.6 multiplier, no punishment or reward), most participants do not understand that the income-maximizing strategy is to contribute nothing, regardless of others' actions. Many participants classified as 'conditional cooperators' are actually confused, not prosocial. The same behavioral patterns are observed when playing with computers (where cooperation cannot benefit others) as with humans. There is no evidence that a subpopulation of 'comprehenders' (those who understand the game) are more cooperative with humans than with computers. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatment. However, it is highly informative about baseline (control) PGGs without punishment: it shows that observed cooperation in such games may be largely due to misunderstanding, not prosocial preferences. For prediction tasks, this suggests that control group efficiency in standard PGGs may overestimate the true baseline level of prosociality, especially if participants misunderstand the payoff structure. The paper does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency, nor does it provide evidence on moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & West, S. A. (2013). Prosocial preferences do not explain human cooperation in public-goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that (1) cooperation levels in public-goods games are not explained by prosocial preferences, as participants contribute similarly even when unaware their actions affect others; (2) providing enhanced information about others' earnings reduces cooperation; (3) even when cooperation is profitable, participants do not reach full cooperation. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. However, it does provide high-quality evidence on how information and framing affect cooperation rates in standard and variant PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does reinforce the importance of information structure and participant understanding as moderators of cooperation, which may interact with punishment in other studies.

- source: Horita, Y., Takezawa, M., Inukai, K., Kita, T., & Masuda, N. (2017). Reinforcement learning accounts for moody conditional cooperation behavior: experimental results. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that moody conditional cooperation (MCC) patterns are present in both repeated multiplayer PDG and PGG, though the specific form differs from previous literature. In the PGG, conditional cooperation is observed in both fixed and mixed group treatments. Reinforcement learning models (Bush-Mosteller and Roth-Erev) fit the observed behavioral data as well as or better than CC and MCC models, especially when direct reciprocity is absent. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics and the explanatory power of behavioral models in repeated PGGs with and without stable group composition. For the prediction task, this paper may inform expectations about baseline (control) cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment efficacy.

- source: Gong, X., & Sanfey, A. G. (2017). Social rank and social cooperation: Impact of social comparison processes on cooperative decision-making. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social comparison, operationalized as being assigned a high or low rank in a prior task, significantly affects subsequent contribution decisions in a standard public goods game. High rank leads to increased contributions, low rank to decreased contributions, compared to a baseline with no ranking. These effects are robust to whether the PGG group is the same as the ranking group, and are more pronounced in individuals with high social comparison orientation. The experiment does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are limited to how social comparison affects contribution behavior. While the game design (standard PGG) is relevant, the absence of punishment means it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior or the impact of social comparison, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Lotz, S. (2015). Spontaneous Giving under Structural Inequality: Intuition Promotes Cooperation in Asymmetric Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that priming participants to use intuition (vs. reflection) increases their contributions to the public good in a one-shot, four-player PGG with asymmetric MPCRs (structural inequality). The effect of intuition is robust to controls for age, gender, comprehension, and paragraph length. There is no significant main effect of MPCR (high vs. low) on cooperation, nor an interaction between intuition and MPCR. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant as a baseline for understanding cooperation rates in asymmetric, one-shot PGGs without punishment or reward. The findings may inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar asymmetric games, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on contribution rates (not payoffs) limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., Nax, H. H., & West, S. A. (2015). Payoff-based learning explains the decline in cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in repeated public goods games, participants' contribution behavior is best explained by payoff-based learning: individuals adjust their contributions in response to changes in their own payoffs, rather than out of concern for others' payoffs or by matching others' contributions. Pro-social learning is not supported and, if anything, participants appear to weight others' payoffs negatively. Conditional cooperation is only weakly supported and disappears when the consequences of cooperation are made clearer. The decline in cooperation over time is thus attributed to individuals learning to maximize their own income, not to social preferences or conditional cooperation. The paper does not study punishment or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. It is useful for understanding baseline dynamics of contribution decline in repeated public goods games without punishment, showing that such decline is best explained by payoff-based learning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper can inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency trajectory, but provides no evidence on how punishment would alter efficiency. It does, however, reinforce that in the absence of punishment, efficiency is likely to decline over time due to learning, not social preferences.

- source: Cárdenas, J. C., Janssen, M. A., Ale, M., Bastakoti, R., Bernal, A., Chalermphol, J., Gong, Y. Z., Shin, H., Shivakoti, G., Wang, Y. B., & Anderies, J. M. (2017). Fragility of the provision of local public goods to private and collective risks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the returns from the public good are risky (collective risk), participants invest less in the public good compared to when only private returns are risky or when there is no risk. Communities more integrated with the market (higher share of crops sold externally, more members with outside income) show lower cooperation, especially under collective risk. Risk aversion is associated with higher cooperation in the baseline, but not under risk treatments. The main outcome is the rate of investment in the public good; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. It does provide evidence on how risk and market integration affect cooperation rates in public good games, but not on efficiency or payoff outcomes, and not on the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly relevant.

- source: Rand, D. G., Kraft-Todd, G., & Gruber, J. (2015). The Collective Benefits of Feeling Good and Letting Go: Positive Emotion and (dis)Inhibition Interact to Predict Cooperative Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two large-sample studies using one-shot PGGs (and some continuous PDs), the presence of positive emotion in participants' narratives predicts higher contributions, while the presence of inhibition predicts lower contributions. There is a significant interaction: the positive effect of emotion is strongest when inhibition is absent. These effects are robust to controls for negative emotion and demographic variables. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze any game design dimensions related to punishment, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Its findings on psychological predictors of cooperation may be of indirect interest for understanding baseline cooperation rates in one-shot games, but are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Huang, K. K., Wang, T., Cheng, Y., & Zheng, X. P. (2015). Effect of Heterogeneous Investments on the Evolution of Cooperation in Spatial Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in cooperative investments (modeled as a uniform distribution around a mean) increases the steady-state fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. This effect is stronger with greater variance in investment. When both strategy and investment coevolve, the promotion of cooperation is weaker because investment heterogeneity is reduced. The main dependent variable is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that heterogeneity in investment can promote cooperation (as measured by the fraction of cooperators), but do not address payoff-based outcomes or the effect of punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Stivala, A., Kashima, Y., & Kirley, M. (2016). Culture and cooperation in a spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that cooperation (measured as the fraction of conditional cooperators) only survives in multicultural states with high cultural diversity and very low or zero noise. In monocultural states or with any significant noise, cooperation dies out. The number of participants in public goods games is inversely related to cultural diversity. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it does provide contextual evidence that group structure, diversity, and noise can strongly affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding the role of cultural diversity and conditional cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Zhong, L. X., Xu, W. J., Shi, Y. D., & Qiu, T. (2013). Coupled dynamics of mobility and pattern formation in optional public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that both population density and agent mobility (free-floating probability) have non-monotonic effects on the frequency of cooperation in the OPGG. At low density and low mobility, cooperation is high; increasing either density or mobility generally reduces cooperation by making it easier for defectors to exploit cooperators. There is an optimal population density for cooperation, and the relationship between mobility and cooperation follows a power law. The results are explained in terms of agent collisions and cluster formation. However, the paper does not consider punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how population density and mobility affect cooperation rates in optional participation settings without sanctions. It should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. W., Jiang, L. L., Nie, S., & Wang, B. H. (2015). Uncovering cooperative behaviors with sparse historical behavior data in the spatial games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to study how conformity-driven behavior affects cooperation in public goods games with binary heterogeneous endowments (rich and poor). The main finding is a 'double-edged sword' effect: when the multiplication factor (synergy of cooperation) is high, conformity enhances cooperation by strengthening network reciprocity; when the multiplication factor is low, conformity inhibits cooperation, especially among the poor, by allowing defection to infiltrate cooperative clusters. These effects are robust to variations in conformity strength, the proportion of rich, and the extension to multiplayer PGGs. However, the study only reports on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on conformity and cooperation rates in heterogeneous endowment settings may be contextually informative for understanding behavioral dynamics in PGGs, but are not directly transferable to efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor to the effects of punishment. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Kim, J., Chae, H., Yook, S. H., & Kim, Y. (2015). Spatial evolutionary public goods game on complete graph and dense complex networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The SEPGG with voluntary participation exhibits distinct stationary states depending on the multiplication factor (r), group size (N), and network density (mean degree). On complete graphs (all-to-all), either defectors or loners dominate depending on r, with no stable cooperator-dominant state. On sparse networks, cooperation can dominate for sufficiently low mean degree. The paper provides scaling laws for the transitions between these states. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff; it focuses on the prevalence of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how group size and network structure affect cooperation and opting out in voluntary participation PGGs, but not for the effect of punishment. The results may be useful as a baseline for the behavior of voluntary participation PGGs without punishment, but cannot inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Yang, Y. Q., & Wang, T. (2015). Behavioral evolution in evacuation crowd based on heterogeneous rationality of small groups. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the degree of rationality (parameter alpha) in a subset of players leads to a higher fraction of cooperators in the population. When more players use rational allocation strategies, cooperation is promoted, and the phase transition points for cooperation occur at lower enhancement factors. The ratio of rational to conventional players also modulates cooperation: more rational players lead to more cooperation. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or direct efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in rationality and investment strategies can promote cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than payoffs limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Chen, M. H., Wang, L., Wang, J., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2015). Impact of individual response strategy on the spatial public goods game within mobile agents. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the order in which agents update their strategy and move (strategy update order) can significantly affect the evolution of cooperation and social instability in spatial PGGs with mobile agents. Different aspiration evaluation methods (how agents assess satisfaction based on their own and neighbors' payoffs) have only minor effects on cooperation and agglomeration, but can influence social instability. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how mobility and aspiration-based strategy updating affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. It may provide weak contextual information about the role of behavioral updating and mobility, but is not informative for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Barr, A., Packard, T., & Serra, D. (2014). Participatory accountability and collective action: Experimental evidence from Albania. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is a robust positive correlation between cooperative behavior in a one-shot, binary public goods game and participation in both school-level and national accountability systems. At both the individual and district level, higher rates of cooperation in the game are associated with higher rates of participation in parent representative elections and higher voter turnout.
