The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the public goods game, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or discuss punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are limited to the relationship between cooperative tendencies and real-world participation in collective action. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game environments.

- source: Nishi, A., Christakis, N. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Cooperation, decision time, and culture: Online experiments with American and Indian participants. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that American participants are more likely to cooperate than Indian participants in a repeated public goods game, both in the first round and in subsequent rounds, regardless of the social environment. Decision time for cooperation versus defection varies by culture: Americans decide faster when cooperating (except in non-cooperative environments), while Indians decide faster when defecting. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or efficiency outcomes are present. It does, however, provide contextual evidence about baseline cooperation rates in repeated public goods games with different cultural groups, which may be useful for understanding baseline control conditions in similar designs. However, it cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Bravo, G., & Squazzoni, F. (2013). Exit, Punishment and Rewards in Commons Dilemmas: An Experimental Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a modified public goods game with six players per group and ten rounds per treatment, the introduction of punishment (a 5 MU fine for non-cooperation) significantly increased cooperation and efficiency compared to the control (no incentives). However, the highest efficiency (profit as a proportion of the theoretical optimum) was achieved when both voluntary participation (exit option) and positive incentives (5 MU reward for cooperation) were present. Punishment alone (No-Neg) led to 67% of the optimum, while reward plus exit (Ex-Pos) led to 69%. The control (No-Null) and punishment plus exit (Ex-Neg) both yielded 58%. Thus, while punishment increases efficiency over the control, it is less effective than the reward-plus-exit combination. The paper also finds that exit alone has little effect, and that the interaction between exit and incentives is important for maximizing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with six players, ten rounds, no communication, and fixed institutional punishment (5 MU fine per defection), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), but the efficiency gain is smaller than that achieved by combining rewards and voluntary participation. The efficiency effect of punishment is positive but not maximal. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, fixed MPCR, fixed punishment cost/tech, no reward in punishment-only treatments, no identification of punishers, and full information about group outcomes) are all specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under similar conditions.

- source: Dekel, S., Fischer, S., & Zultan, R. (2017). Potential Pareto Public Goods. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a linear public goods game with two majority players (who benefit from the public good) and one minority player (who is harmed by the public good), punishment institutions do not increase contributions or efficiency. Punishment is used ineffectively, with minority players sometimes punishing contributors, and majority players reluctant to punish free riders. As a result, efficiency is significantly lower in the punishment treatments compared to the baseline. Communication does not improve outcomes in the punishment condition. In contrast, when a reward institution is available and communication is allowed, majority players compensate the harmed minority, which increases majority contributions and efficiency. The presence of a harmed minority undermines the usual positive effects of punishment and communication on cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public goods games with a harmed minority, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and may even reduce it, relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of communication. The game design features—three players, 30 rounds, continuous contributions, known group composition, and a high punishment impact (3:1 ratio)—do not rescue the effectiveness of punishment in this context. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar settings (especially with harmed minorities), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency, even if control efficiency is moderate. The paper also shows that reward institutions, especially with communication, can increase efficiency by enabling compensation.

- source: van Miltenburg, N., Przepiorka, W., & Buskens, V. (2017). Consensual punishment does not promote cooperation in the six-person prisoner's dilemma game with noisy public monitoring. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three punishment institutions (individual decision rule, CDR2, CDR3) in a six-person prisoner's dilemma, with and without 20% noise in public monitoring of contributions. In the absence of noise, all punishment institutions increase cooperation and earnings relative to the baseline, with collective decision rules (especially CDR3) performing best. With noise, however, all punishment institutions result in lower earnings than the baseline (no-punishment) condition. The individual decision rule (IDR) produces slightly higher cooperation than collective rules under noise, but still does not improve earnings. The main reason is that noise leads to both mistaken punishment of cooperators and missed punishment of defectors, so resources are wasted and cooperation is not effectively enforced. Thus, punishment does not promote efficiency in noisy environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a six-person all-or-nothing prisoner's dilemma with 20% noise in public monitoring, enabling punishment (regardless of whether it is individual or requires consensus) reduces group efficiency compared to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust across different punishment decision rules. The findings suggest that, when noise is present, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than the control efficiency. The results are directly relevant for settings with similar group size, binary contribution, and noisy monitoring, and less so for linear PGGs or settings with perfect information.

- source: Kosfeld, M., & Rustagi, D. (2015). Leader Punishment and Cooperation in Groups: Experimental Field Evidence from Commons Management in Ethiopia. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This study uses a field experiment with real-world leaders of Ethiopian forest user groups, combining a third-party punishment public goods game (TPPG) with observational data on group performance in commons management. Leaders are classified by their punishment motives: non-punishers, equality-driven, equality-and-efficiency-driven, and antisocial. The main finding is that groups led by equality-and-efficiency-driven punishers achieve substantially higher efficiency (as measured by young trees per hectare) than those led by non-punishers, while groups led by antisocial punishers perform significantly worse. Equality-only punishers have no significant effect in the first assessment but a positive effect in the second. The effect sizes are large relative to the mean outcome. The results are robust to a wide range of controls and alternative specifications. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not uniform: it depends on the leader's punishment motive and implementation.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not universally positive or negative, but depends on the nature of punishment. When punishment is targeted at free riders (motivated by equality and/or efficiency), efficiency increases. When punishment is antisocial or indiscriminate, efficiency decreases. The experimental design (two-player linear PGG with third-party punishment, MPCR=0.75, punishment cost=1, punishment impact=3, no chat, continuous contributions) is close to standard PGGs, but the real-world outcome is a field measure of group welfare. The findings suggest that the presence of punishment can increase or decrease efficiency depending on the implementation and motives, and that heterogeneity in punishment type is a key moderator. For prediction, the effect of enabling punishment should be conditioned on whether punishment is likely to be prosocial (targeting defectors) or antisocial (targeting cooperators or indiscriminately).

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2013). Empowering neighbors versus imposing regulations: An experimental analysis of pollution reduction schemes. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a non-point source pollution problem with heterogeneous agents, four treatments were compared: baseline (no intervention), group tax only, punishment only, and group tax plus punishment. The group tax mechanism (formal regulation) led to large and statistically significant increases in efficiency (from ~55% in baseline to ~87% with tax, and ~90% with tax plus punishment). Peer punishment alone increased efficiency only slightly and not significantly. When combined, punishment reduced variance and helped keep pollution below the target more often, but the efficiency gain was offset by the deadweight loss from punishment costs. Punishment was used less frequently and less intensely than in standard public goods games, limiting its effectiveness. The results suggest that formal regulatory mechanisms are more effective than informal peer punishment in increasing efficiency, but peer punishment can play a complementary role.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with a negative externality (pollution), enabling peer punishment alone yields only modest and inconsistent efficiency gains, while a formal group penalty (tax) mechanism yields large and robust efficiency improvements. Adding punishment to the group tax further reduces damages and increases the likelihood of meeting the target, but does not significantly increase efficiency due to the costliness of punishment. For prediction, the presence of a formal group penalty is the key driver of efficiency, and the marginal effect of enabling peer punishment is small or negligible unless punishment is much more frequent or severe than observed here. The game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, rounds, information) are well-documented, making this study highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar settings.

- source: Ozono, H., Jin, N., Watabe, M., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Solving the second-order free rider problem in a public goods game: An experiment using a leader support system. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when leaders are given the ability to punish followers using resources provided by follower support (support-present condition), both the frequency and effectiveness of punishment increase compared to a no-support condition. This leads to a higher probability of groups achieving high cooperation (as measured by contribution rates) and higher average profits for both leaders and followers, especially under 'linkage' punishment leaders who punish both non-cooperators and non-supporters. The no-support condition, where leaders have externally provided punishment resources, results in weaker punishment and lower group cooperation. The findings suggest that the structure of punishment funding and the type of leader punishment strategy are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling leaders to punish using resources provided by group members (rather than external resources) increases the efficiency of punishment in public goods games, as measured by group and individual profits and the frequency of high-cooperation groups. The effect is especially pronounced when leaders punish both first- and second-order free riders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader support system, the type of punishment strategy, and the source of punishment capital are key moderators. The control (no-support) condition provides a baseline for efficiency, and the support-present condition demonstrates a substantial positive shift in efficiency when punishment is enabled and properly incentivized.

- source: Gächter, S., Mengel, F., Tsakas, E., & Vostroknutov, A. (2017). Growth and inequality in public good provision. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dynamic public good game with endogenous endowments, contributions and group wealth increase over time even without punishment. Introducing punishment does not increase group wealth or efficiency; in some cases, it reduces them, particularly in longer games. Punishment increases contributions but destroys resources, leading to lower or unchanged group wealth. Early inequality is strongly negatively correlated with later group wealth in the punishment condition, and anti-social punishment (shirkers punishing contributors) is more prevalent in unequal groups, further reducing efficiency. Removing inequality experimentally restores the positive effect of punishment on contributions and wealth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic public good games with endogenous endowments, enabling punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to control (no punishment). The negative effect is especially likely when early inequality is high, as anti-social punishment becomes more common and destructive. For prediction tasks, if the game design includes endogenous endowments and dynamic wealth accumulation, the presence of punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency, and may decrease it, especially as the number of rounds increases. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in standard (static, exogenous endowment) PGGs, punishment increases efficiency, but in dynamic, endogenous settings, it does not.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2013). If Cooperation Is Likely Punish Mildly: Insights from Economic Experiments Based on the Snowdrift Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This study uses repeated group snowdrift games (a close variant of public goods games) with and without punishment to test the effects of mild versus severe punishment on cooperation and average payoff. In groups of five, with 25 rounds per session, the authors manipulate the cost-to-benefit ratio (r = 0.2 or 0.8) and the punishment fine (p = 2.0 or 4.0, with constant cost to punisher). When cooperation is likely (r = 0.2), both mild and severe punishment increase cooperation, but severe punishment does not further increase cooperation and reduces average payoff compared to mild punishment. When cooperation is unlikely (r = 0.8), only severe punishment substantially increases cooperation and average payoff; mild punishment is less effective and can even reduce average payoff compared to no punishment. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: mild punishment is optimal when cooperation is already likely, while severe punishment is only justified in highly defection-prone environments.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the underlying difficulty of cooperation (cost-to-benefit ratio, analogous to MPCR in PGGs). If the control game (no punishment) already achieves high efficiency, adding severe punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency; mild punishment is sufficient. If the control game has low efficiency (cooperation is rare), only severe punishment can increase efficiency. The cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the baseline cooperation rate, are critical moderators. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in snowdrift- or PGG-like games with peer punishment.

- source: Wang, Y. F., Vasilakos, A. V., & Ma, J. H. (2015). VPEF: A Simple and Effective Incentive Mechanism in Community-Based Autonomous Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Network and Service Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: VPEF, an incentive mechanism based on voluntary participation, round-based entry fees, and voluntary costly punishment, leads to high efficiency in community-based collaborative networks. The system spends most of its time in the punisher state, where all participants contribute and punish defectors, minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss is analytically shown to be 4/(8+M), which becomes negligible for large communities. Simulations confirm the theoretical predictions. The mechanism is robust to changes in group size, entry fee (as long as it is not too high), and multiplexing/diversity factor. The model assumes strong selection (users imitate higher-payoff strategies) and does not require tracking long-term behavior or permanent exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling voluntary costly punishment in a public-goods-like environment with voluntary participation and a small entry fee can dramatically increase efficiency, driving the system to near-full cooperation (punisher state) and minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss formula (4/(8+M)) can be used to predict the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game, given the number of users. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values, provided the multiplexing/diversity factor is above 1 and defectors can be reliably detected. The model is most applicable to dynamic, anonymous, and voluntary participation settings, and less so to settings with continuous contribution levels or where punishment/reward is not voluntary.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Toriumi, F., & Sasaki, T. (2015). The Effect of Incentives and Meta-incentives on the Evolution of Cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game framework, cooperation and high efficiency (group payoff) can only be stably achieved if the incentive system includes second-order incentives for first-order rewards. Systems with only first-order punishment or reward, or with second-order incentives for punishment but not for reward, cannot maintain cooperation or efficiency in the long run due to the second-order free-rider problem. The cost-effect ratio of incentives (how much impact per unit cost) is a critical parameter: higher ratios make it easier for cooperation to be sustained. The model predicts that systems with second-order rewards for first-order incentives are more robust and can achieve high efficiency with fewer incentive-providers than systems with second-order punishments. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency but gives qualitative and parametric conditions for when efficiency is high or low under different incentive structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment and reward systems (including meta-incentives) on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient for high efficiency; the structure of incentives, especially the presence of second-order incentives for rewarding, is crucial. If a game design includes only first-order punishment, efficiency will not improve in the long run. If second-order incentives for first-order rewards are present and the cost-effect ratio is high, efficiency can be high. The model also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional on the broader incentive structure. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence or absence of second-order incentives, and the cost-effectiveness of punishment/reward, are key moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Farjam, M., Faillo, M., Sprinkhuizen-Kuyper, I., & Haselager, P. (2015). Punishment Mechanisms and Their Effect on Cooperation: A Simulation Study. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment can facilitate higher group efficiency (average energy) in environments where resources are scarce (low MPCR), but only when the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid antisocial punishment and minimize costs (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun). Unrestricted punishment consistently reduces efficiency. When resources are abundant (high MPCR), punishment mechanisms do not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to no-punishment. The results are robust to variations in group size and punishment cost ratio. The study provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by resource availability (analogous to MPCR in PGGs) and by the design of the punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in PGG-like environments depends critically on both the marginal per-capita return (MPCR, here operationalized as resource availability) and the specific punishment mechanism. When MPCR is low (resources scarce), enabling well-designed punishment (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun) increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but unrestricted punishment reduces efficiency. When MPCR is high (resources abundant), punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of punishment on efficiency should be modeled as conditional on both MPCR and the punishment mechanism. The results are robust to moderate changes in group size and punishment cost ratio, so these dimensions are less likely to moderate the direction of the effect.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2017). Collective action and the evolution of social norm internalization. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to show that norm internalization evolves more readily and has larger effects on cooperation when peer punishment of free riders is promoted. In public-goods-like ('us-vs.-nature') games, the evolution of norm internalization and punishment increases cooperation and material payoffs (efficiency). In intergroup contest ('us-vs.-them') games, norm internalization and punishment increase cooperation but reduce material payoffs due to overproduction and rent dissipation. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in public-goods-like settings and negative in intergroup contest settings. The model also predicts that group size, punishment cost, and the strength of social norms moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the type of collective action problem. In standard public goods (us-vs.-nature) games, enabling punishment increases efficiency by reducing free riding. In intergroup contest (us-vs.-them) games, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to excessive effort and resource dissipation. The model also suggests that group size and punishment cost are important moderators. For prediction, the context of the collective action (public goods vs. contest), group size, and punishment parameters should be considered.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2013). The co-evolution of social institutions, demography, and large-scale human cooperation. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the co-evolution of institutions with sanctioning (punishment) and demography enables the stable emergence and maintenance of large-scale cooperation, resulting in a dramatic increase in group carrying capacity and efficiency. In the absence of institutionalized punishment, cooperation cannot be stably maintained, and group size/efficiency remains low. The model demonstrates that the optimal institution invests most, but not all, of the public good into technological improvement (increasing carrying capacity), while retaining enough for sanctioning to deter defectors. Sensitivity analyses show that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to changes in key parameters, though it is diminished by high migration rates or low benefits of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling institutionalized punishment in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency, especially in structured populations. The model directly links the presence and effectiveness of punishment to higher group payoffs and larger sustainable group sizes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing or strengthening punishment mechanisms should increase efficiency, provided the cost of punishment is not prohibitive and the benefit from cooperation is sufficient. The model also highlights the importance of population structure (limited migration) and the allocation of resources between sanctioning and productive investment. While the model is not an empirical experiment, its detailed mapping to public goods game structure and explicit focus on efficiency make it highly informative for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gao, L., Wang, Z., Pansini, R., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). Collective punishment is more effective than collective reward for promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective punishment is more effective than collective reward in promoting and stabilizing cooperation in multiplayer snowdrift games, especially for small groups or high initial cooperation. When the punishment-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold, a stable state of full cooperation emerges for both small and large groups, while such a state does not appear for large groups with only reward. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, which is closely related to group payoff but not a direct measure of efficiency. The results suggest that, in environments where individual-level discrimination is not possible, collective punishment can maintain high cooperation and thus higher group fitness or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in multiplayer public-goods-like environments (specifically, snowdrift games), enabling collective punishment (even when it cannot be targeted at individuals) can substantially increase the frequency and stability of cooperation, especially in small groups or when the punishment intensity is high. This implies that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of collective punishment should be expected to increase efficiency or group payoff, particularly in small groups. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation, so the findings should be interpreted as indicating a strong positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes via increased cooperation rates and stable cooperative equilibria.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2016). The impact of personal responsibility on the (un)willingness to punish non-cooperation and reward cooperation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that people are more reluctant to punish non-cooperators when they feel personally responsible (i.e., when deciding individually), but this reluctance is attenuated when responsibility is shared in a group. This effect is specific to punishment and does not apply to rewarding cooperation. The effect is mediated by feelings of personal responsibility and entitlement, not by external accountability. The experiments use a common resource dilemma with sanctioning opportunities, but do not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs. Thus, the findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral moderators of punishment willingness, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish non-cooperation is higher when punishment decisions are made jointly by groups rather than by individuals, due to reduced personal responsibility and increased entitlement. However, since the study does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or common resource games. The results are useful for predicting when punishment will be more or less likely to be used, but not for predicting the efficiency consequences of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment decision (individual vs. group) may moderate the behavioral use of punishment, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2014). On the willingness to costly reward cooperation and punish non-cooperation: The moderating role of type of social dilemma. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that participants are more likely to reward cooperative behavior than to punish non-cooperative behavior, and that this tendency is stronger in public good dilemmas (where cooperation involves giving up private property) than in common resource dilemmas (where cooperation involves refraining from taking collective property). When given a choice, most participants prefer to reward rather than punish. The size of rewards is also larger than the size of punishments, especially in public good dilemmas. The study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that the preference for reward over punishment may have implications for collective welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good and common resource games. It shows that, when given a choice, people are more willing to reward than to punish, and that punishment is used less frequently and less intensely, especially in public good dilemmas. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the mere availability of punishment may not lead to high usage, especially if reward is also available, and that the type of social dilemma (public good vs. common resource) moderates willingness to punish. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in public good dilemmas if participants are reluctant to use it, particularly when reward is also an option.

- source: Mussweiler, T., & Ockenfels, A. (2013). Similarity increases altruistic punishment in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that perceived similarity (induced by a cognitive priming task) increases the willingness to engage in costly punishment of uncooperative behavior in a two-person cooperation game with a punishment phase. In contrast, group identity (in-group vs. out-group) reduces altruistic punishment toward in-group members. The effect of similarity is not explained by group identity, and the two have opposite effects on punishment. The study does not directly measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the cooperation phase is single-shot, so there is no opportunity for punishment to feed back into increased cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that psychological framing (similarity focus vs. group identity) can significantly alter the frequency and intensity of costly punishment in a cooperation game with a punishment phase. However, it does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report most of the structural game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological moderators of punishment behavior, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design is limited.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2015). Punitive preferences, monetary incentives and tacit coordination in the punishment of defectors promote cooperation in humans. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when only one group member is needed to punish a defector (step-level public good), groups with punishment cost heterogeneity (one strong, low-cost punisher) achieve higher rates of punishment and lower rates of defection than symmetric groups. Tacit coordination on the strong punisher leads to more efficient punishment (i.e., punishment is more often carried out by only one group member). The presence of a penalty for defectors (i.e., punishment that also harms the defector) is necessary to reduce defection rates. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment cost heterogeneity can improve the effectiveness and efficiency of norm enforcement in such games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment cost heterogeneity (i.e., having a designated or focal punisher with lower costs) increases the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment in step-level public good games, as measured by behavioral outcomes (punishment and defection rates). While it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, the findings suggest that game designs enabling tacit coordination on a single, low-cost punisher are likely to yield higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, especially when a penalty for defectors is present. The results are most directly relevant for step-level or volunteer's dilemma variants of public goods games, rather than standard linear PGGs. The absence of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction, but the qualitative pattern is clear: punishment is more effective and less wasteful when cost heterogeneity allows for coordination on a single punisher.

- source: Delton, A. W., Nemirow, J., Robertson, T. E., Cimino, A., & Cosmides, L. (2013). Merely Opting Out of a Public Good Is Moralized: An Error Management Approach to Cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people who opt out of contributing to public goods are moralized and sometimes punished, even if they have not yet free ridden. This preventive moralization is stronger in public goods than in club goods, and is mediated by the perceived likelihood that the non-contributor will free ride in the future. The effect is robust across implicit, explicit, and behavioral measures, and is observed in both student and nationally representative adult samples. However, the studies do not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and moralization are triggered by non-contribution in public goods contexts, even before actual free riding occurs. It demonstrates that costly punishment is more likely in public goods than in club goods, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms can increase the likelihood of punishment being used in public goods games, especially when free riding is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence about how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to informing about the behavioral context in which punishment is likely to be applied.

- source: de Melo, G., & Piaggio, M. (2015). The perils of peer punishment: Evidence from a common pool resource framed field experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Nonmonetary peer punishment (social disapproval) reduces resource extraction (increases cooperation) in mixed-community groups but has no significant effect in in-group (same community) groups. The effect is measured as a reduction in the average number of nets used per person. There is substantial antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators), which can undermine the effectiveness of punishment. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but lower extraction implies higher efficiency. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of a unique social norm limit the positive effect of punishment. The findings suggest that the context (group composition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation, and that coordination is needed for punishment to be effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling peer punishment (social disapproval) can reduce resource extraction (increase cooperation) in common pool resource games, especially in mixed-group settings. However, the effect on efficiency is not directly measured; it must be inferred from extraction levels. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of norm consensus can limit or even reverse the positive effect of punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency in mixed-group contexts than in homogeneous groups, but the risk of antisocial punishment and unclear norms can reduce or negate efficiency gains. The specific game design (field context, nonmonetary punishment, no communication, continuous extraction choice) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., Vollan, B., & Nuppenau, E. A. (2015). Resource scarcity, and democratic elections in commons dilemmas: An experiment on forest use in Ethiopia. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that resource scarcity (lower initial stock) leads to less cooperative behavior (higher HR), and that the way sanctions are implemented matters: democratically elected sanctions increase cooperation (lower HR) both under abundance and scarcity, while imposed sanctions can reduce cooperation (higher HR) under abundance (crowding-out effect), but not under scarcity. The main outcome is behavioral (HR), not efficiency or group payoff. Theoretical efficiency levels are provided, but not empirically analyzed as outcomes. The findings suggest that participatory rule-making is more effective than top-down sanctions for sustaining cooperation, especially when resources are not extremely scarce.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how the method of sanction implementation (election vs. imposition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation in a common-pool resource game with real-world framing. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results must be mapped with caution to efficiency prediction tasks. Theoretical efficiency levels are given, but empirical results focus on HR. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, no explicit MPCR, probabilistic punishment, no rewards, summary info shown, anonymous punishment) are well described, supporting mapping to prediction models. The main implication is that democratically elected sanctions are more likely to increase cooperation (and thus efficiency) than imposed sanctions, especially under resource abundance. Under scarcity, the difference is smaller, but elections still outperform imposition.

- source: Tang, T. Z., & Ye, H. (2016). The Blessing of Sexuality: Evolution of Altruism with Mating Preference. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulation results show that altruistic punishment alone does not sustain high levels of cooperation; defectors tend to dominate unless sexual attractiveness (mating preference for altruists) is present. When both punishment and sexual attractiveness are enabled, altruists (cooperators and punishers) become dominant, and the mean altruistic investment approaches its maximum. Variable population size and individual variation in investment further amplify the prevalence of altruism. The model is a close variant of a public goods game, but the main outcomes are population composition and mean investment, not efficiency or group payoff. Thus, the findings are most relevant for predicting the conditions under which punishment can sustain high cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment, when combined with additional incentives (such as sexual attractiveness or mating preference for altruists), can lead to high levels of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. However, in the absence of such additional incentives, punishment alone may not sustain cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment. Instead, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on the presence of complementary mechanisms that reward altruists. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment's positive effect on efficiency is conditional and may be weak or absent without additional incentives.

- source: Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). A bottom-up institutional approach to cooperative governance of risky commons. *Nature Climate Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that local, group-level sanctioning institutions are much more effective than global, population-level institutions at promoting successful collective action in a threshold public goods game with risk of collective loss. Local institutions increase the fraction of groups that reach the public good threshold (group achievement), especially when the perceived risk of failure is low. Global institutions are unstable and provide only marginal improvements. The effect is stronger in smaller groups. The model does not report efficiency or total payoff, but the results strongly suggest that local punishment institutions increase the likelihood of group success, which is closely related to efficiency in this context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling local, group-level punishment institutions in threshold public goods games (with risk of collective loss) will increase the rate of successful group outcomes compared to no punishment or global punishment. While the model does not report efficiency or total payoff, the 'group achievement' outcome is a close proxy for efficiency in threshold settings. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when punishment is local, groups are small, and risk perception is low. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so its value is mainly in qualitative prediction and mechanism understanding.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2013). Individual heterogeneity and costly punishment: a volunteer's dilemma. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that costly punishment, when implemented as a volunteer's dilemma, is effective in deterring defection (stealing) in groups. When only one group member needs to punish and punishment costs are heterogeneous, punishment is more efficiently coordinated (mainly by the lowest-cost punisher), and deterrence of defection is as strong as with much higher penalties in a symmetric-cost setting. The introduction of punishment reduces the stealing rate by about 50 percentage points overall, with greater reductions in conditions with high penalty or with cost heterogeneity. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment can sustain cooperation and that heterogeneity in punishment costs facilitates more effective and efficient norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong behavioral evidence that introducing punishment in a volunteer's dilemma setting (where only one punisher is needed) greatly reduces defection rates, especially when punishment costs are heterogeneous. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will likely increase efficiency (by reducing defection) in similar settings, and that cost heterogeneity among punishers can substitute for higher penalty size in deterring defection. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, the quantitative effect on efficiency must be inferred indirectly from the large reductions in defection rates. The findings are most applicable to step-level public goods games with punishment opportunities and cost heterogeneity among punishers.

- source: Johnson, S. (2015). Escaping the tragedy of the commons through targeted punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in large-N social dilemmas (modeled after public goods games), targeted punishment strategies—where only a small, strategically chosen subset of defectors are punished at a time—can enable a small number of punishers to shift a population from full defection to full cooperation. This is in contrast to standard punishment strategies, which require much higher punishment capacity to achieve the same result. The main outcome is the stationary proportion of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The model is robust to heterogeneity in player predispositions and to some randomness in the ordering of players. The findings are relevant for understanding how to design punishment institutions or strategies to escape the tragedy of the commons, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the structure and targeting of punishment can strongly affect the ability to achieve cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when initial cooperation is low. However, because the model's outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results suggest that, all else equal, targeted punishment strategies may be more effective at increasing cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) than uniform punishment, but the lack of direct payoff analysis limits quantitative prediction. The model assumes binary (all-or-nothing) contribution choices, no explicit payoff or MPCR parameters, and does not model communication, reward, or information features. Thus, its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment targeting and heterogeneity, rather than providing parameter-specific efficiency predictions.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2015). The reputation of punishers. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the reputation consequences of punishment are context-dependent. In forced play games where punishment is possible, a punitive reputation can induce fear and stabilize cooperation, potentially supporting group efficiency. However, when partner choice is available, individuals with a nonpunishing cooperative reputation are typically preferred over punishers, unless punishment is the only available signal of cooperative intent. The efficiency of punishment (fee:fine ratio) also moderates reputational outcomes: 'efficient' punishment (fee:fine > 1) may be viewed as self-serving, while 'inefficient' punishment (fee:fine < 1) is more likely to be seen as genuinely norm-enforcing. The paper synthesizes prior empirical and theoretical work but does not provide new data or direct estimates of efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is most useful for identifying moderators and mechanisms that may affect the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public goods games and similar environments. It highlights the importance of partner choice, the efficiency of punishment, and the availability of alternative signals of cooperation. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is primarily in identifying which dimensions are likely to be important moderators (e.g., partner choice, punishment efficiency, information structure).

- source: Simpson, B., & Willer, R. (2015). Beyond Altruism: Sociological Foundations of Cooperation and Prosocial Behavior. *Annual Review of Sociology, Vol 41*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews sociological mechanisms that promote cooperation in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It finds that both formal (centralized) and informal (peer) punishment can increase cooperation, but the welfare effects are ambiguous due to the costs of sanctioning and potential for over- or under-punishment. Communication and endogenous rule formation improve outcomes. Centralized punishment (e.g., by elected leaders) is more effective and less prone to retaliation than peer punishment. However, the presence of sanctions and extrinsic incentives can undermine intrinsic motivation and trust, leading to lower cooperation when such mechanisms are removed. Reputation and status also promote cooperation, but can introduce ambiguity about motivations. Network structure (e.g., density, centrality, dynamic ties) shapes cooperation, with denser and more dynamic networks generally supporting higher cooperation, but also potentially undermining generalized trust.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides high-level theoretical and empirical synthesis relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in public goods games and similar environments. It supports the general finding that punishment increases cooperation, but cautions that the net effect on efficiency or group welfare depends on the costs and structure of punishment, the presence of communication, and whether enforcement is centralized or peer-based. The review suggests that communication and endogenous institution formation are important moderators, and that centralized punishment is more effective than decentralized peer punishment. However, it also warns that punishment can undermine intrinsic motivation and trust, potentially reducing efficiency in the long run. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent and moderated by game design features such as communication, institution formation, and enforcement structure, but does not provide quantitative estimates or direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Joireman, J., Parks, C. D., & Van Dijk, E. (2013). The psychology of social dilemmas: A review. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The review concludes that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemmas, including public goods games, but their effectiveness depends on factors such as trust, group size, decentralization, and cultural context. The paper notes that punishment can sometimes have adverse effects, such as reducing cooperation if sanctions are low or undermining intrinsic motivation. It also discusses the importance of communication, reputation, and structural solutions (e.g., leadership, local management) in sustaining cooperation. While the review references meta-analyses and empirical studies showing positive effects of punishment and reward on cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment (and reward) mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods and related social dilemmas, and that their effects are moderated by structural and psychological factors. However, it does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions. For prediction tasks, this review supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will tend to increase cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency data limits its value for precise quantitative prediction. The review is most useful for identifying moderators and mechanisms that may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other studies.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., Vogel, M., & Singer, T. (2017). Caring Cooperators and Powerful Punishers: Differential Effects of Induced Care and Power Motivation on Different Types of Economic Decision Making. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Inducing a Care motive increases a latent cooperation factor (which includes public goods contributions), while Power induction increases a punishment factor (punishment in 2nd/3rd party punishment games, ultimatum, impunity). The two factors are largely orthogonal. Care increases cooperation relative to control, but does not increase punishment. Power increases punishment relative to Care, but not relative to control. No direct efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and cooperation are distinct behavioral tendencies, and that motivational context (Care vs. Power) can differentially affect them. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does suggest that punishment behavior can be increased by Power motives, but this is not linked to group payoff or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by clarifying that punishment and cooperation are separable and can be independently manipulated.

- source: Freytag, A., Güth, W., Koppel, H., & Wangler, L. (2014). Is regulation by milestones efficiency enhancing? An experimental study of environmental protection. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This laboratory experiment tests whether adding intermediate contribution targets ('milestones') to a cumulative threshold public goods game increases efficiency, measured by group payoffs and the probability of reaching a final target. The game is played in groups of 5 over 6 rounds, with treatments varying the strictness of milestones, the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), and the probability of group loss if targets are missed. The main findings are: (1) In a scenario with no continuous efficiency gains (i.e., only reaching the target matters), strict milestones increase both contributions and the probability of reaching the target, thus enhancing efficiency. (2) In a scenario with continuous efficiency gains (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones actually reduce average contributions and efficiency, as groups focus on meeting milestones rather than maximizing payoffs. (3) In an intermediate scenario, the positive and negative effects cancel out. The effect of milestones is thus context-dependent, and their efficiency impact is not universally positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like milestone mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure. When the only way to generate group payoffs is to reach a threshold (no continuous efficiency gains), strict milestones (punishment for missing intermediate targets) can improve efficiency by increasing coordination and contributions. However, when continuous efficiency gains are present (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones can reduce efficiency, as they may distract from maximizing group welfare. For prediction, this means that the presence and structure of milestone/punishment mechanisms, the marginal per-capita return, and the risk of group loss must all be considered. Control efficiency (without milestones/punishment) is a strong moderator: if control efficiency is already high, milestones may not help and can even hurt. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions in cumulative threshold public goods games with milestone/punishment features.

- source: Griffin, C., & Belmonte, A. (2017). Cyclic public goods games: Compensated coexistence among mutual cheaters stabilized by optimized penalty taxation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a cyclic public goods game with three species, the system without penalty taxation is unstable and leads to the dominance of a single species, with the interior coexistence point being a point of minimal total population growth (i.e., lowest efficiency). Imposing a penalty tax (punishment) on population growth can stabilize the interior fixed point and allow for coexistence of all species, but this tax is not redistributed and thus reduces overall efficiency further. The optimal tax is the minimal one that achieves stability, but any positive tax reduces total population growth below the already minimal level at the coexistence point. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include empirical or simulation data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a cyclic public goods game with three types of mutual cheaters, introducing a penalty tax (punishment) can stabilize coexistence but always reduces total population growth (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment case. The penalty is not redistributed, so it is a pure cost. Thus, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (as a non-redistributive tax) is predicted to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most relevant for settings with cyclic/multispecies structure and non-redistributive punishment, and less so for standard two-strategy PGGs or for punishment that is redistributed. The results are directly informative for predicting the direction of efficiency change (less positive) when enabling punishment in such models.

- source: Asgharpourmasouleh, A., Sadeghi, A., & Yousofi, A. (2017). A Grounded Agent-Based Model of Common Good Production in a Residential Complex: Applying Artificial Experiments. *Sage Open*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The agent-based model simulates a common good game in a residential complex, with agent strategies grounded in qualitative fieldwork. The model shows that in blocks with more defectors, common good production and payoffs collapse. Artificial experiments demonstrate that (1) introducing punishers in place of defectors, (2) increasing all agents' desirability for the common good, and (3) combining both interventions, all increase common good production and total payoffs. The most dramatic improvement occurs when both punishers are introduced and desirability is maximized, resulting in all agents adopting punishing strategies and achieving the highest payoffs and common good production. Thus, punishment mechanisms, especially when agents value the common good, can transform a low-efficiency, low-payoff equilibrium into a high-efficiency, high-payoff one.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (and increasing the number of punishers) in a public-goods-like environment can increase efficiency and group payoffs, especially when agents also value the common good. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar, with continuous contributions, punishment, and payoff functions analogous to PGGs. The results suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment will increase efficiency, particularly in settings where baseline cooperation is fragile or low. The effect is amplified if agents' intrinsic motivation (desirability) for the common good is high. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low.

- source: Sääksvuori, L. (2014). Intergroup conflict, ostracism, and the evolution of cooperation under free migration. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a dynamic public goods game with endogenous group formation, free migration, and treatments varying the presence of intergroup competition and ostracism (social exclusion by majority vote). The main findings are: (1) Ostracism increases average group size and contributions to the public good, resulting in higher net payoffs and aggregate returns, both with and without intergroup competition. (2) Intergroup competition alone does not increase group size or contributions, and leads to more imbalanced group structures, which reduces aggregate returns. (3) The combination of ostracism and competition still produces higher cooperation and payoffs than competition alone. (4) The threat of ostracism reduces the number of free-agents and increases the desirability of joining groups, outweighing the effect of involuntary exclusion. (5) Intergroup competition reduces the likelihood of ostracism, as groups are less willing to expel members when facing external threats.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social sanctioning mechanism (ostracism) in a dynamic, migration-allowed public goods environment increases both cooperation and group-level payoffs (i.e., efficiency), regardless of whether intergroup competition is present. The effect of ostracism is robust and positive for efficiency-related outcomes. Intergroup competition alone does not improve efficiency and may even reduce it by fragmenting groups. The design features—such as endogenous group formation, free migration, and ostracism by majority vote—are important moderators. The paper does not use classic costly punishment (with explicit cost per punishment point), but ostracism functions as a group-level sanction. Thus, for environments with similar group formation and migration, the presence of ostracism is a strong positive predictor for efficiency gains from sanctioning.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2017). When is bigger better? The effects of group size on the evolution of helping behaviours. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that as group size increases, the marginal benefits of helping, the effectiveness of reciprocity, and relatedness all tend to decrease, making cooperation less likely to evolve or be stable in large groups. Punishment can stabilize cooperation, but its effectiveness also declines with group size unless institutional mechanisms are in place. The authors argue that human societies have overcome these limitations through the evolution of institutions that coordinate sanctioning and information sharing, thereby enabling large-scale cooperation. The paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding how game design features (such as group size, punishment, and information structure) interact to affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative predictions or new data on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and institutional punishment on cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that, all else equal, increasing group size will tend to reduce the effectiveness of both reciprocity and punishment in sustaining cooperation, unless institutional mechanisms are present to coordinate sanctioning and information. The paper highlights the importance of considering diminishing marginal returns, monitoring costs, and second-order free-riding in large groups. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in larger groups unless institutional features (e.g., centralized punishment, coordinated monitoring) are present. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates or experimental data for efficiency outcomes, so its support is primarily conceptual and mechanistic.

- source: Schlüter, M., Tavoni, A., & Levin, S. (2016). Robustness of norm-driven cooperation in the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that cooperation enforced by social sanctioning is robust under certain conditions but can collapse with increased resource abundance or small increases in resource variability. High resource variability or scarcity can promote cooperation. The effectiveness of sanctioning depends on the proportion of norm followers (social capital). When two groups are ecologically connected, resource leakage from a more cooperative group can destabilize cooperation in the less cooperative group. The model highlights the complex, nonlinear interplay between social and ecological factors in sustaining cooperation. However, the main outcome is the level of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (here, non-costly social disapproval) on cooperation in CPR/public goods settings, especially under varying resource conditions. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. The model's focus on non-costly, endogenous social sanctioning differs from standard experimental punishment treatments. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited and mainly contextual/mechanistic.

- source: Murnighan, J. K., & Wang, L. (2016). The social world as an experimental game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews decades of research on experimental games, including public goods games and social dilemmas. It highlights that cooperation is common even when game theory predicts defection, and that punishment and reward mechanisms can influence cooperation rates. Altruistic punishment is often found to encourage cooperation, but punishment can also have negative side effects such as reducing trust or inducing revenge. The review notes that the structure and framing of games, as well as social context, can significantly affect behavior. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative context for how punishment and reward mechanisms may affect cooperation in public goods and social dilemma games. It suggests that punishment can increase cooperation, but may also have negative side effects. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its value is mainly in contextualizing the importance of design features such as group size, framing, and the presence of punishment or reward, rather than providing quantitative prediction support.

- source: Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2017). Helping in humans and other animals: a fruitful interdisciplinary dialogue. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that mechanisms such as punishment and reward, studied extensively in human public goods games, are also relevant in non-human animal cooperation.
