It reviews how laboratory studies (often using public goods or n-player social dilemma games) have inspired comparative research, and how features like punishment, partner choice, and communication can affect cooperation rates. The authors emphasize the need for more ecologically valid experiments and for understanding how laboratory findings translate to real-world helping and cooperation. The review notes that while punishment and reward can increase cooperation in public goods games, most evidence is about behavioral outcomes, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for how punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, but does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators that may influence the effect of punishment on cooperation, but cannot be used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of ecological validity, the role of communication, and the need to consider real-world payoff structures.

- source: Wu, B., García, J., Hauert, C., & Traulsen, A. (2013). Extrapolating Weak Selection in Evolutionary Games. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games with finite populations, the ranking of strategies by abundance (e.g., cooperation, defection, punishment) can change as the intensity of selection increases. This is shown for public goods games with punishment as well as for general multiplayer and multi-strategy games. The main implication is that predictions based on weak selection (where payoffs have little effect on fitness) may not hold for moderate or strong selection. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on which strategies are most common under different evolutionary conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it shows that the abundance of strategies (including punishers) can change non-monotonically with selection intensity, and that results from weak selection may not extrapolate to stronger selection. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests caution in using weak selection results to predict efficiency effects of punishment, especially in games with more than two strategies or players. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency is limited.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Mobilia, M., Jiang, L. L., Szczesny, B., Rucklidge, A. M., & Perc, M. (2014). Cyclic dominance in evolutionary games: a review. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The review highlights that cyclic dominance can emerge in evolutionary games with three or more strategies, including PGG variants with volunteering, punishment, and reward. In structured populations, spatial structure and mobility play crucial roles in maintaining coexistence and generating complex patterns (e.g., spirals, waves). Punishment and reward can stabilize cooperation through cyclic dominance, but the outcomes are typically described in terms of coexistence and oscillatory dynamics rather than efficiency or group payoff. The review also notes that alliances and subsystem solutions can act as effective strategies, further complicating the dynamics. Theoretical and simulation results are emphasized, with limited direct reference to empirical payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative evidence that the introduction of punishment (and reward) in PGG-like environments can promote the coexistence of cooperative strategies through cyclic dominance, especially in structured populations. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this review supports the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation and prevent collapse to all-defection, but it does not inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes. The evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., spatial structure, mobility, network topology) rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Vollan, B., Prediger, S., & Frölich, M. (2013). Co-managing common-pool resources: Do formal rules have to be adapted to traditional ecological norms?. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This field-lab experiment with communal farmers in southern Africa shows that the efficiency of formal, democratically chosen rules for managing a common-pool resource depends on their alignment with local ecological norms. The quota rule (limiting extraction) increases group earnings and resource availability in both high and low sustainability groups, but is unpopular. The rotation rule only increases efficiency in low sustainability groups (who previously failed to coordinate), but is ineffective or even counterproductive in high sustainability groups (who already tacitly coordinated for sustainability), due to high rates of rule breaking. The lottery rule is ineffective in both types of groups. Rule compliance is lower when rules conflict with local norms, and rule breaking is not mainly motivated by profit. Thus, the effect of rule-based interventions on efficiency is highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of introducing a formal rule (akin to a punishment or restriction mechanism) on efficiency in a common-pool resource game, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect depends on the compatibility of the rule with pre-existing group norms. Rules that align with local sustainability norms (e.g., quotas in groups that value sustainability) can increase efficiency, while rules that conflict with these norms (e.g., rotation in high-norm groups) may reduce efficiency due to non-compliance. The presence of a sanctioning mechanism (imperfect monitoring and penalty) is not sufficient to ensure compliance or efficiency gains. Thus, the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency is moderated by group-level norms and prior behavior, not just by the formal game design. This paper is highly informative for settings where the main intervention is a formal rule or restriction, but less so for classic peer-punishment PGGs.

- source: Schonmann, R. H., & Boyd, R. (2016). A simple rule for the evolution of contingent cooperation in large groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that contingent cooperation in large groups can invade and persist if relatedness is above a threshold that depends sensitively on the threshold fraction of cooperators required for cooperation to be sustained. The required relatedness increases rapidly as the threshold for cooperation increases. The number of repeated interactions (iterations) required for cooperation to invade also increases rapidly with the threshold and decreases with relatedness. The model shows that, under realistic levels of relatedness, only strategies that require cooperation from a small fraction of the group can invade; strategies that require near-universal cooperation are unlikely to evolve. The paper argues that contingent punishment (not formally modeled here) is a more plausible mechanism for the evolution of widespread cooperation, because even a small fraction of punishers can induce high levels of cooperation. The results are supported by mathematical derivations and numerical simulations of evolutionary dynamics in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, relatedness, and the threshold for contingent cooperation affect the evolutionary success (and thus efficiency) of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It does not directly model or analyze punishment, but its results suggest that punishment-based mechanisms are more likely to support high efficiency in large groups than pure contingent cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper's formulas can be used to estimate the conditions under which cooperation (and thus high efficiency) can be sustained, given the group structure and behavioral rules. However, because the paper does not model explicit punishment or compare efficiency with and without punishment, it provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Richter, A., & Grasman, J. (2013). The transmission of sustainable harvesting norms when agents are conditionally cooperative. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of conditional cooperators in a common-pool resource setting can lead to complex and sometimes abrupt transitions between cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria. Efficiency, measured as resource stock and group profit relative to the maximum economic yield, is highly sensitive to social dynamics. When temptation to defect increases or when group size increases, the system can shift from high-efficiency (cooperative) to low-efficiency (defective) states, sometimes with hysteresis or limit cycles. The model highlights that social norm enforcement via moral persuasion can sustain cooperation, but is vulnerable to collapse if conditional cooperators lose motivation. The absence of explicit punishment means that norm enforcement is 'soft' and collapses are possible even without changes in external parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like common-pool resource games, the efficiency of group outcomes is highly sensitive to the social composition (intrinsic vs. conditional cooperators vs. defectors) and to the strength of norm enforcement mechanisms. While it does not model explicit punishment, it suggests that in the absence of strong enforcement (e.g., costly punishment), efficiency is fragile and can collapse due to endogenous social dynamics. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that enabling or strengthening norm enforcement (potentially via punishment) could stabilize high-efficiency outcomes, especially in larger groups or when temptation to defect is high. However, the model also warns that even with some enforcement, efficiency can be lost if social dynamics shift. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is absent or weak, and thus provide a baseline for understanding the potential value of adding punishment.

- source: Richter, A., van Soest, D., & Grasman, J. (2013). Contagious cooperation, temptation, and ecosystem collapse. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a closed community harvesting a renewable resource, cooperation can be sustained through social norm contagion, but is vulnerable to collapse if the temptation to defect becomes too strong (e.g., due to increased population or technology). The system can flip from a high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibrium to a low-efficiency (defective) equilibrium, with substantial welfare loss. Hysteresis is present: reversing the parameter change that caused collapse is not sufficient to restore cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for welfare under cooperation and Nash equilibrium, showing large efficiency losses after collapse. The presence of social norm diffusion (but not explicit punishment) can sustain cooperation up to a tipping point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the efficiency dynamics of common-pool resource games with social norm diffusion, but it does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is maintained by social pressure, persuasion, or contagion, rather than by costly punishment. The model shows that efficiency can be high and robust up to a tipping point, after which collapse is sudden and efficiency is lost. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence about the role of population size, technology, and social norm strength in determining efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of enabling or disabling punishment. It is best used as a theoretical baseline for environments without explicit punishment.

- source: Hashim, M. J., Kannan, K. N., Maximiano, S., & Ulmer, J. R. (2014). Digital Piracy, Teens, and the Source of Advice: An Experimental Study. *Journal of Management Information Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Advice from parents, especially when they are punished for their child's piracy, increases purchasing and group profit and reduces piracy compared to other advice sources. Advice from record labels is the least effective and can even reduce group profit. Advice from a regulator is largely ignored. The effect of advice is strongest when the adviser has both a social tie and a stake in the outcome. The study does not include standard peer punishment or reward mechanisms; the only 'punishment' is an exogenous penalty to parents in one treatment. The game is a close variant of a public goods game, but not a standard PGG. Efficiency-related outcomes (group profit, purchasing) are reported, but not as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that exogenous punishment of a third party (parents) for a player's free-riding can increase public good provision and group profit, especially when combined with a social tie. However, it does not test endogenous peer punishment or standard PGG punishment institutions. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms may be more effective when they are socially salient and affect someone with a close tie to the player, but direct transfer to standard PGG with peer punishment is limited. Control efficiency (group profit without punishment) is reported, and the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (parent penalty) can be inferred, but the mechanism differs from standard punishment. The absence of peer punishment, reward, or communication means the results are only adjacent for direct prediction of PGG punishment effects.

- source: Howe, E. L., Murphy, J. J., Gerkey, D., & West, C. T. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity, Resource Sharing, and Environmental Risk: Evidence from Field Experiments in Siberia. *Plos One*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment found that voluntary sharing was substantial and directed toward those in need, especially when reputations could be built. Sharing was conditioned on cooperative behavior when such information was available. However, the presence of sharing, rewards, and reputation mechanisms did not increase contributions to the public good (i.e., did not increase cooperation in the production domain). The expected returns from contributing to the group activity, even with the possibility of receiving sharing, were not high enough to incentivize greater cooperation. Thus, the mechanisms studied did not improve group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in environments where sharing is voluntary and not highly efficient, and where rewards/punishments are not strong or direct, the introduction of sharing and reputation mechanisms may not increase group efficiency or cooperation in public goods games. The findings imply that unless the payoff structure makes rewards or punishments highly effective, simply enabling sharing or reputation is unlikely to boost efficiency. For prediction, if the control game has low efficiency and the only change is the addition of a voluntary, non-committal sharing/reward mechanism (with no direct punishment and no high-impact reward), the efficiency in the treatment is unlikely to increase. The paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms depends on their efficiency and observability.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2015). Institutional Sources of Legitimate Authority: An Experimental Investigation. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that citizens are more willing to help salaried (fixed-compensation) authorities than those who personally benefit from collected penalties, but only when transparency is high. When transparency is low, support for salaried authorities collapses, while support for appropriator authorities is low regardless of transparency. Contribution rates and average earnings are reported, but do not differ significantly between treatments under full information; under limited information, contribution rates are lower for salaried authorities. The main outcome is a behavioral measure of legitimacy (citizen intervention), not efficiency or group payoff. The deterrence index (successful punishment of noncontributors minus punishment of contributors) is associated with higher contributions, but the causal effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly estimated.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how institutional features (transparency, authority incentives) affect citizen support for enforcement and, through deterrence, contribution rates. However, it does not directly estimate the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The design is a binary (all-or-nothing) public goods game with centralized, probabilistic punishment and a unique intervention stage. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of punishment (as measured by citizen support) is sensitive to transparency and authority incentives, which may in turn affect deterrence and contributions, but the direct impact on efficiency is not quantified. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative about mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct treatment effect estimates for efficiency.

- source: Shirado, H., Fu, F., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Quality versus quantity of social ties in experimental cooperative networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing dynamic partner selection in a networked cooperation game leads to the highest levels of cooperation at intermediate rates of network rewiring. At low rewiring rates, players accumulate many ties, including to defectors, which undermines cooperation. At very high rewiring rates, defectors can re-attach to cooperators faster than cooperators can detach, also undermining cooperation. The ability to break ties with defectors (interpreted as a form of social punishment) helps maintain cooperation, but only when the rate of change is not too high or too low. The main outcome is the cooperation rate; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of dynamic partner selection (ostracism-like social punishment) on cooperation in networked public goods games. It suggests that enabling players to break ties with defectors can sustain cooperation, but only at intermediate rates of network change. However, because the study does not include explicit costly punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social exclusion or partner choice is the main sanctioning mechanism, rather than costly punishment. The results suggest that the design dimension of network dynamism (rewiring rate) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation, which may translate to efficiency if payoff outcomes were measured.

- source: Yoeli, E., Hoffman, M., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Powering up with indirect reciprocity in a large-scale field experiment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that making participation in a voluntary demand response program observable to neighbors nearly triples participation rates compared to an anonymous condition. The effect of observability is much larger than that of a $25 monetary incentive. The effect is strongest in contexts where reputational concerns are likely to matter more (e.g., apartment buildings, homeowners vs. renters, and when the program is framed as a public good). The results support the theory that indirect reciprocity and reputational incentives can drive large-scale cooperation in real-world public goods dilemmas. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing observability (reputational incentives) can dramatically increase participation in a real-world public goods context, especially when social ties and public good framing are salient. However, because the outcome is participation rate (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. It does, however, suggest that non-monetary, reputational incentives can be more effective than small monetary incentives in increasing cooperation.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2016). How the expanded crowd-funding mechanism of some southern rural areas in China affects cooperative behaviors in threshold public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both publishing the list of cooperators and the presence of lobbyists can significantly increase the number of cooperators in a threshold public goods game, especially when the synergy factor is not too high. The effectiveness of these mechanisms depends on the population structure (proportion of unconditional cooperators vs. defectors), the synergy factor, and emotional influence parameters. When the synergy factor is high, publishing the list is always beneficial and the role of lobbyists becomes negligible. When the synergy factor is low, the decision to publish the list should consider the relative numbers of cooperators and defectors and the strength of emotional inhibition. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social recognition (publishing the list) and persuasive agents (lobbyists) can increase cooperation rates in threshold public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or similar mechanisms on efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which social mechanisms affect cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Hurlstone, M. J., Wang, S., Price, A., Leviston, Z., & Walker, I. (2017). Cooperation studies of catastrophe avoidance: implications for climate negotiations. *Climatic Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in collective-risk social dilemma games is strongly affected by perceived risk, inequality, uncertainty about thresholds, intergenerational discounting, and the possibility of reputation-based rewards or punishments. High perceived risk, communication (pledges), intermediate targets, and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation (i.e., the proportion of groups that avert catastrophe). Inequality and uncertainty about the threshold for catastrophe reduce cooperation. The paper discusses the need for credible enforcement mechanisms (punishment) in real-world climate agreements but does not review experiments that directly implement or vary punishment in the game. The main findings are about behavioral cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the importance of enforcement (punishment) mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in public-goods-like threshold games, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. It is useful for understanding moderators of cooperation and the conceptual role of punishment, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a public goods game. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that credible punishment mechanisms are likely to be important, especially under uncertainty, but does not provide data or effect sizes.

- source: Aktipis, A. (2016). Principles of cooperation across systems: from human sharing to multicellularity and cancer. *Evolutionary Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper identifies three general principles that facilitate cooperation: (1) the ability to 'Walk Away' from uncooperative partners, (2) need-based resource transfers, and (3) the necessity of increasingly sophisticated cheater suppression mechanisms as cooperation scales up. It reviews agent-based models (from prior work) showing that conditional movement (Walk Away) can stabilize cooperation in both dyadic and group contexts, including public goods game-like environments. The paper discusses how positive assortment (cooperators interacting more with each other) is key to the evolution of cooperation. It also highlights the challenges of cheater suppression in large-scale systems and draws analogies between human societies, multicellular organisms, and social insects. However, it does not provide new empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it directly analyze the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on mechanisms that support cooperation, such as partner choice (Walk Away) and cheater suppression, which are relevant to understanding the effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. The discussion of cheater suppression includes punishment as one possible mechanism but is not specific or detailed enough to inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The most relevant insight is that as cooperative systems scale up, more sophisticated and costly cheater suppression mechanisms (including punishment) are required, but the paper does not specify the efficiency consequences of these mechanisms in controlled game environments.

- source: Zhang, J. L., & Zhang, C. Y. (2015). The evolution of altruism in spatial threshold public goods games via an insurance mechanism. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public goods games, offering insurance to cooperators (but not defectors) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies, especially when the compensation for failed games is high and the threshold for success is high. However, if defectors are also allowed to purchase insurance, insured defection can become dominant, leading to a decrease in cooperative behaviors. The network structure (random regular vs. scale-free) also moderates these effects. The main outcomes are the steady-state fractions of each strategy; efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, but can be inferred from the modeled payoffs. The results suggest that insurance can substitute for some risk-mitigating effects of punishment, but the mechanism is distinct.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies insurance as a risk-mitigation mechanism rather than punishment or sanctions. However, it does provide indirect evidence about how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can affect cooperation and, by implication, group welfare. The findings suggest that mechanisms which reduce the risk of loss for contributors (such as insurance) can promote cooperation, but if such mechanisms are also available to defectors, they may undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of risk and insurance, not punishment. It does not provide direct parameterizations for the target game design dimensions related to punishment.

- source: Majolo, B., & Maréchal, L. (2017). Between-group competition elicits within-group cooperation in children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that simulated between-group competition (BGC) increases within-group cooperation among children aged 3-10, as measured by the number of stickers shared with group members (experiment 1) and the number of stickers contributed to a public good (experiment 2). The effect is robust to age (especially for children 6+), and is not explained by gender, kinship, or familiarity. However, the experiments do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main dependent variables are behavioral measures of cooperation, not payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does provide indirect evidence that between-group competition can increase cooperative behavior in children, but since punishment is not manipulated or measured, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are partially reported, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper should not be weighted heavily for the prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2016). Take One for the Team! Individual Heterogeneity and the Emergence of Latent Norms in a Volunteer's Dilemma. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated volunteer's dilemma games, efficient public good provision emerges through different behavioral patterns depending on group structure. In symmetric groups with fixed partners, turn-taking emerges, leading to high efficiency and equal payoffs. In asymmetric groups, especially when the lowest-cost actor is much stronger or when roles are randomized each round, solitary volunteering by the strong actor dominates, also leading to high efficiency. When distributional conflict is present (fixed roles), efficiency is somewhat lower due to reluctance of strong actors to always volunteer. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus provides no evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any condition. However, it is informative about baseline efficiency levels in repeated volunteer's dilemma games under various structural conditions (symmetry/asymmetry, matching protocol). For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions for step-level public good games with similar parameters, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Skatova, A., Spence, A., Leygue, C., & Ferguson, E. (2017). Guilty repair sustains cooperation, angry retaliation destroys it. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated social dilemma game without punishment, group efficiency (measured as group earnings and energy savings) declines over time due to increased defection and retaliation. Retaliation (increasing usage after being exploited) is associated with lower group payoffs, while repair (reducing usage after overusing) is associated with higher group payoffs. Groups with more prosocial members (who are more likely to repair and less likely to retaliate) achieve higher efficiency. Emotional responses (anger and guilt) mediate these behaviors: anger leads to retaliation, guilt leads to repair. The absence of punishment means that emotional self-regulation, especially guilt-driven repair, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated public-goods-like games without punishment, efficiency tends to decline over time due to retaliation and lack of repair. The presence of prosocial individuals who engage in guilty repair can partially sustain efficiency, but overall, the lack of punishment leads to lower group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be lower and decline over time, especially in groups with more selfish individuals. The findings are most relevant for control conditions (no punishment), and highlight the importance of emotional and social preference heterogeneity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot inform the incremental effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Mak, V., Zwick, R., Rao, A. R., & Pattaratanakun, J. A. (2015). Pay what you want as threshold public good provision. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that in a repeated threshold public good environment, pre-payment chat (communication) among players enables highly efficient coordination, resulting in sustained provision of the public good (PWYW) for most rounds. Without chat, groups fail to coordinate and the public good is rarely sustained. The effect of chat is robust to variations in feedback (full vs. partial information about others' payments) and market information (knowledge of player types). The main outcome is group efficiency, operationalized as the proportion of rounds in which the threshold is met and the public good continues. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism; instead, efficiency is achieved through communication and social contracting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public good games without punishment, enabling pre-play communication (chat) can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when other forms of information are limited. For prediction tasks, the presence of chat should be treated as a major moderator of efficiency in repeated threshold public good environments. The absence of punishment means the results are not directly informative about the effect of punishment per se, but they do establish a high-efficiency baseline for games with communication and no punishment. Control efficiency (no chat) is low; treatment efficiency (with chat) is high. Other design dimensions (player count, MPCR, etc.) are held constant, so the main actionable dimension is chat.

- source: Efferson, C., Roca, C. P., Vogt, S., & Helbing, D. (2016). Sustained cooperation by running away from bad behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing players to move away from their current neighborhood (endogenous movement) in a spatial social dilemma leads to higher cooperation rates and, crucially, eliminates the average payoff disadvantage of cooperation compared to defection. This is achieved through positive assortment: cooperators cluster together, shielding themselves from defectors. In the control condition (assign location), where movement is exogenous, cooperation remains costly and less frequent. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so the results are about migration/partner choice as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Instead, it demonstrates that allowing migration/partner choice can eliminate the payoff disadvantage of cooperation, even without punishment. For prediction tasks about punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., migration) can also support efficiency. If a game design includes migration but not punishment, this paper is highly relevant; if the focus is on punishment, it is not.

- source: Feltovich, N., & Grossman, P. J. (2015). How does the effect of pre-play suggestions vary with group size? Experimental evidence from a threshold public-good game. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that pre-play cheap-talk communication (suggestions) significantly increases cooperation, the frequency of efficient (all-cooperate) outcomes, and group payoffs in a one-shot threshold public-good game, but only for small groups. As group size increases, the positive effect of communication on efficiency and payoffs declines rapidly, becoming statistically insignificant or negative for groups of size 7 and 15. This pattern holds for both one-way (random leader) and all-way (full broadcast) communication treatments. The results are robust to controls for risk preferences and other covariates. The study highlights the importance of group size as a moderator of communication's effectiveness in promoting efficient outcomes in public-good-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of communication (pre-play suggestions) on efficiency in threshold public-good games is highly dependent on group size. For small groups (n=2,3), enabling communication can substantially increase efficiency relative to control. For larger groups (n=7,15), the effect is minimal or even negative. When predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, group size should be treated as a key moderator of communication's impact. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means these results are most applicable to settings where only communication is varied. The findings suggest that extrapolating positive communication effects from small-group experiments to larger groups is not warranted.

- source: Capraro, V., Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Heuristics guide the implementation of social preferences in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma experiments. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that in a one-shot, continuous-strategy PD, cooperation is trimodally distributed (none, half, all). Increasing the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c) increases the average amount of cooperation by shifting people from no cooperation to full cooperation, but does not affect the proportion of 50% cooperators. The results suggest that both efficiency concerns and heuristic reasoning (such as fairness heuristics) guide cooperation. There is no analysis of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The study is relevant for understanding how b/c affects cooperation in two-player, one-shot, continuous public goods settings, but does not inform about punishment or efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide informative evidence about how the marginal per-capita return (b/c) affects cooperation rates in a two-player, one-shot, continuous-contribution setting without punishment. It shows that higher b/c increases cooperation, but a substantial fraction of participants use heuristics (e.g., giving half) regardless of b/c. Since there is no punishment or efficiency outcome reported, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation as a function of b/c in the absence of punishment.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., & Jeong, J. (2015). Decoding covert motivations of free riding and cooperation from multi-feature pattern analysis of EEG signals. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that neural oscillations measured by EEG immediately after result presentation can predict whether a participant will cooperate or free ride in the next round, across three different payoff structures that manipulate motivations (fear and greed). Behaviorally, free riding rates are highest in the standard condition, lower in the 'no fear' condition, and lowest in the 'no greed' condition. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how payoff structure (fear/greed) affects cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Contagion of Cooperation in Static and Fluid Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in fixed and viscous networks, both cooperation and defection are contagious: individuals are more likely to switch to cooperation (or defection) if their neighbors did so in the previous round. In fluid networks, where players can more frequently change their partners, cooperation is not initially contagious—players are likely to cooperate regardless of their neighbors' behavior, likely to attract new cooperative partners. As the network stabilizes and becomes less fluid, contagion of cooperation emerges. Defection remains contagious in all network types. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show how network structure and partner choice affect the contagion of cooperation and defection in binary cooperation games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as context for how network fluidity and partner choice may interact with behavioral contagion. It does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Zhou, Y. F., Zheng, X. M., & Wu, W. W. (2015). Effect of local information within network layers on the evolution of cooperation in duplex public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a D-PGG where agents allocate all their resources to one of two network layers, the use of local information for imitation (i.e., agents observe and imitate based on payoffs from a single layer) generally promotes the prevalence of cooperative strategies compared to global information. The extent of this promotion depends on the network structure and the similarity between the two network layers. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how information structure and network multiplexity affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff or efficiency outcomes, nor for the effect of punishment. It should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, but may provide contextual background on how information and network structure can moderate cooperation.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2017). Cooperative strategies outside the laboratory - evidence from a long-term large-N-study in five countries. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a context-rich, long-term, repeated threshold public goods environment (an online game), the distribution of cooperative strategies differs from laboratory public goods games: free riding is as common as in the lab, but high cooperation is less frequent and conditional cooperation is somewhat less common. These strategies are stable over time and across different group contexts. The results suggest that context matters for the prevalence of cooperative strategies, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor on the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the baseline prevalence and stability of cooperative strategies in a long-term, context-rich threshold public goods environment without punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in the absence of punishment, free riding remains common, high cooperation is rare, and conditional cooperation is less prevalent than in some lab settings. The findings may help calibrate expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar environments, but do not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Excessive abundance of common resources deters social responsibility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a collective-risk social dilemma is most sustainable when the initial amount of common resources and the effectiveness of cooperative contributions are at intermediate levels. Excessive abundance of resources or overly effective cooperation allows defectors to free-ride, leading to the eventual collapse of cooperation and resource depletion. Conversely, too little resource or ineffective cooperation also leads to collapse. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fraction of cooperators and the sustainability of the common resource pool, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as defined in the prediction task. Its findings about resource abundance and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding resource dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. F., Chen, X. J., Gao, J., & Wang, L. (2013). Reputation-based mutual selection rule promotes cooperation in spatial threshold public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a reputation-based mutual selection rule in spatial threshold public goods games can promote cooperation, with moderate tolerance ranges and lower member thresholds being most favorable for the persistence of cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on cooperation rates under mutual selection and threshold group formation may be contextually informative for understanding how group composition and selection mechanisms affect cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes in games with or without punishment.

- source: Shi, Y. D., Zhong, L. X., & Xu, W. J. (2013). Effects of group sensitivity on cooperation in N-person snowdrift game with dynamic grouping. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in an N-person snowdrift game where agents can adjust their preferred group size based on group sensitivity (a parameter reflecting emotional response to group outcomes), moderate group sensitivity leads to the highest levels of cooperation. Both the cost-to-benefit ratio and group sensitivity affect the evolution of preferred group size and cooperation frequency. There is a functional relationship between the frequency of cooperators and the average preferred group size. However, the paper does not include punishment, sanctions, or direct efficiency/payoff analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how group size dynamics and emotional factors (group sensitivity) can affect cooperation rates, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Conflict, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both state trust (expectations of others' cooperation) and dispositional trust are positively correlated with cooperation in social dilemmas. The strength of this relationship is moderated by the degree of conflict in the game: trust is a stronger predictor of cooperation when the conflict of interest is higher (lower index of cooperation). The type of social dilemma (public goods, prisoner's dilemma, resource dilemma) does not significantly moderate the trust-cooperation relationship. Other moderators (iterations, group size, country, intergroup vs. individual) are also explored. The paper does not analyze or report on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about how the structure of the game (degree of conflict, as measured by the index of cooperation) moderates the relationship between trust and cooperation. Since efficiency is not measured, and punishment is not studied, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, but may be useful for understanding how baseline cooperation varies with game structure in the absence of punishment.

- source: Kimbrough, E. O., & Rubin, J. (2015). Sustaining Group Reputation. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated trust game with random rematching and group reputation, the introduction of an in-group punishment mechanism (jury) leads to a significant increase in the amount sent by senders, which directly translates to higher total welfare/efficiency. Information sharing about group members' experiences also increases welfare, but mainly by reinforcing positive or negative group reputations (path dependence). The effects of punishment and information sharing are additive rather than superadditive or substitutive. The presence of punishment increases both the amount sent and the amount returned, with the largest gains in the treatment where both punishment and information sharing are present. The effect of punishment is primarily due to deterrence—the threat of being punished is enough to change behavior, not just actual punishment events.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments with group reputation and random rematching, enabling a punishment mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) increases efficiency (total welfare) relative to a control with punishment disabled. The effect is robust and additive with information sharing. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be higher than with punishment disabled, especially when punishment is feasible and not prohibitively costly. The results are most directly applicable to trust games but are relevant for adjacent public-goods-like settings where group reputation and in-group punishment are possible. The paper also provides evidence that the marginal per-capita return (here, the tripling of the amount sent) is important for welfare, and that communication (cheap talk) alone does not have a significant effect. The design dimensions most critical for prediction are the presence and cost of punishment, information sharing, and the structure of the trust game.

- source: Lergetporer, P., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Sutter, M. (2014). Third-party punishment increases cooperation in children through (misaligned) expectations and conditional cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD with children increases cooperation rates from 25% (control) to 58% (punishment treatment). This leads to an increase in average payoffs from 2.49 tokens (control) to 2.98 tokens (punishment), a 20% improvement in efficiency. The effect is not due to frequent actual punishment (which is rare), but rather to children's (over-)expectation of punishment and to conditional cooperation based on higher expected partner cooperation. The effect is robust across age cohorts (7-11 years).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a two-player, one-shot PD with children substantially increases efficiency, even when actual punishment is rare. The effect is mediated by beliefs and conditional cooperation. For prediction, the presence of a third-party punishment mechanism (with a strong, binary punishment technology) in a simple, anonymous, one-shot, two-player game with no communication and known payoffs can be expected to increase efficiency by a substantial margin (here, ~20%). The effect is robust to age (within 7-11), and is not dependent on actual punishment frequency. The design is adjacent to a PGG, but the payoff structure and mechanism are closely related.

- source: Wang, Z., Jusup, M., Wang, R. W., Shi, L., Iwasa, Y., Moreno, Y., & Kurths, J. (2017). Onymity promotes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with three available actions (cooperate, defect, punish), the use of costly punishment is negatively correlated with individual payoffs in both anonymous and onymous conditions. Onymity (making opponents identifiable) increases cooperation and median payoffs, but punishment does not promote efficiency or cooperation. Instead, those who punish more tend to earn less ('winners don't punish' effect). Control trials without punishment show similar qualitative effects of onymity, suggesting that punishment is not necessary for increased cooperation or efficiency. The paper provides direct evidence that punishment, as implemented here, does not improve group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. In fact, punishment is associated with lower payoffs for those who use it. The findings suggest that, all else equal, adding a costly punishment option to a similar game design is unlikely to increase efficiency, and may even reduce it. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, discrete-choice social dilemmas with costly punishment, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to multi-player public goods games. However, the negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust in this setting.

- source: Moser, S., & Musshoff, O. (2016). Ex-ante Evaluation of Policy Measures: Effects of Reward and Punishment for Fertiliser Reduction in Palm Oil Production. *Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, holding expected income effects constant, the design of incentives (reward vs. punishment, certain vs. uncertain) significantly affects both behavioral response and efficiency. Certain rewards are the most efficient (i.e., minimize profit loss for a given reduction in fertiliser use), while uncertain punishments are the least efficient. Certain punishments and uncertain rewards are intermediate. Punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty reduces efficiency. The findings suggest that policymakers aiming for efficient reduction of negative externalities should prefer certain rewards over punishments or uncertain incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-good-like field context, the efficiency impact of punishment (vs. reward) depends strongly on the design: punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty in the incentive (probabilistic punishment or reward) further reduces efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially uncertain punishment) will not increase efficiency as much as enabling certain rewards, even if the expected value is held constant. The results are most applicable to field settings with individual decision-making and externalities, but less so to classic multi-player PGGs with peer punishment. The efficiency outcome is measured as profit difference, directly supporting payoff-based prediction.

- source: Grimalda, G., Pondorfer, A., & Tracer, D. P. (2016). Social image concerns promote cooperation more than altruistic punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, two-player prisoner's dilemma, the presence of a local authority (Big Man) as an observer increases cooperation and leads to the highest average payoffs, exceeding baseline. Introducing a costly punishment stage (where players can spend to reduce the other's payoff) does not increase cooperation above the social image condition and results in lower average payoffs than baseline, due to the costs incurred by both punisher and punished. When both social image and punishment are present, efficiency is still lower than with social image alone and remains below baseline. The presence of an external (outgroup) authority does not increase cooperation or payoffs. Thus, punishment is not an efficient mechanism for promoting cooperation in this setting, and social image concerns are more effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot, two-player PD with costly punishment, enabling punishment reduces efficiency compared to baseline (no punishment). The effect is robust: both punishment alone and punishment combined with social image concerns yield lower payoffs than baseline. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related (two-player PD with all-or-nothing contribution). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (one-shot, small group, all-or-nothing, high punishment cost), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The control (baseline) efficiency is higher than the punishment treatment efficiency. This evidence should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Ali, S. N., & Miller, D. A. (2016). Ostracism and Forgiveness. *American Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated partnership games with private monitoring and evidentiary communication, permanent ostracism (permanent exclusion of deviators) cannot achieve higher efficiency or group payoff than bilateral enforcement, because victims have incentives to conceal deviations rather than report them. This limits the effectiveness of the harshest forms of community punishment. However, if ostracism is temporary and includes forgiveness (eventual readmission of deviators), then higher levels of cooperation and group payoff can be sustained, as the prospect of future cooperation with forgiven players incentivizes truthful reporting. The paper provides formal bounds and equilibrium constructions for these results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment—specifically, whether it is permanent or temporary (with forgiveness)—is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated partnership games with private monitoring. If punishment is permanent ostracism, efficiency does not improve over bilateral enforcement. If punishment is temporary (forgiveness is possible), efficiency can be strictly higher, especially as patience or group size increases. For prediction tasks, the presence of forgiveness/temporary ostracism should be coded as a key moderator, and the effect of punishment on efficiency should be expected to be positive only when forgiveness is possible. The results are derived in a variable-stakes partnership game, which is adjacent to but not exactly a standard public goods game.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). A remarkable effect of the combination of probabilistic peerpunishment and coevolutionary mechanism on the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences, together with a coevolutionary mechanism allowing players to break and form connections, leads to a substantial increase in the average payoff of all players (i.e., group efficiency) in evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on networks. This effect is especially pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate values of the punishment coefficient. The combination of these mechanisms expands the parameter range (punishment coefficient r) over which high cooperation and high average payoff are sustained, compared to models with only one or neither mechanism. The effect is less pronounced or absent in regular networks with high average degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in evolutionary networked prisoner's dilemma games, enabling a punishment mechanism (specifically, probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences) increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when combined with a coevolutionary mechanism for network rewiring. The effect is robust across a range of network topologies and average degrees, but is most pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate punishment cost/effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar networked games is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when players can also adjust their social ties. However, the results are from a simulation model, not a human experiment, and the game is a prisoner's dilemma on a network, not a standard public goods game.

- source: Ezeigbo, I. C. (2017). How costly punishment, diversity, and density of connectivity influence cooperation in a biological network. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study uses agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks (18 players, 1000 rounds per game) to examine how costly punishment, diversity, and network connectivity affect cooperation and average payoff. Costly punishment (paying 1 unit to reduce another's payoff by 4 units) reduces defection but also significantly reduces average payoff compared to no-punishment conditions. Decreasing network connectivity (lower density of connections) increases average payoff when punishment is absent, but not when punishment is present. Diversity (randomizing connections and neighbors) can promote cooperation, but only in synergy with low connectivity. The main efficiency result is that costly punishment, while reducing defection, comes at a substantial cost to group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like network games reduces group efficiency (average payoff), even though it reduces defection. The effect is robust across different network densities and diversity conditions. If the control (no-punishment) game has higher efficiency, enabling punishment is likely to lower efficiency, unless other mechanisms (like diversity and low connectivity) are also present and interact synergistically. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in networked social dilemmas with similar payoff structures.

- source: McBride, M., Kendall, R., D'Orsogna, M. R., & Short, M. B. (2016). Crime, punishment, and evolution in an adversarial game. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, unlike standard social dilemma games (such as the public goods game), the adversarial game studied here can have multiple equilibria, including both efficient (no-crime, high-payoff) and inefficient (high-crime, low-payoff) outcomes. The presence of punishment (reporting and conviction) transforms the game from a pure social dilemma into a coordination game, where the effectiveness of punishment depends on the collective willingness to report. The Informant strategy (defectors who also report) is critical: under imitation dynamics, any presence of Informants is sufficient to reach efficiency, but under best response dynamics, Informants are necessary but not sufficient. The model shows that too severe punishment or high retaliation risk can undermine the emergence of efficient equilibria. Thus, the design of punishment institutions and the social context (e.g., support for reporting) are crucial moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like adversarial games is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment mechanism, the available strategy set (especially the presence of Informants), and the evolutionary dynamics. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate punishment severity, low retaliation risk, and the availability of strategies that combine defection and punishment. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will always improve efficiency; instead, the effect is conditional and can be negative or null if punishment is too harsh or if key strategies are unavailable. The results are most directly applicable to adversarial games with crime and reporting, but the mechanism insights may transfer to public goods games with similar punishment structures.

- source: Wang, X. L., Wu, J., Shu, G., & Li, Y. (2014). Punishment Based on Public Benefit Fund Significantly Promotes Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment funded by a public benefit fund (taxes on high earners) significantly increases both cooperation and average payoff (efficiency) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) is more effective than severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage) in raising average payoff. The effect of punishment is stronger when the temptation to defect is higher. The results are robust across square lattice and small world network topologies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with costs funded by a public benefit fund) increases group efficiency (average payoff) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, compared to no punishment. The effect is stronger for slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) than for severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage). The findings are most directly relevant to spatial PD games with local interactions, not standard PGGs, but the efficiency outcome is directly reported. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially with broad coverage and moderate intensity) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially as the temptation to defect increases.

- source: Vanderschraaf, P. (2016). In a Weakly Dominated Strategy Is Strength: Evolution of Optimality in Stag Hunt Augmented with a Punishment Option. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
