- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Conte, R., & Sabater-Mir, J. (2015). Self-Policing Through Norm Internalization: A Cognitive Solution to the Tragedy of the Digital Commons in Social Networks. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that agents capable of norm internalization (EMIL-I-A) achieve higher levels of cooperation (as measured by successful service transactions) than purely self-interested agents (IUMA) in a digital commons scenario. Internalizers flexibly adapt to environmental changes, internalizing and de-internalizing norms as conditions shift. The presence of punishment and sanction mechanisms supports norm salience and internalization, but the main driver of improved cooperation is internalization rather than external punishment alone. The model demonstrates that norm internalization can enable self-policing and robust cooperation in dynamic, decentralized environments. However, the outcomes are not reported in terms of group efficiency or total payoff as in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment and especially norm internalization mechanisms can increase cooperation and successful collective action in social dilemma-like environments. However, because the scenario is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that when agents internalize norms (potentially supported by punishment/sanction), cooperation is more robust and adaptive, but the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not isolated or quantified. The model's focus on norm salience and internalization may inform predictions about environments where norm-based motivations are strong, but it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Espín, A. M., Exadaktylos, F., & Herrmann, B. (2014). Fair and unfair punishers coexist in the Ultimatum Game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both prosocial and antisocial individuals engage in costly punishment in the Ultimatum Game, as measured by rejection of unequal offers. Prosocial punishers are those who also behave fairly in the Dictator Game, while antisocial punishers are unfair as dictators but still reject unequal offers in the Ultimatum Game. The coexistence of these types is robust across two large-scale experiments (one with a representative city sample, one with university students). The paper highlights that antisocial punishment may undermine the efficiency and stability of social systems, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it focuses on the motivations behind punishment in the Ultimatum Game. However, it offers important contextual evidence that not all punishment is prosocial or norm-enforcing; antisocial punishment can exist and may have negative implications for efficiency in broader social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency, especially if antisocial motives are prevalent. The findings are most relevant for understanding heterogeneity in punishment motives, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Koenig, B. L., & Riley, C. M. (2017). To what reference point do people calibrate cost-free, third-party punishment?. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned preferred punishments (fines) to a perpetrator in a theft scenario, with victim loss and perpetrator gain manipulated independently. Both victim loss and perpetrator gain influenced punishment preferences, with participants tending to select the larger of the two as a reference point. On average, preferred punishments exceeded the sum of victim loss and perpetrator gain. A substantial minority of participants preferred punishments much larger than this outcome differential, while a small minority preferred no punishment. When asked about compensation for the victim (rather than punishment for the perpetrator), participants' preferred amounts were lower and rarely exceeded the outcome differential. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment preferences are not simply proportional to harm or gain, but often exceed both, and that there is considerable heterogeneity in punitive preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is informative about the psychological calibration of punishment preferences in third-party contexts, showing that people often prefer punishments that more than offset both victim loss and perpetrator gain. This suggests that, in environments where third-party punishment is possible and costless, punishment levels may be set high, potentially deterring transgressions more strongly than would be predicted by models based solely on harm or gain. The findings are most relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Lönnqvist, J. E., Rilke, R. M., & Walkowitz, G. (2015). On why hypocrisy thrives: Reasonable doubt created by moral posturing can deter punishment. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in ultimatum games, proposers can reduce the likelihood of being punished for unfair offers by claiming that a random process (coin flip) determined the outcome. This effect holds even when responders do not believe the claim and is observed for both direct victims (responders) and third-party observers (judges). The reduction in punishment is not explained by responders' self-interest or gratitude. The studies do not report on group efficiency or total earnings, but the findings suggest that moral posturing can deter punishment and thus may allow unfair behavior to persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of plausible deniability (moral posturing) can reduce the frequency of punishment in bargaining games, even when punishment is available and even if the claim is not believed. However, the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of uncertainty or doubt about intent, rather than the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wu, Y., Zhang, B., & Zhang, S. H. (2017). Probabilistic reward or punishment promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The introduction of a third-party agent who probabilistically rewards cooperators and punishes defectors increases the fraction of cooperators in both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games across different network structures. The effect is stronger as the reward/punishment magnitude increases and as the probability of encountering the third-party agent increases. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The results are robust across square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, and scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that probabilistic punishment (and/or reward) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, but it does not report on efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game, but a two-player prisoner's dilemma or snowdrift game, so direct prediction of efficiency in PGGs is not possible. The findings may be useful for understanding the general effect of third-party sanctions on cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Vukov, J., Pinheiro, F. L., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). Reward from Punishment Does Not Emerge at All Costs. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured (scale-free network) populations, the introduction of peer punishment does not always promote cooperation. When the cost-to-impact ratio (q/p) is high, punishment can actually reduce the prevalence of cooperation compared to the no-punishment baseline. Only when the cost of punishing is sufficiently low relative to its impact does punishment enhance cooperation. The effect is mapped across a range of game parameters (T, S) and punishment parameters (p, q), showing regions where punishment is beneficial or detrimental for cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, but shows that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, on efficiency in adjacent games) is highly sensitive to the cost-effectiveness of punishment (q/p) and to the network structure. In structured populations, punishment can backfire unless costs are low. For prediction, this suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games may be negative or mixed if punishment is costly relative to its impact, especially in networked or heterogeneous settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task.

- source: Wang, Z., Xia, C. Y., Meloni, S., Zhou, C. S., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Impact of Social Punishment on Cooperative Behavior in Complex Networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a punisher strategy (who pays a cost to reduce defectors' payoffs) in networked 2-person games (PD and SD) increases the fraction of cooperative strategies (cooperators and punishers) at equilibrium, across a range of network topologies (lattice, ER, scale-free) and punishment severities. The effect is robust: even small punishment fines can substantially increase cooperation. The paper analyzes the spatial and network organization of strategies, showing that punishers can form protective clusters that shield cooperators from defectors. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that social punishment increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies in 2-person networked games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the results are only indirectly informative: they suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but the actual payoff consequences (including the cost of punishment) are not analyzed. The game structure is not a public goods game but a 2-person PD/SD, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Niu, X. T., Xu, J. W., & Deng, Z. H. (2017). Conditional punishment resolves social dilemma in spatial network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that introducing and increasing the probability of social punishment (punishers who pay a cost to penalize defectors) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to a higher steady-state density of cooperators. When the willingness to punish is high, defectors are eliminated and cooperation dominates. The mechanism is shown to be robust across a range of temptation-to-defect values. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and spatial patterns).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates may imply higher efficiency, but the cost of punishment and its effect on total group payoff are not analyzed. The model is also not a public goods game, but an adjacent two-player game with spatial structure. Use this paper as indirect support for the behavioral effect of punishment, but not for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Perc, M., Donnay, K., & Helbing, D. (2013). Understanding Recurrent Crime as System-Immanent Collective Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that increasing punishment or inspection does not always reduce crime; instead, the system exhibits complex, sometimes counterintuitive dynamics, including cyclic dominance and abrupt phase transitions between different equilibria (e.g., dominance of criminals, coexistence of criminals and inspectors, or cyclic coexistence of all three strategies). The effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the temptation to commit crime, the cost and incentive for inspection, and the structure of social interactions. The model demonstrates that crime can be recurrent and robust to increased punishment, especially when spatial structure and local interactions are considered. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to predictions about group welfare or efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on crime (and by analogy, on cooperation in social dilemmas) is highly sensitive to the interplay of temptation, punishment cost, and network structure, and that simple linear predictions (e.g., more punishment always increases efficiency) are likely to fail. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for non-monotonic, phase-transition-like effects and the importance of considering spatial and network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests caution in assuming monotonic or simple effects of punishment, especially in spatially structured or networked environments.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Hoffman, M., Bloom, P., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Third-party punishment as a costly signal of trustworthiness. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that TPP is used as a signal of trustworthiness when it is the only available signal, leading to greater trust and reciprocation in a subsequent trust game. When helping is also available as a signal, the use and signaling value of TPP are reduced, while helping remains a strong signal. The results support a reputational account of TPP, showing that people may punish to signal their character rather than solely to deter selfishness. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the signaling value and frequency of punishment depend on the availability and informativeness of alternative prosocial signals (like helping). However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the context and available signals can moderate the behavioral use of punishment, which may indirectly affect efficiency in other designs. For direct prediction of efficiency outcomes, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Ruff, C. C., Ugazio, G., & Fehr, E. (2013). Changing Social Norm Compliance with Noninvasive Brain Stimulation. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that the threat of punishment increases norm compliance (higher transfers) in a modified dictator game, and that noninvasive brain stimulation of the rLPFC can causally modulate this effect. Anodal tDCS (increasing excitability) increases sanction-induced compliance (larger transfer difference between punishment and baseline rounds), while cathodal tDCS (decreasing excitability) reduces it. The effect is stronger in social (human) punishment contexts than in non-social (computer) contexts. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral compliance rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment opportunities increases norm compliance in a dictator-like game, and that this effect is modulated by neural mechanisms. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The behavioral results (increased compliance under punishment) are consistent with the broader literature, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2015). A double-edged sword: Benefits and pitfalls of heterogeneous punishment in evolutionary inspection games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous punishment (punishers who invest different amounts in sanctioning) can reduce the prevalence of second-order free-riders (ordinary people) compared to uniform punishment, but this does not always translate into lower crime rates. In some parameter regions, uniform punishment is as effective or more effective at reducing crime. The system exhibits complex phase transitions and cyclic dominance, which maintain the coexistence of criminals, punishers, and ordinary people. The main obstacle to eliminating crime is the ability of ordinary people to free-ride on the efforts of punishers. The results are presented as fractions of strategies in the population, not as efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the role of punishment heterogeneity and second-order free-riding. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict changes in efficiency when punishment is enabled. The findings suggest that the presence of second-order free-riders and the structure of punishment (uniform vs. heterogeneous) can have complex, non-monotonic effects on cooperation and norm enforcement, which may inform qualitative expectations about efficiency in related games. The results highlight the importance of considering strategy diversity and cyclic dominance in predicting the effects of punishment, but quantitative predictions about efficiency require additional evidence.

- source: Li, H. Y. (2017). Evolutionary Game Analysis of Emergency Management of the Middle Route of South-to-North Water Diversion Project. *Water Resources Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Theoretical and numerical analysis show that: (1) Local governments are more likely to show strong willingness to implement emergency policies if the profit from doing so exceeds the increased cost; (2) To avoid all local governments selecting weak cooperation, the central authority (MWR) should increase penalties for weak willingness, making the penalty greater than the cost saved by weak cooperation. The model demonstrates how the strength of punishment and the cost/benefit structure affect the evolution of cooperation, but does not directly quantify efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment (penalties) for non-cooperation can increase the willingness to cooperate in multi-agent settings similar to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings are most useful for understanding the mechanism by which punishment can shift equilibrium behavior toward greater cooperation, which may be associated with higher efficiency in analogous settings. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that higher punishment effectiveness and costliness of non-cooperation can promote cooperative equilibria, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency change.

- source: Li, K., Szolnoki, A., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). The coevolution of overconfidence and bluffing in the resource competition game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that in the absence of punishment, both overconfidence and bluffing evolve to high levels, with bluffing generally higher than overconfidence. Introducing punishment (with probability p) for revealed deception reduces both overconfidence and bluffing, with overconfidence being more sensitive to punishment. The structure of the interaction network (degree, heterogeneity) modulates these effects: higher degree or more heterogeneous networks promote bluffing and can make punishment less effective. System bias (delta) that increases conflict frequency also enhances the effectiveness of punishment. Players with higher real ability tend to evolve higher overconfidence and bluffing. The paper provides some analysis of average payoff for punished vs. non-punished players, showing that those with lower ability and lower payoff are punished more often, but does not report group-level efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and probability of punishment can reduce the prevalence of overconfidence and bluffing in resource competition games, especially overconfidence. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (deception levels) and not group efficiency or total payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games is limited. The model is adjacent to PGGs: it is a resource competition game with pairwise contests, not a standard PGG. The punishment mechanism is modeled as a reduction in deception traits rather than direct payoff reduction. The paper does provide some subgroup payoff analysis, suggesting that punishment targets and reduces the payoff of lower-ability, higher-deception individuals, but does not quantify overall group efficiency. Thus, the main decision support is qualitative: punishment reduces harmful behavioral biases, and its effectiveness depends on network structure and conflict frequency, but the effect on group efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2014). The cost of cowardice: punitive sentiments towards free riders in Turkana raids. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana participants are significantly more likely to express punitive sentiments (both direct and indirect) toward hypothetical warriors who free ride (display cowardice) in combat, compared to those who fail to contribute due to inability (lack of skill). Direct sanctions include criticism and corporal punishment, while indirect sanctions include withholding social support, lending, and marriage opportunities. The findings support the idea that both direct punishment and indirect reciprocity are important mechanisms for sustaining large-scale cooperation in acephalous societies. However, the study does not measure actual payoffs, group efficiency, or the effect of punishment on group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punitive motivations toward free riders are present in a real-world, high-stakes collective action context, and that both direct and indirect sanctions are socially endorsed. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a controlled game, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The findings are most useful for understanding the psychological and social underpinnings of punishment in collective action, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: McAuliffe, K., & Dunham, Y. (2016). Group bias in cooperative norm enforcement. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that group bias robustly affects sharing and norm enforcement behaviors: people share more with in-group members and are more likely to punish out-group norm violators or protect in-group victims. However, the evidence for increased punishment of in-group norm violators (as would be predicted by a norms-focused account) is weak or inconsistent. The review highlights that most studies do not directly address efficiency or group payoff, and the behavioral outcomes reported (punishment, sharing, norm enforcement) are not directly translatable to efficiency. The paper calls for more standardized methodologies and direct tests of the relationship between group bias, norm enforcement, and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is useful for understanding the social and psychological moderators of punishment behavior (such as group identity), but does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group identity can influence the direction and frequency of punishment, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare. Its main value is in highlighting that group context can moderate punishment behavior, which may indirectly affect efficiency in some designs, but this is not empirically established here.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Li, Y., Fermin, A. S. R., Kanai, R., Takagishi, H., Matsumoto, Y., Kiyonari, T., & Sakagami, M. (2017). Behavioural Differences and Neural Substrates of Altruistic and Spiteful Punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals who engage in costly punishment in the TPPG (altruistic punishers) also behave more pro-socially in a variety of non-strategic games, while those who only reject unfair offers in the UG (spiteful punishers) behave more selfishly. The distinction is supported by neural data: spiteful punishers have larger left caudate nucleus and nucleus accumbens volumes. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in a public goods or social dilemma context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or social dilemma games. It does, however, suggest that the motivations behind punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) are heterogeneous and that not all punishment is associated with pro-social or efficiency-enhancing behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always increase efficiency, depending on the underlying motives of punishers, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Balafoutas, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Rockenbach, B. (2014). Direct and indirect punishment among strangers in the field. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that (1) direct punishment of norm violators is not rewarded by third parties in one-shot field interactions; (2) indirect punishment, operationalized as withholding help from a norm violator, is common and significantly reduces the likelihood that violators receive help; (3) the availability of indirect punishment opportunities crowds out the use of direct punishment; and (4) there are notable sex differences in the application of direct and indirect punishment. The experiment does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, but provides evidence on the behavioral mechanisms of norm enforcement in natural settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its main contribution is to show, in a natural field setting, that indirect punishment (withholding help) is more common and preferred over direct punishment when both are available, and that direct punishment is not socially rewarded. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that in environments where indirect punishment is possible and not costly, direct costly punishment may be less prevalent, but the paper does not inform how this affects group efficiency or payoff. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as the norm violation (littering) is a negative externality, but there is no explicit group payoff or contribution structure. Thus, the paper is more informative about behavioral mechanisms than about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Brethel-Haurwitz, K. M., Stoycos, S. A., Cardinale, E. M., Huebner, B., & Marsh, A. A. (2016). Is costly punishment altruistic? Exploring rejection of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game in real-world altruists. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that extraordinary real-world altruists (altruistic kidney donors) do not engage in more costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the Ultimatum Game than controls. However, individuals with higher self-reported normative altruism (SRA) are more likely to reject unfair offers, indicating that costly punishment is associated with norm sensitivity rather than altruism per se. The study provides evidence that costly punishment in the UG is better understood as norm-enforcing behavior rather than altruistic behavior. No direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare is conducted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the motivations behind costly punishment in the UG, suggesting that such punishment is driven by norm enforcement rather than altruism. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers little direct guidance on how punishment affects efficiency, as it does not report or analyze payoff-based outcomes. However, it does reinforce that the presence of punishment in UG-like games is not necessarily linked to increased altruism or prosociality, and that behavioral outcomes (like rejection rates) may not translate to efficiency gains.

- source: Eriksson, K., Stringing, P., Andersson, P. A., & Lindholm, T. (2017). Costly punishment in the ultimatum game evokes moral concern, in particular when framed as payoff reduction. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across a series of online experiments, the authors find that framing costly punishment as 'reduction' (explicitly paying to reduce the other's payoff) leads to less frequent use of punishment, greater moral concern about using punishment, and a stronger association with punitive motives, compared to the standard 'rejection' framing in the ultimatum game. The reduction frame is more likely to be seen as immoral and as punishment, while rejection is more often seen as a quest for fairness. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are limited to the ultimatum game, which is structurally adjacent but not equivalent to the public goods game. The main outcomes are behavioral (frequency of punishment, motives, moral judgments), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, except as a caution that framing and perceived morality can influence the use of punishment. It does, however, highlight that the way punishment is framed can substantially affect its use, which may be relevant for interpreting behavioral outcomes in other game contexts.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Liebe, U. (2016). Generosity is a sign of trustworthiness-the punishment of selfishness is not. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals who punish selfishness in a dictator game are not more trustworthy nor more trusted in a subsequent trust game, regardless of whether punishment is second-party or third-party. In fact, second-party punishers are trusted less than non-punishers. Generosity in the dictator game, however, is a strong predictor of trustworthiness and is perceived as such by others. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and belief-based measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it uses a binary dictator game with punishment and a trust game, and reports only on behavioral outcomes (punishment, generosity, trust, trustworthiness, beliefs). The findings suggest that punishment, at least in this context, does not enhance trust or trustworthiness, and by implication may not improve efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The design and results may be informative for understanding the signaling value of punishment versus generosity, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs.

- source: White, S. F., Brislin, S. J., Sinclair, S., & Blair, J. R. (2014). Punishing unfairness: Rewarding or the organization of a reactively aggressive response?. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that as participants punish unfair offers more severely, there is increased activation in brain regions associated with reactive aggression (PAG, dmPFC, AIC) and decreased activation in reward-related regions (vmPFC, PCC). The behavioral data show that participants spend more to punish as offers become more unfair. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The findings challenge the view that punishment in social exchange games is motivated by reward, instead supporting a reactive aggression interpretation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social exchange games. It is relevant for understanding the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior, but does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment based on game design dimensions. Its main value is in informing mechanistic or motivational models of punishment, not payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Kurz, T., Thomas, W. E., & Fonseca, M. A. (2014). A fine is a more effective financial deterrent when framed retributively and extracted publicly. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The field experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of financial punishment in deterring undesired behavior (lateness) depends strongly on both the framing (retributive vs. compensatory) and the public/private nature of the sanction. Retributive and public fines significantly increase punctuality compared to compensatory or private fines, which are no more effective than no fine. A follow-up scenario study shows that compensatory fines reduce the perceived immorality of transgression, while retributive fines do not. The results support a theory that the moral framing and public visibility of punishment are key moderators of its behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the framing and public administration of punishment can substantially moderate its behavioral effectiveness, even when the material cost is held constant. While the context is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in collective action settings, punishment will be more effective at promoting cooperation if it is framed as retributive (moral) and administered publicly. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible. The results are most useful for informing the design of punishment mechanisms (e.g., public vs. private, moral vs. compensatory framing) rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Berger, J. (2016). The Sanctioning Dilemma: A Quasi-Experiment on Social Norm Enforcement in the Train. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that peer-sanctioning (negative sanctioning of a norm violator) occurs in about 50% of interventions. Contrary to the classic diffusion of responsibility hypothesis, the likelihood of sanctioning increases with the number of passengers present. Passengers who are closer to the norm breaker and those whose activities require silence are more likely to sanction. The likelihood of sanctioning decreases as the train approaches its destination. No significant difference in sanctioning rates is found between silent-area and non-silent-area train cars. The study does not measure or report any payoff, efficiency, or group welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it is conceptually adjacent—framing norm enforcement as a second-order public good and using volunteer's dilemma theory—the outcomes are strictly behavioral (sanctioning occurrence and timing). There is no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, nor any manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related game design dimensions. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, though it may provide some indirect, conceptual support for the importance of heterogeneity and group size in norm enforcement contexts.

- source: Konishi, N., Oe, T., Shimizu, H., Tanaka, K., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2017). Perceived Shared Condemnation Intensifies Punitive Moral Emotions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that perceived shared condemnation increases moral outrage and, to a lesser extent, moral disgust, while reducing empathy for norm violators. These emotional shifts are associated with greater willingness to punish, both in hypothetical scenarios and in self-reported real-life interventions. The experimental manipulation in Study 2 shows that providing information about others' condemnation causally increases moral outrage and willingness to impose a fine. However, the studies do not use economic games, do not measure actual punishment behavior with real stakes, and do not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. It does, however, suggest that social cues about shared condemnation can increase willingness to punish, which may be relevant for understanding psychological drivers of punishment in group settings. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment is more likely when people believe others will also punish, but it does not address how this affects group efficiency, payoff, or cooperation rates in economic games.

- source: Mathew, S. (2017). How the Second-Order Free Rider Problem Is Solved in a Small-Scale Society. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana adults express strong disapproval toward second-order free riders (those who do not punish norm violators), as well as toward those who punish excessively, unjustly, or without proper group consensus. Punishment is seen as legitimate only when collectively decided and administered by a preordained group. These meta-norms help regulate peer punishment and may solve the second-order free rider problem in this society. However, the evidence is based on responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that strong social norms and meta-norms can support the legitimacy and regulation of peer punishment in a real-world society, potentially solving the second-order free rider problem. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual economic outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of meta-norms and collective legitimacy for the functioning of peer punishment systems.

- source: Eriksson, K., Andersson, P. A., & Strimling, P. (2016). Moderators of the disapproval of peer punishment. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment of norm violators is typically disapproved of by observers, especially when the punishment is severe or appears aggressive. This disapproval extends to viewing the punisher as a problem for the group and the act as inappropriate. Disapproval is moderated by the observer's own trait aggression (less disapproval among more aggressive raters), the severity of punishment (more severe = more disapproval), and whether the punishment is collective or individual (collective = slightly less disapproval). Economic sanctions are disapproved of less than physical ones, and unselfish economic sanctions (where the punisher does not profit) are disapproved of less than selfish ones. Verbal sanctions are also disapproved of, even when effective. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social approval and the perceived legitimacy of peer punishment, but not for predicting how punishment affects group efficiency or earnings. The design is adjacent to PGGs (common resource, norm violation, sanctioning), but the outcomes are purely attitudinal and perceptual. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency, though it may inform hypotheses about the social acceptability of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Wang, L., & Murnighan, J. K. (2017). The Dynamics of Punishment and Trust. *Journal of Applied Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that punishment increases observers' trust in the punisher when the punished individual is seen as deserving, but decreases trust when the punishment is undeserved or too severe. Medium or large punishment for deserving targets increases trust, but large punishment for less deserving targets decreases trust. Punishers who are perceived as acting out of self-interest (e.g., who benefit from the punishment) are trusted less than altruistic punishers. These effects are robust across scenario-based and behavioral lab studies. However, the paper does not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all dependent variables are trust-related or perception-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social dilemma games with or without punishment. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment affects trust in the punisher, especially as a function of deservingness, punishment magnitude, and punisher motives. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency from game design plus control efficiency), this paper offers no direct evidence, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational effects of punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Gordon, D. S., Madden, J. R., & Lea, S. E. G. (2014). Both Loved and Feared: Third Party Punishers Are Viewed as Formidable and Likeable, but These Reputational Benefits May Only Be Open to Dominant Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that third party punishment is perceived as both a dominant and likable act, distinguishing it from other aggressive behaviors. However, only dominant individuals are seen as able to punish effectively and at low cost, with reduced risk of retaliation and greater ability to use non-violent (social) punishment. Subordinate individuals are perceived as less likely to succeed and at higher risk if they attempt punishment. The reputational benefits of punishment (increased likability and dominance) are thus mainly accessible to dominant individuals. These findings are based on observer judgments in hypothetical scenarios, not on actual behavior in economic games or real payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that heterogeneity in the cost and effectiveness of punishment (due to dominance/status) may be important moderators of the impact of punishment on cooperation and norm enforcement. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that the effectiveness and social acceptability of punishment may depend on the perceived status of the punisher, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Zhang, Y., & van der Schaar, M. (2013). Robust Reputation Protocol Design for Online Communities: A Stochastic Stability Analysis. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in a repeated random matching game (asymmetric gift-giving, not a standard PGG), a reputation protocol with probabilistic punishment (parameterized by punishment probability beta) can induce full cooperation and achieve maximal efficiency (social welfare = b-c) in the long run, provided the discount factor and punishment strength are within certain bounds. If punishment is too weak or too strong, efficiency is lower. Theoretical analysis is supported by numerical simulations showing that the optimal protocol achieves much higher average social welfare than direct reciprocity (tit-for-tat) or less robust reputation schemes. The protocol's design is robust to stochastic perturbations and ensures convergence to the efficient equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated random matching environments with binary contribution (gift-giving), the introduction of a well-calibrated reputation-based punishment protocol can move the system from zero efficiency (no cooperation) to full efficiency (all cooperate, social welfare maximized). The effect is robust to noise and initial conditions, provided the punishment probability is neither too low nor too high. While the game is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure and explicit efficiency analysis make the results highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The findings suggest that, when punishment is enabled via a reputation protocol and properly tuned, efficiency can increase dramatically compared to the no-punishment baseline.

- source: Shinya, O., Yusuke, I., & Hiroki, T. (2016). The Condition for Generous Trust. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with uncertainty about partner intention and competence, neither pure punishment nor pure generosity alone can sustain high efficiency. Generous trust (forgiveness) becomes viable and can support efficient outcomes when uncertainty is moderate and is combined with moderate punishment. If uncertainty (incompetence) is too high, efficiency collapses. The agent-based simulation shows that a mix of matching generosity (forgiving in partner selection) and exit generosity (forgiving in relationship maintenance) strategies coevolve and support higher efficiency, but only above a threshold of partner competence. The results highlight the importance of balancing punishment and forgiveness to achieve efficient cooperation under uncertainty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the level of uncertainty (competence) and the balance between punishment and forgiveness. In environments with moderate uncertainty, introducing moderate punishment alongside generosity increases efficiency compared to either strategy alone. However, if uncertainty is too high, even punishment cannot sustain efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment will be highest when the environment allows for some, but not excessive, uncertainty, and when punishment is not too strict or too lenient. The findings are most directly relevant to trust games and adjacent repeated dyadic games, rather than standard public goods games, but the mechanisms may transfer to PGG variants with similar uncertainty and partner selection features.

- source: Quiñones, A. E., van Doorn, G. S., Pen, I., Weissing, F. J., & Taborsky, M. (2016). Negotiation and appeasement can be more effective drivers of sociality than kin selection. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Negotiation and appeasement mechanisms, modeled as responsive strategies where dominants can threaten aggression (punishment) and subordinates can appease by helping, lead to higher group productivity than kin-selected (unconditional) helping. The model shows that when negotiation is possible, even unrelated individuals can achieve high levels of help and group output, with low realized aggression. High relatedness, however, reduces the effectiveness of punishment/enforcement, leading to lower help and lower group productivity. Thus, punishment-like negotiation mechanisms are more effective at driving efficient cooperation than kin selection alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of responsive punishment or enforcement mechanisms (negotiation) increases group efficiency in public-goods-like environments, compared to settings where only kin selection operates. The model is not a literal PGG, but the structure is closely analogous: individuals can help (contribute) or not, dominants can punish (aggression), and group output (productivity) is tracked. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment/negotiation will increase efficiency, especially when relatedness is low. The effect is robust across a range of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment-enabled conditions on efficiency, and highlights that high relatedness may reduce the marginal benefit of punishment.

- source: Xu, J., Song, Y. B., & van der Schaar, M. (2014). Sharing in Networks of Strategic Agents. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that distributed rating-based sanctioning protocols can fully or nearly fully restore efficiency in repeated network sharing games with strategic agents, provided certain incentive compatibility conditions are met. When the network's connectivity is not too high and agents' discount factors are sufficient, the protocol achieves the social optimum (PoA=1). As network connectivity increases or agents become more impatient, efficiency declines but remains higher than with direct reciprocity mechanisms. The protocol is robust to imperfect monitoring and can be tuned for dynamic networks. Theoretical and simulation results show that the protocol outperforms Tit-for-Tat and similar direct punishment schemes, especially in heterogeneous or asymmetric networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that distributed, rating-based punishment mechanisms can substantially increase efficiency in repeated networked cooperation environments, often achieving the social optimum. The effect is most pronounced when the network is not too highly connected and agents are sufficiently patient. The protocol's performance is robust to imperfect monitoring and network dynamics. For prediction tasks, the presence of a distributed, indirect punishment mechanism (ratings affecting future access) is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially compared to direct, bilateral punishment. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with costly sharing, which are adjacent to but not identical to standard public goods games.

- source: O'Connor, C. (2016). The Evolution of Guilt: A Model-Based Approach. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that guilt, modeled as a behavioral trait leading to cooperation, apology, and reparative actions, can be individually adaptive in evolutionary games. In both stag hunt and prisoner's dilemma settings, guilt-proneness increases the likelihood of cooperation, which in turn raises individual and group payoffs. When punishment for defection is present, guilt-proneness further increases individual fitness by reducing the risk of being punished. The models show that costly or honest signals of apology (as a proxy for guilt) can stabilize cooperation and prevent the invasion of defectors or 'fakers.' The efficiency of the group (measured as average fitness or payoff) is higher in populations where guilt-proneness and apology are present, especially when punishment mechanisms exist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms, when combined with behavioral traits like guilt-proneness and apology, can increase efficiency (group payoff) in social dilemma games. While it does not directly model public goods games, the findings are adjacent and suggest that enabling punishment and supporting honest signals of intent (like apology) are likely to increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The models also highlight the importance of the cost and credibility of punishment and apology mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment (especially with some cost or honest signaling) will increase efficiency, provided that the game environment allows for reciprocity and repeated interaction.

- source: Handfield, T., Huang, P. H., & Simpson, R. M. (2016). Climate change, cooperation and moral bioenhancement. *Journal of Medical Ethics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that enhancing retributive (punishment) tendencies in social dilemma games (including public goods and prisoner's dilemma paradigms) is likely to lower group efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The authors review theoretical models and cite experimental evidence indicating that harsh punishment can make groups poorer, and that those who punish often gain less than those who do not. The effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio but relatively insensitive to punishment severity. The paper also notes that anti-social punishment (punishing cooperators) can be highly destructive to cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that enabling or increasing punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is likely to reduce group efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or misapplied. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, adding or intensifying punishment mechanisms may not increase and may even decrease efficiency, despite possible increases in cooperation rates. The effect is particularly negative when punishment is severe or when anti-social punishment is possible. The paper is less informative about the precise quantitative effect sizes but is clear about the direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: Mendoza, S. A., Lane, S. P., & Amodio, D. M. (2014). For Members Only: Ingroup Punishment of Fairness Norm Violations in the Ultimatum Game. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that in the ultimatum game, participants are more likely to reject marginally unfair offers from in-group members than from out-group members, even though rejection is costly to both parties. This pattern is interpreted as stricter enforcement of fairness norms within groups. The effect is most pronounced for offers that are only slightly unfair (e.g., $8/$20), and is stronger among participants with higher in-group identification. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, and the outcome is measured in terms of behavioral responses (accept/reject) rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and social moderators of costly punishment in a bargaining context (ultimatum game), specifically the role of group membership and social identity. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to public goods games. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment is more likely to be used (e.g., in-group contexts, marginal norm violations), but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Verma, P., Nandi, A. K., & Sengupta, S. (2017). Bribery games on inter-dependent regular networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the spread of honest officers (and thus reduction in bribery) is favored over a larger region of parameter space compared to mixed populations. However, the effect of network connectivity is non-monotonic: increasing the degree of the citizen network initially helps honest strategies, but beyond an optimal point, further increases hinder the spread of honesty. Similarly, the degree of the interaction network (citizen-officer links) has an optimal value for maximizing the prevalence of honest officers. Asymmetry in population sizes (more citizens per officer) makes it harder to eradicate bribery. The cost of complaining and the bribe amount are key parameters: lower cost of complaining and lower bribe amounts favor honest strategies. The main dependent variables are the fixation probability of honest officers and the phase space area where honest strategies dominate, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it analyzes how network structure, punishment asymmetry, and behavioral parameters affect the prevalence of honest (non-corrupt) strategies. In public-goods-like environments with asymmetric punishment, structured populations can reduce corruption, but only up to an optimal level of network connectivity. The findings suggest that, for similar games, enabling punishment (asymmetric, targeting only one side) in a structured population can increase the prevalence of socially beneficial behavior, but the effect depends on network connectivity and behavioral costs. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve outcomes (in terms of honest behavior) in structured settings, but warns that too much connectivity can undermine this effect. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used as indirect evidence.

- source: Li, Q., Iqbal, A., Perc, M., Chen, M. Y., & Abbott, D. (2013). Coevolution of Quantum and Classical Strategies on Evolving Random Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when agents can punish by reducing link weights, cooperation can dominate for a wider range of temptation values, especially when the network structure is updated frequently. Stronger punishment (lower minimum link weight) increases the range where cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, quantum strategies eventually dominate. The number of neighbors (network degree) has little effect if all agents have equal degree. The network evolves to have higher clustering and small-world properties. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about strategy prevalence, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (reducing link weights) on the prevalence of cooperation in an evolving network Prisoner's Dilemma. It suggests that stronger punishment and more frequent network updating support cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation under some conditions, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. The game is not a public goods game, but a networked PD with quantum strategies, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Gingins, S., Werminghausen, J., Johnstone, R. A., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2013). Power and temptation cause shifts between exploitation and cooperation in a cleaner wrasse mutualism. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: Cleaner wrasse strategically adjust their foraging behavior in response to the threat of early termination (sanction) and temptation to cheat. When the threat of early termination is high and the temptation to cheat is low, cleaners behave more cooperatively (eat more less-preferred items, which is analogous to providing better service). When the temptation to cheat is high and the threat is low, cleaners exploit the situation (eat more preferred items). The non-cleaner control species does not adjust its behavior in response to these manipulations. The study provides evidence that simple partner control mechanisms (sanctions) can maintain cooperation in asymmetric interactions, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the presence of a sanction mechanism (threat of early termination) can shift behavior from exploitation to cooperation, especially when the temptation to cheat is low. However, the outcomes are behavioral and not directly about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, enabling a sanction mechanism may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and whether the sanction is costly or not. The study is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which sanctions can promote cooperation, but less directly informative for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Mameli, M. (2013). Meat made us moral: a hypothesis on the nature and evolution of moral judgment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of moral judgment is rooted in emotional dispositions that support the enforcement of group-beneficial norms through punishment and meta-punishment. It draws on ethnographic and evolutionary accounts of hunter-gatherer societies, where collective punishment of cheaters and bullies (those who disrupt cooperation and resource sharing) is common and is enforced through both direct and reputational means (e.g., gossip, ostracism, disapproval). The author suggests that these mechanisms enabled stable cooperation in small groups, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than empirical or quantitative. The paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and meta-punishment mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in small, egalitarian groups, especially in contexts analogous to public goods dilemmas (e.g., meat sharing after cooperative hunting). However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with or without punishment. Its relevance is mainly in supporting the plausibility of punishment as a stabilizer of cooperation, especially when punishment is collective, reputational, and embedded in group norms. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in small, highly interactive, reputation-sensitive groups, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance.

- source: Riehl, C., & Frederickson, M. E. (2016). Cheating and punishment in cooperative animal societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in animal societies, cheating (adaptive non-cooperation that increases the cheater's fitness at the expense of others) is rare, and when it does occur, punishment is also rare or not clearly evolved as a response to cheating. Most uncooperative individuals have lower, not higher, fitness than cooperators, and variation in cooperation is often due to condition or context rather than strategic cheating. Where punishment or policing is observed (e.g., worker policing in social insects, aggression toward non-helping subordinates), it is often not clearly linked to increased group efficiency or evolved specifically to suppress cheating. The review concludes that cooperation is usually stable because it is directly or indirectly beneficial, not because punishment enforces it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the context of game design dimensions. It suggests that, in natural animal societies, punishment is not a robust or general mechanism for increasing group efficiency or suppressing cheating, and that cooperation is often stable for other reasons. For prediction tasks, this implies that the presence of punishment mechanisms may not reliably increase efficiency unless cheating is truly adaptive and punishment is both targeted and effective. The review also highlights the importance of context, such as group size, relatedness, and the direct benefits of cooperation, which may moderate the effect of punishment.

- source: Li, L. F., & Xiao, E. (2014). Money Talks: Rebate Mechanisms in Reputation System Design. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when feedback reporting is costly, market efficiency (proportion of efficient trades and participant earnings) declines. Introducing a rebate mechanism, where sellers can offer a partial rebate to buyers for leaving feedback, increases efficiency—especially when sellers frequently offer rebates. The rebate acts as a signal of seller cooperativeness, increasing buyers' willingness to purchase and sellers' willingness to ship. The effect is robust even when the rebate does not fully cover the reporting cost. The more often sellers offer rebates, the higher the efficiency and earnings for both buyers and sellers. If sellers do not offer rebates, efficiency can be even lower than in the no-rebate, high-cost condition. Over time, sellers learn to offer rebates more frequently, further improving efficiency.
