  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a mechanism for sellers to precommit to (partially) covering buyers' feedback costs increases efficiency in a repeated trust game with feedback, especially when sellers frequently use the mechanism. The effect is strongest when the rebate is offered often, and the mechanism works even if the rebate is less than the full reporting cost. For prediction, if a game design allows for a precommitment rebate mechanism and sellers are likely to use it, efficiency should be higher than in a comparable game with high feedback costs and no rebate. The control efficiency (with high feedback cost and no rebate) is a lower bound; the treatment efficiency (with rebate) will be higher, approaching the efficiency of the low-cost or automatic feedback conditions as rebate frequency increases. The findings are most directly relevant to trust/reputation games with feedback as a public good, and less directly to standard PGGs or punishment games, but the mechanism is analogous to reward/precommitment interventions in PGGs.

- source: Riedl, A., Rohde, I. M. T., & Strobel, M. (2016). Efficient Coordination in Weakest-Link Games. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated weakest-link games, groups with fixed interaction structure (all interact with all) rapidly converge to inefficient equilibria, especially as group size increases. In contrast, when players can choose their interaction neighbourhoods (i.e., whom to interact with), groups quickly achieve and sustain near-optimal efficiency and welfare, even in groups of 24. The key mechanism is that high-effort players exclude low-effort players, who then increase their effort to regain inclusion. This exclusion is often costly to both parties, resembling costly punishment, but the effect persists even when exclusion is not materially costly (i.e., in a 'self-protection' treatment). The results are robust to severe reductions in information, alternative payoff functions, and large group sizes. The findings suggest that enabling endogenous partner choice is a powerful mechanism for achieving efficient coordination in weakest-link environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous neighbourhood choice (partner selection) in weakest-link games leads to large, robust increases in group efficiency and welfare, compared to fixed-group baselines. The effect is observed across a range of group sizes (8 and 24), information conditions, and payoff structures. The mechanism is exclusion of low-effort members, which induces higher effort and thus higher group payoffs. Importantly, the effect does not depend on exclusion being materially costly (punishment per se is not required). For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of endogenous partner choice is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of exclusion/punishment-like mechanisms in weakest-link (and possibly related public goods) environments. However, the results are for weakest-link (minimum effort) games, which are adjacent but not identical to standard linear public goods games.

- source: Barker, J. L., & Barclay, P. (2016). Local competition increases people's willingness to harm others. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that when competition is local (i.e., participants compete for prizes within small groups), people are more willing to pay a cost to harm others, resulting in lower group and individual payoffs (efficiency). In contrast, under global competition (competing against all participants), less money is spent on harming, and payoffs are higher. This effect holds whether endowments are earned or given. The findings are robust across three studies, including one where participants could harm both ingroup and outgroup members: under local competition, harming is targeted at ingroup members (the direct competitors), further reducing within-group efficiency. The results are interpreted as a 'tragedy of the commons' driven by local competition, where wasteful harming reduces overall welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in economic games where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs (money-burning), the structure of competition (local vs global) is a critical moderator of efficiency. When competition is local, efficiency is much lower due to increased costly harming, even when the cost-effectiveness of harming is high (burning $1 to reduce $4). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local competition (small group contests) should be expected to strongly reduce efficiency when punishment/harming is possible, compared to global competition. The results are robust to whether endowments are earned or given, and to whether harming is targeted at ingroup or outgroup members. However, the game is not a standard public goods game or punishment game: the main action is costly harming, not punishment for norm violation or contribution decisions. Still, the efficiency implications are directly relevant for settings where costly sanctions are possible.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Santos, F. C., & Lenaerts, T. (2013). Good Agreements Make Good Friends. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that prior commitment strategies, where players agree in advance to cooperate and specify compensation for defection, can achieve higher levels of cooperation and average payoff than traditional costly punishment, provided the arrangement cost is not too high. Sharing the cost of commitment further increases the viability and efficiency of these strategies. The efficiency gains are robust across a range of compensation and arrangement cost parameters, and commitment-based cooperation is less susceptible to antisocial punishment. The findings are derived from analytical risk-dominance conditions and evolutionary simulations, focusing on the stationary distribution of strategies and average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms analogous to prior commitment with compensation (distinct from standard punishment) can yield higher efficiency than costly punishment, especially when the cost of arranging the agreement is low or shared. While the model is based on the one-shot PD rather than a public goods game, the efficiency results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with similar cost/impact structures. The findings imply that, for games with low punishment/arrangement costs and high punishment/compensation effectiveness, enabling commitment or cost-sharing mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to standard punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of cost-to-impact ratios and the potential for free-riding if arrangement costs are high and not shared.

- source: Pérez-Escudero, A., Friedman, J., & Gore, J. (2016). Preferential interactions promote blind cooperation and informed defection. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games where one player can gather information before cooperating or defecting, aversion to information gathering (i.e., a preference for 'blind' cooperation) can be sustained either by punishment (ending the game if the player looks) or by preferential interactions (continuing only with reliable partners). In homogeneous populations, punishment is required to enforce blind cooperation and thus higher payoffs. In heterogeneous populations, preferential interactions can achieve similar or greater efficiency without punishment. The model also predicts that if detecting information gathering is costly, punishment-based mechanisms break down in homogeneous populations, but preferential interactions remain effective in heterogeneous ones. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in homogeneous populations but redundant or unnecessary in heterogeneous ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games depends on population heterogeneity. In homogeneous populations, punishment (ending the game after information gathering or defection) is necessary to enforce cooperation and increase efficiency. In heterogeneous populations, preferential partner selection can achieve high efficiency without punishment. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should consider the degree of population heterogeneity and the possibility of preferential interactions. If the environment is homogeneous and punishment is strong, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If the environment is heterogeneous and allows for preferential interactions, punishment may have little or no additional effect on efficiency.

- source: Agranov, M., Fréchette, G., Palfrey, T., & Vespa, E. (2016). Static and dynamic underinvestment: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that underprovision of the public good is substantial in both static and dynamic settings, with dynamic inefficiency (the additional inefficiency due to intertemporal linkage) increasing as the survival rate of the public good rises. When the public good is highly durable, dynamic inefficiency accounts for a large share of total inefficiency. Subjects use both minimum winning coalition (MWC) strategies and strategic cooperation, including informal punishment (exclusion from coalitions) to reward or punish past behavior. However, the game does not include a formal punishment technology (e.g., costly punishment as in standard PGGs). Welfare losses are decomposed into static and dynamic components, and the results show that dynamic inefficiency is empirically significant and increases with the strength of the dynamic link. The findings are robust across different strategy types and are consistent with theoretical predictions, though the magnitude of inefficiency is somewhat less than predicted by theory.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how dynamic linkages and coalition bargaining affect efficiency in public good provision, especially in legislative or committee settings. However, it does not manipulate or include a formal punishment mechanism as in standard PGGs. Instead, it studies endogenous, informal punishment via coalition exclusion. Therefore, while the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in dynamic, coalition-based public goods environments, they are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of adding formal punishment to a standard PGG. The main lesson is that efficiency is sensitive to dynamic linkages and coalition rules, and that informal punishment can partially substitute for formal mechanisms, but the quantitative effect of formal punishment cannot be inferred from this study.

- source: Harbaugh, R., & To, T. (2014). Opportunistic discrimination. *European Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with two groups, the ability to sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes depends on the punishment strategy adopted by individuals. Solidarity-based punishment (where all individuals punish any opportunism) supports high efficiency and deters cheating. However, if individuals only punish opportunism against their own group (discriminatory trigger), the minority group is more likely to be cheated, leading to lower efficiency for the minority and lower overall surplus. The model also shows that selective legal enforcement (protecting only the majority) can reduce the effectiveness of reputational punishment, making the minority worse off. Only anti-discrimination enforcement (penalizing group-based opportunism) robustly increases efficiency and reduces discrimination. The results highlight that the structure of punishment and enforcement mechanisms critically shapes efficiency and the distribution of payoffs across groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency depends on whether punishment is coordinated across groups (solidarity) or limited to within-group (discriminatory). In environments where punishment is only triggered by harm to one's own group, efficiency gains from punishment are limited, especially for minorities. The model also warns that adding legal enforcement in a way that only protects the majority can reduce overall efficiency for minorities by undermining reputational incentives. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs enabling broad, group-independent punishment (solidarity) will yield higher efficiency gains from punishment, while designs that fragment punishment or allow selective enforcement may see limited or even negative effects on efficiency, especially for minority subgroups.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Balafoutas, L., Batsaikhan, M., Campos-Ortiz, F., Putterman, L., & Sutter, M. (2016). Securing property rights: A dilemma experiment in Austria, Mexico, Mongolia, South Korea and the United States. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper studies a property rights dilemma game in which subjects allocate effort among production, theft, and protection. Three institutional treatments are compared: (1) no collective protection (NCP, baseline), (2) voluntary collective protection (VCP), and (3) collective protection by majority vote (VOTE, mandatory if chosen). Efficiency, measured as group earnings relative to the social optimum, is lowest in NCP (24.6%), higher in VCP (36.6%), and highest in VOTE (48.0%). The VOTE treatment achieves the highest efficiency when the mandatory scheme is adopted, but this occurs in only 64% of opportunities. The efficiency gains from collective protection are robust across five countries, but the magnitude varies with national trust levels. The results show that institutional mechanisms for collective protection (analogous to public enforcement or taxation) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemmas involving appropriation, but their effectiveness depends on both institutional design and cultural context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of introducing a collective protection or sanctioning institution in a social dilemma with appropriation (theft), this paper provides direct evidence that such institutions increase efficiency, with mandatory (voted) collective protection yielding the largest gains. The efficiency increase from baseline to voluntary collective protection is modest but significant, while the increase to mandatory collective protection is larger but not maximal due to incomplete adoption and suboptimal implementation. The design dimensions most relevant are the presence and type of collective protection, the method of funding (voluntary vs. mandatory), and the cultural context (trust). While the game is not a standard PGG, the efficiency results are closely analogous and informative for settings where punishment or public enforcement is introduced. The findings suggest that enabling effective, enforceable collective protection (akin to punishment) is likely to increase efficiency, but the realized gains may be less than theoretical predictions due to behavioral and cultural factors.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Barclay, P. (2016). Exploring the trade-off between quality and fairness in human partner choice. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: When given a choice between a rich-stingy and a poor-fair partner in a modified Dictator Game, choosers often select the fair partner, even at a cost to their own expected pay-off. This tendency is stronger when the wealth of partners is unstable. When both partners are equally fair or unfair, choosers prefer the richer partner, aligning with maximizing expected pay-off. The findings indicate that fairness is prioritized over ability (wealth) in partner choice, especially under uncertainty, and that individuals are willing to incur personal costs to avoid rewarding unfairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments where individuals can choose partners, people may sacrifice efficiency (personal or group payoff) to avoid unfair partners, especially when partner quality is unstable. While not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting that the introduction of partner choice or similar sanctioning mechanisms may not always increase efficiency—if individuals use these mechanisms to punish unfairness even at a cost to themselves. The results suggest that the effect of punishment or exclusion on efficiency may be less positive or even negative when individuals are motivated by fairness rather than payoff maximization.

- source: Mani, A., Rahwan, I., & Pentland, A. (2013). Inducing Peer Pressure to Promote Cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing peer pressure mechanisms—where peers are incentivized to exert costly pressure on each other to reduce negative externalities—can lead to higher social surplus (efficiency) than traditional Pigouvian mechanisms, especially in large, sparse networks. Theoretical results show that peer pressure reduces equilibrium action levels (i.e., reduces over-consumption or under-contribution), but not all the way to the social optimum unless subsidized. Social mechanisms that reward peers for their neighbors' good behavior are more cost-effective than direct rewards to individuals. The model predicts that the efficiency gain from peer pressure increases as the cost of exerting pressure decreases and as social ties strengthen. An illustrative experiment on physical activity supports these predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling peer-based sanctions or rewards (peer pressure) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of peer pressure is moderate and the network is sparse. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing peer-based punishment or reward mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to control, and that the effect is stronger when the cost of peer pressure is lower and when social ties are stronger. The model is not an exact PGG, but is closely related and the efficiency results are directly relevant. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates for standard PGG parameters, but the qualitative direction is clear: peer pressure mechanisms are more efficient than no intervention, and often more efficient than centralized punishment/reward, especially at low budgets.

- source: Al-Dhanhani, A., Mizouni, R., Otrok, H., & Al-Rubaie, A. (2014). A game theoretical model for collaborative groups in social applications. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of reciprocity and punishment strategies in repeated social dilemmas (modeled as asynchronous iterated prisoner's dilemma) has a strong effect on group efficiency and the distribution of gains between cooperative and selfish (free riding) users. Classical Tit for Tat leads to deadlock and declining payoffs. Generous Tit for Tat avoids deadlock and increases group gain, but allows free riders to benefit disproportionately. Reputation-based Tit for Tat reduces free rider gain but can also reduce cooperative user gain, especially if the reputation threshold is set high or the initialization phase is short. Group reputation-based Tit for Tat (indirect reciprocity) achieves the highest cooperative user gain and lowest free rider gain, thus maximizing group efficiency and survivability. The presence of free riders always reduces group efficiency, but the right punishment/reciprocity mechanism can mitigate this loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment/reciprocity mechanisms (especially those based on group reputation and indirect reciprocity) can significantly improve group efficiency in repeated social dilemma environments, even in the presence of free riders. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related and the efficiency outcomes are directly comparable. The findings suggest that enabling punishment or exclusion based on group-level reputation (rather than only direct reciprocity) is likely to increase efficiency relative to no punishment or to simple direct reciprocity. The results are most relevant for settings with repeated interactions, observable histories, and the ability to track and act on group-level reputation. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to all design dimensions, but the qualitative direction is clear: more sophisticated, group-based punishment/reciprocity mechanisms are more effective at sustaining efficiency.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). The shift between the Red Queen and the Red King effects in mutualisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in multiplayer snowdrift games modeling mutualisms, the introduction and increase of a collective reward mechanism can shift the allocation of mutualistic benefits from favoring the faster-evolving species (Red Queen effect) to favoring the slower-evolving species (Red King effect). Asymmetries in the number of interacting partners or in payoffs can also shift the balance of surplus allocation. The model predicts that, depending on the reward intensity and asymmetries, the efficiency (total surplus generated and its division) can be increased or made more unequal. The model is theoretical and does not provide empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the presence and intensity of a reward mechanism (analogous to a positive incentive or bonus for cooperation) can substantially alter the efficiency and allocation of surplus in multiplayer social dilemmas. While it does not directly model punishment or public goods games, its results suggest that reward mechanisms can increase group efficiency and shift the distribution of payoffs, especially in the presence of asymmetries. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient intensity) can increase efficiency and alter the distribution of payoffs, but the effect depends on the details of the reward and the structure of the game (e.g., group size, asymmetries). The findings are most relevant for settings with reward rather than punishment, and for snowdrift-like rather than public-goods-like games.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2017). The evolution of conditional moral assessment in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the 'Staying' norm, which prescribes abstaining from moral assessment when a donor interacts with a recipient of bad reputation, is more effective at establishing and maintaining cooperation than the four most studied social norms in indirect reciprocity. Under Staying, discriminators (conditional cooperators) are more likely to invade populations of defectors, and the evolutionary dynamics favor higher average payoffs and more efficient group outcomes. The model demonstrates that the range of initial conditions leading to efficient, cooperative equilibria is wider under Staying, and that the strict application of moral judgment (as in other norms) is less effective for promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games (a close relative of public goods games), the design of the assessment/punishment norm can have a large effect on group efficiency. Specifically, conditional assessment (Staying) leads to higher efficiency than unconditional assessment (Scoring, etc.), especially when assessment errors are low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the details of the punishment/assessment mechanism (e.g., whether punishment is conditional or unconditional, and how errors are handled) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. The findings support the idea that more nuanced, context-sensitive punishment/assessment rules can yield higher efficiency than strict, always-on punishment rules.

- source: Bird, R. B., & Power, E. A. (2015). Prosocial signaling and cooperation among Martu hunters. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In populations of SLA agents without memory, those with low penalty sensitivity dominate, leading to high defection rates and low average payoffs. (2) SLA strategies are not stable against invasion by TFT or ALLD; TFT can dominate and lead to higher population payoffs. (3) Adding memory to SLA agents enables them to learn to cooperate with TFT, increasing cooperation and average payoffs, but this effect is limited to interactions with TFT; among themselves, SLA agents still defect. (4) The penalty parameter (analogous to punishment sensitivity) is a key moderator: higher penalty sensitivity leads to more cooperation and higher payoffs, but is outcompeted by less sensitive types in evolutionary dynamics. (5) The model predicts that, absent memory or external cooperative strategies, populations of simple learning agents will evolve toward defection and low efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments without explicit punishment mechanisms, agents with low sensitivity to penalties (i.e., low punishment cost or low punishment effectiveness) evolve toward defection and low efficiency. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, the presence and effectiveness of punishment (or penalty sensitivity) are critical for sustaining cooperation and high efficiency. However, the model does not include explicit peer punishment as a game design feature, so direct quantitative predictions for the effect of enabling punishment in public goods games are limited. The results do support the qualitative prediction that increasing the effectiveness or salience of punishment (or penalty) responses can increase efficiency, but such strategies may be evolutionarily unstable without additional mechanisms (e.g., memory, reputation, or external enforcement).

- source: dos Santos, M., Placì, S., & Wedekind, C. (2015). Stochasticity in economic losses increases the value of reputation in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that stochasticity in economic losses does not increase mean helping rates or mean group earnings, but it does increase the payoff advantage of having a good reputation. Under stochastic losses, generous players are helped more and selfish players are helped less, leading to a steeper positive relationship between generosity and final earnings. In stable environments, this relationship is absent. Thus, stochasticity increases the efficiency of reputation-based cooperation by making investment in reputation more beneficial, even though overall group efficiency does not increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in repeated helping games with indirect reciprocity, environmental stochasticity (randomness in losses) increases the payoff return to cooperative behavior (reputation), even if mean group efficiency does not change. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion or helping) on efficiency may be more positive in environments with stochastic payoffs or risks, because the social system more effectively rewards cooperators and penalizes defectors. However, the game is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment; rather, it is an indirect reciprocity helping game. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or exclusion is the main sanctioning mechanism, and where environmental risk is present.

- source: Rand, D. G., Tarnita, C. E., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Evolution of fairness in the one-shot anonymous Ultimatum Game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Theoretical and simulation results show that, in finite populations with stochastic evolutionary dynamics, fairness (both higher offers and nonzero rejection thresholds) can be favored by natural selection in the Ultimatum Game, even without reputation or repeated interaction. The favored strategies depend on mutation rate and selection strength. The behavioral experiment supports the prediction that greater uncertainty about others' success and less behavioral consistency are associated with higher offers and/or demands. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the Ultimatum Game structure is only adjacent to public goods games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the evolution of costly punishment and fairness in strategic interactions, but its findings are not directly transferable to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The Ultimatum Game is a two-player bargaining game with a binary accept/reject punishment mechanism, not a multi-player public goods game with explicit punishment options. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer and demand), not group efficiency. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that costly punishment (rejection) can be favored and lead to fairer outcomes, it does not provide quantitative evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper for mechanism insight, not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2015). Apology and forgiveness evolve to resolve failures in cooperative agreements. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated social dilemmas modeled as noisy iterated Prisoner's Dilemma, the ability to make commitments and to retaliate (revenge) after commitment breakdown increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies. When apology and forgiveness are added, cooperation is further boosted, but only if apology is sufficiently costly (sincere). If apology is too cheap, fake committers exploit the system, reducing cooperation. The model shows that the optimal apology cost is above the cost of cooperation but below the penalty for breaking commitment. The results are robust to noise and repeated interaction length. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent, and the mechanisms studied (commitment, retaliation, apology, forgiveness) are relevant to understanding how punishment-like mechanisms affect cooperation and group payoff in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, noisy social dilemmas, mechanisms analogous to punishment (revenge/retaliation) and costly apology/forgiveness can increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoff/efficiency, especially when the cost and effectiveness of these mechanisms are well-calibrated. The findings suggest that enabling punishment-like responses after norm violations (commitment breakdown) and allowing for costly apology/forgiveness can improve efficiency, but only if the costs are set appropriately. The results are most transferable to repeated games with commitment and noise, and less so to one-shot or standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency, but supports the idea that punishment and compensation mechanisms can be beneficial for group outcomes under certain conditions.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). When agreement-accepting free-riders are a necessary evil for the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot games, the coexistence of proposers and acceptors can be necessary to keep defectors out of the population, especially when the cost of commitment must be paid before knowing if the other will accept (prior commitments). In repeated games, proposers alone can sustain cooperation, and the presence of acceptors can actually reduce cooperation by making it easier for defectors to invade. The introduction of an apology-forgiveness mechanism (where apologies after defection can restore cooperation) can be exploited by acceptors if the apology cost is too low, leading to lower cooperation. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and the stationary distribution of strategies, with average payoff as the fitness criterion, but do not directly report group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does offer adjacent theoretical insights about how commitment mechanisms (which can be seen as a form of pre-emptive contract or compensation for defection) affect the prevalence of cooperation and the exclusion of defectors. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (timing of costs, ability to recognize intentions, presence of apology-forgiveness) can strongly moderate the success of cooperation-promoting strategies and thus the average payoff in the population. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma and not a public goods game, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning as in PGGs, its direct predictive value for the downstream task is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding how alternative mechanisms to punishment (commitments, compensation, apology) might affect cooperation and payoff in related social dilemmas.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Reputation Effects in Public and Private Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperative strategies in indirect reciprocity depends critically on the observability of private actions (q), the frequency of public interactions (p), and the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c). Honest cooperation (leading to full group efficiency) is only stable when private actions are sufficiently observable and b/c is high. When private actions are rarely observed or b/c is low, only partial cooperation or defection is stable, resulting in lower group efficiency. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for these transitions. The presence of public observation increases the incentive for cooperation, but if private actions are unobserved, individuals may defect in private, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how observability (analogous to information feedback or monitoring in public goods games) and the benefit-to-cost ratio moderate the effect of reputation-based incentives on group efficiency. While not a direct PGG or punishment study, its results suggest that increasing the probability that actions are observed (or reputationally consequential) increases the likelihood of stable, high-efficiency cooperation. In prediction tasks, similar mechanisms (e.g., transparency, monitoring, or reputation updating) should be expected to increase the efficiency gains from reputation or sanctioning systems, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high. However, if observability is low, the efficiency impact of such systems will be limited.

- source: Phillips, T., Li, J. W., & Kendall, G. (2014). The Effects of Extra-Somatic Weapons on the Evolution of Human Cooperation towards Non-Kin. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Simulating a population of 40 players using 25 well-known IPD strategies, the authors compare two conditions: a standard evolutionary IPD and a 'weapons use' IPD where repeated defections trigger a risk of elimination (interpreted as a proxy for lethal punishment or high-cost disputes). Cooperative strategies, on average, survive significantly longer under weapons use, while non-cooperative strategies survive for fewer generations. The 'Always Cooperate' strategy, typically the least successful, becomes the most successful cooperative strategy as the risk of elimination increases. The model suggests that high costs for repeated defection (analogous to punishment) can shift evolutionary success toward cooperation, even in the absence of explicit monitoring or repeated interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that increasing the cost of within-group conflict (modeled as a risk of elimination after repeated defections) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperative strategies and decrease that of non-cooperative strategies. While the model is not a public goods game and does not report group efficiency per se, the findings are relevant for predicting that introducing or increasing the severity of punishment in repeated social dilemmas is likely to increase the relative payoff or evolutionary success of cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is severe and unavoidable, and less so to standard PGGs with explicit, voluntary punishment stages. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, chat), but supports the general principle that costly sanctions can promote cooperation and group-level success.

- source: Diekmann, A., Jann, B., Przepiorka, W., & Wehrli, S. (2014). Reputation Formation and the Evolution of Cooperation in Anonymous Online Markets. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Using large-scale eBay auction data, the study shows that sellers with more positive feedback (reputation) are more likely to sell their items and receive higher prices, while negative feedback reduces both outcomes. The effect of negative feedback is stronger than that of positive feedback. Participation in the feedback system is high, and feedback behavior is consistent with strong reciprocity and altruism, which helps maintain the reputation system. The reputation system thus mitigates trust problems and supports cooperation in one-shot, anonymous exchanges, increasing overall market efficiency and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation-based feedback systems in anonymous, one-shot exchange environments (adjacent to PGGs) increase efficiency by incentivizing cooperative behavior. While not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing or strengthening reputation/feedback mechanisms in similar environments: efficiency (measured as successful trade and higher prices) increases when reputation mechanisms are active and well-participated. The effect is stronger for negative feedback (punishment) than for positive feedback (reward). The study also highlights the importance of high participation in the feedback system, which is sustained by reciprocity and altruism. For prediction, the presence of a reputation/feedback mechanism (even if not formal monetary punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with no such mechanism.

- source: Nakashima, N. A., Halali, E., & Halevy, N. (2017). Third parties promote cooperative norms in repeated interactions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party intervention in repeated adversarial interactions (modeled as a Peacemaker Game) increases cooperation rates among adversaries. Early intervention is more effective at establishing persistent cooperative norms than late intervention. Third parties are more likely to intervene when adversaries have a history of cooperation, and adversaries are more likely to cooperate following third party intervention. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and intervention rates), not efficiency or group payoff. The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Gillenkirch, R. M., & Kreienbaum, H. (2017). What guides subjective performance evaluation: Incentive alignment or norm enforcement?. *Review of Managerial Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that superiors are primarily motivated by norm enforcement: they pay more for information that allows them to reward high and punish low effort, and their allocation decisions reflect a willingness to punish norm violations. Subordinates increase their effort when they know their effort can be observed and evaluated. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total surplus, but higher effort levels in conditions with more informative evaluation suggest that group output and subordinate payoffs increase when norm enforcement is possible. However, the focus is on behavioral mechanisms (effort, information acquisition, allocation) rather than on efficiency as a formal outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling punishment (via subjective evaluation and information acquisition) increases effort and likely increases group output in a team production setting with a free rider problem. However, it does not report efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The design is adjacent to PGGs: two subordinates choose effort (costly), output is the sum of efforts plus noise, and a superior (not a group member) allocates the output. The 'punishment' is implemented via allocation decisions, not explicit costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that mechanisms enabling norm enforcement (punishment) can increase effort and likely improve group outcomes, but the lack of direct efficiency data and the non-standard game structure limit its direct applicability to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Halevy, N., & Halali, E. (2015). Selfish third parties act as peacemakers by transforming conflicts and promoting cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) The possibility and higher probability of third-party intervention increase cooperation rates among disputants in both interpersonal and intergroup contexts. (2) Third parties' willingness to intervene is highly sensitive to the risk and potential rewards of intervention; reducing risk or increasing potential gain increases intervention rates. (3) Despite the potential for increased collective gains, third parties often intervene less frequently than would be optimal, leading to missed opportunities for higher group payoffs. (4) The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as primary outcomes, but discusses them in the context of missed opportunities and the structure of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the introduction of a punishment/reward mechanism (via third-party intervention) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma-like settings, even when the underlying game is not a standard public goods game. However, because the primary outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/intervention rates) and not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms (including the risk and incentive structure for enforcers) is critical for their effectiveness. The evidence is most relevant for adjacent settings where third-party intervention is possible, but less directly transferable to standard PGGs with endogenous peer punishment.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Mui, V. L. (2014). Coordinating Resistance Through Communication and Repeated Interaction. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that repetition alone only modestly reduces leader DAC (transgression) rates, as leaders can punish beneficiaries who resist, deterring future resistance. Communication between responders, even when non-binding and private, substantially increases the rate of coordinated resistance and reduces leader DAC. The combination of repetition and communication is most effective at reducing DAC. The study also shows that hiding responder identities from the leader (so the leader cannot target challenging beneficiaries) increases resistance. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total surplus, or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The results are most relevant for understanding how communication and repeated interaction affect coordination in social dilemmas with endogenous roles and targeted punishment, rather than standard public goods games with peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the importance of communication and repeated interaction for coordinated resistance in social dilemmas with endogenous roles. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that communication between potential punishers (or resisters) can be as important as, or more important than, repetition for deterring exploitative behavior. The results highlight that the ability to target punishers (e.g., identifying who punished) can undermine the effectiveness of resistance. For prediction tasks in public goods games, the main lesson is that communication channels and anonymity of punishment/reward can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes, but it does suggest that communication is a powerful moderator.

- source: Kuwabara, K. (2015). Do Reputation Systems Undermine Trust? Divergent Effects of Enforcement Type on Generalized Trust and Trustworthiness. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Reputation systems, as a form of diffuse, non-material enforcement, reinforce generalized trust and trustworthiness more than contractual (material punishment) or relational (repeated partner) enforcement. After exposure to reputational enforcement, participants are more trusting and trustworthy in subsequent unenforced exchanges than after exposure to contracts or repeated partners. Contractual enforcement, in contrast, can undermine intrinsic motivation to trust or be trustworthy once enforcement is removed. These effects are robust across multiple studies and are supported by both behavioral and self-report measures. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the experimental paradigm is the investment game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like enforcement (contracts) and reputation systems on subsequent cooperation, but not on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that material punishment (contracts) can undermine intrinsic motivation for cooperation, while reputation systems can reinforce it. However, because the outcomes are trust/trustworthiness (not efficiency) and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The evidence is more relevant for understanding how enforcement mechanisms affect willingness to cooperate after enforcement is removed, rather than direct effects on group efficiency during enforcement.

- source: Cuesta, J. A., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Ferrer, A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2015). Reputation drives cooperative behaviour and network formation in human groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that providing information about others' past actions (reputation/memory) leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates in a dynamic network Prisoner's Dilemma. When no memory is available, cooperation decays and the network becomes highly connected (as players have no reason to avoid defectors). With memory, players break links with defectors, leading to lower connectivity but higher cooperation. The effect is stronger as the memory window increases. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The 'punishment' mechanism is the breaking of links, not a costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reputation-based partner selection (link breaking) on cooperation in dynamic network games. While the setting is an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (not a PGG), and the 'punishment' is not costly, the results suggest that allowing players to sanction defectors by breaking ties (when reputation is available) can sustain higher cooperation. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not measured, and the game is not a PGG, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that information about others' behavior (reputation) and the ability to avoid defectors can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Karagonlar, G., & Kuhlman, D. M. (2013). The role of Social Value Orientation in response to an unfair offer in the Ultimatum Game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the paper finds that Prosocials (as measured by SVO) are less likely to reject an unfair offer (20% of the pie) in the Ultimatum Game than Proselfs. This difference is not due to differences in fairness perceptions or expectations, but is mediated by more positive emotions and more positive attributions toward the proposer among Prosocials. The findings support the view that emotion regulation and cognitive reappraisal, rather than altruistic punishment, drive acceptance of unfair offers among Prosocials. The study does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The Ultimatum Game is structurally different from public goods games, and the outcome of interest is the accept/reject decision rather than group payoff or efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding individual differences in responses to norm violations and costly punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does, however, suggest that motivations for punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) may differ by individual orientation, which could be contextually relevant for interpreting punishment effects in other games.

- source: Xiang, T., Lohrenz, T., & Montague, P. R. (2013). Computational Substrates of Norms and Their Violations during Social Exchange. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that social norm expectations, manipulated via preconditioning to low, medium, or high offers in a repeated ultimatum game, strongly affect rejection rates for identical offers. Subjects preconditioned to low offers (unfair) are less likely to reject medium offers than those preconditioned to high offers (hyper-fair), even though the offers are objectively the same. Subjective feelings about offers and neural responses (in regions such as vmPFC, striatum, and anterior insula) track these norm prediction errors. The results support a model in which norm violations, rather than simple payoff inequality, drive punishment behavior (rejection), and that these processes are reflected in both behavior and brain activity. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, and the outcome of interest is not efficiency but rather the psychological and neural mechanisms of norm-based punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, specifically regarding how expectations and norm adaptation modulate punishment (rejection) behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment (here, costly rejection) on behavior is highly sensitive to context and prior expectations, which may inform models of punishment in other social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as contextual or mechanistic evidence about the psychological drivers of punishment, not as a quantitative source for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Camera, G., Casari, M., & Bigoni, M. (2013). EXPERIMENTAL MARKETS WITH FRICTIONS. *Journal of Economic Surveys*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The new experiment shows that allowing free-form chat among randomly matched strangers in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma leads to much higher cooperation rates than either no communication or structured, anonymous public messages. Structured communication does not significantly improve cooperation or coordination relative to no communication, likely due to the prevalence of deceptive use of messages. The study finds that public messages are informative about intentions, but deception dilutes their effectiveness. The experiment does not include a punishment treatment, but the literature review summarizes prior findings that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, especially in small groups. The main outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation rates, communication patterns), not payoff-based (efficiency or group earnings).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. The experimental design is an indefinitely repeated, random-matching prisoner's dilemma among strangers, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main manipulated dimension is communication: free-form chat vs. structured messages vs. no communication. The findings suggest that free-form communication can strongly increase cooperation, but structured, anonymous communication does not. The experiment does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The literature review section summarizes prior work showing that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but these are not tested in the new experiment. Thus, this paper is most informative about the effect of communication (chat) and monitoring (public/anonymous) on cooperation, and only indirectly about punishment or efficiency. It should be used as contextual or indirect evidence for prediction, especially regarding the role of communication and monitoring, but not as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bednarik, P., Fehl, K., & Semmann, D. (2014). Costs for switching partners reduce network dynamics but not cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing costs for forming new links in dynamic networks reduces the rate at which participants break links (i.e., switch partners). However, cooperation rates in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma remain high across all dynamic treatments, regardless of the cost level, and are significantly higher than in static networks where no partner switching is allowed. The ability to break links—even if rarely used—appears to be enough to maintain high cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of these dynamics on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with dynamic partner networks, the introduction of costs for switching partners reduces network dynamism but does not reduce cooperation rates. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment or partner-switching mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that the mere threat of partner switching can sustain cooperation, but the efficiency implications (e.g., total group payoff relative to maximum possible) remain unaddressed.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Zhen, S. S., & Yu, R. J. (2016). Tend to Compare and Tend to Be Fair: The Relationship between Social Comparison Sensitivity and Justice Sensitivity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Han, X., Cao, S. N., Shen, Z. S., Zhang, B. Y., Wang, W. X., Cressman, R., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). Emergence of communities and diversity in social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in static network structures (both regular and random bipartite networks), proposers form stable local communities with diverse offers, while in randomly rewired networks, proposers become homogeneous. The diversity in offers (SD of p) is much higher in structured networks. Responders' behavior is heterogeneous but not affected by network structure. The mechanism is that rational proposers best-respond to their local responder 'leaders,' whose heterogeneous minimum acceptance levels drive local agreement. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not include a public goods game or explicit costly punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experiment uses the ultimatum game, where 'punishment' is the rejection of offers, not a costly sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer diversity, community formation), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. It may provide some indirect context about how local interaction structure and heterogeneity can affect behavioral diversity, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Brevers, D., Noël, X., Ermer, E., Dabiri, D., Verbanck, P., & Kornreich, C. (2013). Unfairness sensitivity and social decision-making in individuals with alcohol dependence: A preliminary study. *Drug and Alcohol Dependence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Alcohol-dependent individuals are more likely to reject unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game compared to healthy controls, suggesting greater emotional reactivity to unfairness and reduced ability to make self-advantageous decisions in social contexts involving frustration. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and the design does not include a punishment phase or a repeated public goods structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to the target environment, as rejection of unfair offers can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment, but the context is one-shot, dyadic, and does not involve group cooperation or repeated interactions. The findings may inform understanding of individual differences in punishment sensitivity, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or the effect of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.

- source: Tanaka, H., Ohtsuki, H., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2016). The price of being seen to be just: an intention signalling strategy for indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that participants in a dyadic donation game use a costly signalling option (abandoning a resource) to manage their reputation, especially after justified defection or implementation error. Others interpret this signal as a sign of cooperative intent and are more likely to cooperate with signallers. The signalling option allows players to maintain a good reputation without requiring second-order information. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that such signalling can stabilize cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The experimental design is a dyadic donation game, not a multi-player PGG, and the 'punishment' is a costly self-imposed signal (abandoning a resource) rather than a punishment directed at others. The main outcomes are behavioral (signalling and cooperation rates), not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings suggest that providing players with a costly signalling option can help maintain cooperation in the absence of second-order information, which may be relevant for understanding mechanisms that stabilize cooperation in related environments. The results do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Ma, L. K., Tunney, R. J., & Ferguson, E. (2014). Gratefully Received, Gratefully Repaid: The Role of Perceived Fairness in Cooperative Interactions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that recipients of unconditional (free) help perceive the donor as more genuinely helpful, feel more gratitude, and report a higher tendency to reciprocate in the future compared to recipients of conditional (repayment-required) help. Among those who received conditional offers and accepted, 38% defaulted (either partially or fully) on repayment, suggesting a behavioral response to perceived unfairness. The effect of offer conditionality on willingness to reciprocate is serially mediated by perceived helpfulness and gratitude. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game is a one-shot, dyadic trust game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how conditionality (akin to punishment or sanctions) affects willingness to reciprocate and actual repayment behavior in a one-shot, dyadic trust game. It does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that perceived fairness and the framing of help (conditional vs. unconditional) can influence cooperation and defaulting, which may be relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms in cooperation, but the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with punishment enabled or disabled.

- source: Schram, A., & Charness, G. (2015). Inducing Social Norms in Laboratory Allocation Choices. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that providing impartial advice about what one 'ought to do' (shared understanding) only affects behavior when choices are made public, i.e., when social (not just moral) norms are salient. In the advice + public condition, dictators are significantly less selfish than in other conditions. Advice alone (private) does not change behavior, nor does public observability alone without advice. The effect is interpreted as the creation of a social norm, enforced by the threat of social disapproval (shame), not by material punishment. There is no direct measurement or analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. The findings are relevant for understanding norm induction and the role of observability, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments. It does not implement punishment, rewards, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how social norms (via advice and observability) can shift behavior toward less selfish choices in allocation games. For prediction tasks, the main takeaway is that nonmaterial social sanctions (public observability) combined with shared understanding can induce prosocial behavior, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a PGG or punishment mechanism limit its direct applicability.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen.
