This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

- source: Rand, D. G., Brescoll, V. L., Everett, J. A. C., Capraro, V., & Barcelo, H. (2016). Social Heuristics and Social Roles: Intuition Favors Altruism for Women but Not for Men. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuition (via time pressure, conceptual priming, or cognitive load) increases altruistic giving in the Dictator Game for women, but not for men. This effect is further moderated by gender role identification: women who identify with masculine attributes become less altruistic when deliberating, while those who identify with feminine attributes are unaffected by deliberation. Men are generally less altruistic, and their giving is not significantly affected by cognitive processing mode or gender role identification. The study does not involve strategic interaction, punishment, or group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is the Dictator Game, which is a unilateral giving game with no strategic interaction, no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and no group payoff or efficiency concept. While the paper discusses social norms and the internalization of altruism (and mentions that women are punished more for failing to be altruistic), it does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report any efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as very indirect context about gender, social heuristics, and norm compliance.

- source: Shutters, S. T., & Hales, D. (2013). Tag-Mediated Altruism is Contingent on How Cheaters Are Defined. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the sustainability of tag-mediated altruism is highly sensitive to how cheaters are defined. When cheaters are defined as agents who can never donate (strong cheaters), altruism collapses completely and rapidly. With medium cheaters (who can revert to altruism via mutation), donation rates are very low. Only with ultraweak or weak cheaters do substantial donation rates persist. The paper also finds that parameters such as neighborhood size, number of pairings, and mutation rate affect donation rates, but the presence of strong cheaters overwhelms these effects. The authors suggest that additional mechanisms, such as punishment or multi-level selection, may be necessary to sustain cooperation in the presence of strong cheaters, but these are not modeled directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. It does not implement punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative in showing that, in tag-based donation models, the presence of strong cheaters (analogous to unpunished defectors) eliminates cooperation, and that additional mechanisms (such as punishment) may be required to sustain altruism. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that in environments where strong, unpunished defection is possible, efficiency will be low unless additional mechanisms (like punishment) are present. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct manipulation of punishment, so its value for prediction is mainly conceptual and indirect.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Li, Z. J., & Yang, Y. Q. (2015). Understanding Cooperative Behavior Based on the Coevolution of Game Strategy and Link Weight. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to coevolve with strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can significantly enhance the fraction of cooperators, especially at intermediate values of link weight amplitude. The mechanism works by reinforcing links to successful neighbors and weakening links to less successful ones, which leads to the formation and expansion of cooperator clusters. The effect is non-monotonic: too little or too much link weight adaptation is less effective than an intermediate level. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG) and studies the coevolution of strategies and link weights (not explicit punishment or sanctions). The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings may be contextually informative for understanding how dynamic network structures or adaptive relationships can promote cooperation, which could be relevant for models where network adaptation or endogenous partner selection is a feature. For the core prediction task, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

- source: Vukov, J. (2014). Accurate reactions open up the way for more cooperative societies. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that agents with more accurate and detailed memory (mem-1 strategies) can achieve much higher levels of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma than agents with only reactive memory. This effect is robust across a wide range of temptation-to-defect values, provided that the copying process is accurate (low error/noise). When copying errors increase, cooperation collapses. The paper interprets these results as showing that cognitive sophistication enables more stable and widespread cooperation, which in turn is associated with higher average payoffs, though payoff is not the primary outcome. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning institution; instead, retaliation and forgiveness are embedded in the strategy space.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms. Instead, it analyzes how cognitive sophistication in memory and strategy (allowing for retaliation and forgiveness) affects cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling accurate, context-sensitive responses (such as retaliation or forgiveness) can promote cooperation and, by implication, higher payoffs, but this is not directly mapped to efficiency or to institutional punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the role of memory, strategy complexity, and error in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., Paiva, A., & Santos, F. C. (2017). Structural power and the evolution of collective fairness in social networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the structural power of a network—a measure of how often individuals appear in each other's groups—strongly predicts the evolution of fairer strategies in Multiplayer Ultimatum Games. Networks with high SP (such as regular rings or modular community structures) promote higher average offers and acceptance thresholds, leading to more equitable outcomes. The mechanism is that repeated interactions within overlapping groups allow individuals to exert more influence and enforce fairness, analogous to effective punishment. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral measures of fairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in public-goods-like environments with punishment, by showing that network structure (specifically, high structural power) can promote fairer behavioral outcomes in group bargaining games. However, because the model is based on the Multiplayer Ultimatum Game (not a public goods game), does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that structural features enabling repeated peer influence may enhance the effectiveness of fairness enforcement (analogous to punishment), but do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other design dimensions.

  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (helping behavior) among union members is sustained not by explicit punishment, perfect monitoring, or reputation, but by a focal-point equilibrium where members help others in expectation of reciprocal help when they need it. Most members leave the union after their own disputes are resolved, indicating that helping is conditional and self-interested rather than purely altruistic. The equilibrium is supported by direct observation and mutual monitoring during joint activities, but not by formal sanctions or payoff-based incentives. The paper provides qualitative evidence and a theoretical model, but does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It provides qualitative, contextual evidence about how cooperation can be sustained in a real-world OLG setting with limited observability, but does not report or manipulate efficiency, payoff, or formal punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the range of mechanisms that can support cooperation in loosely knit groups, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Glowacki, L., & von Rueden, C. (2015). Leadership solves collective action problems in small-scale societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in both the Tsimane and Nyangatom societies, leaders tend to have more 'capital' (age, strength, social connections), and that leadership is more likely to become institutionalized in larger, denser, or more conflict-prone communities. Leadership is associated with improved group coordination and conflict mediation, and leaders often incur higher costs (e.g., risk in battle, time spent mediating). However, there is no direct evidence that leaders or leadership increase group efficiency or total payoffs in a way that maps onto public goods game outcomes. The paper notes that leader-based monitoring and sanctioning may reduce free-riding and improve the efficiency of reward or punishment allocation, but this is discussed in general terms and not measured as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for understanding the social and ecological conditions under which leadership, monitoring, and sanctioning emerge to solve collective action problems. It provides indirect support for the idea that leadership (and by extension, centralized punishment or monitoring) can improve group coordination and reduce free-riding, especially as group size and conflict increase. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the efficiency impact of punishment in controlled game environments, this paper offers only contextual and theoretical guidance, not direct empirical input.

- source: Simpson, B., Harrell, A., & Willer, R. (2013). Hidden Paths from Morality to Cooperation: Moral Judgments Promote Trust and Trustworthiness. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, the authors show that (1) making moral judgments of others' immoral behavior increases subsequent trustworthiness in a trust game (measured by amount returned), (2) this effect is mediated by increased self-perceived moral identity, and (3) observers trust and prefer to interact with strong moral judges, perceiving them as more moral and trustworthy. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level trust and trustworthiness, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The design does not include public goods games, explicit punishment, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that making moral judgments (not material punishment) can increase trustworthiness in trust games, and that observers trust moral judges more. However, there is no manipulation of punishment, no public goods game, and no group payoff or efficiency outcome. The results may be relevant for understanding how non-material social judgments can affect prosocial behavior, but they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2016). How institutions shaped the last major evolutionary transition to large-scale human societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that the ability to create and enforce institutional rules, including sanctioning mechanisms, is a key driver of large-scale human cooperation and division of labor. Institutions can transform the default equilibrium of social dilemmas (such as public goods games) to more cooperative, higher-payoff outcomes by enabling coordination, information sharing, and self-enforcing sanctioning. Theoretical arguments and historical/anthropological examples are used to support the claim that institutions can increase group efficiency and welfare, but the paper does not provide new empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms within an institutional framework can increase efficiency and group payoffs in public-goods-like environments, especially as group size increases. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes as a function of game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value for prediction is in supporting the plausibility and mechanism of positive effects of institutions (including punishment) on efficiency, but it does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of these effects.

- source: Forber, P., & Smead, R. (2016). The Evolution of Spite, Recognition, and Morality. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that mechanisms enabling recognition and conditional behavior can support both altruism and spite, but with important asymmetries. Recognition based on exogenous signals (e.g., greenbeard effects) is more evolutionarily stable with spite than with altruism, because mimics threaten altruism more than spite. In contrast, reciprocity based on past behavior (as in repeated games) is more conducive to the evolution of altruism than spite. The models show that reciprocal altruism evolves readily in repeated games, while reciprocal spite is rare. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, but rather the evolutionary stability and frequency of these behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its models are adjacent, focusing on the evolutionary conditions for the emergence and stability of conditional harming (spite) and helping (altruism) behaviors, and the coevolution of recognition mechanisms. While it discusses repeated games and conditional strategies, it does not report or predict group efficiency, welfare, or payoff outcomes. The main relevance is conceptual: it suggests that the evolutionary stability of punishment-like behaviors depends on the mechanism of recognition and the type of conditionality (signal-based vs. reciprocity), but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency effects in PGG-like games.

- source: McNally, L., & Jackson, A. L. (2013). Cooperation creates selection for tactical deception. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that conditional cooperation mechanisms (including punishment and reward) can select for the evolution of tactical deception, as deceivers can exploit conditional cooperators by hiding their defection. Theoretical results show that, under certain conditions (notably negative frequency dependence in deception detection), cooperation and deception can coexist at equilibrium. Comparative analysis in primates supports the prediction that more cooperative species exhibit more tactical deception. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its theoretical model is based on the iterated prisoner's dilemma and focuses on the evolutionary dynamics of deception and cooperation, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. While it discusses punishment as one of several conditional cooperation mechanisms, it does not analyze the quantitative impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the idea that enforcement mechanisms can have complex evolutionary consequences, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Sterelny, K. (2016). Cooperation, Culture, and Conflict. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation evolved in two major transitions: first, among Pleistocene foragers, where cooperation was individually advantageous and stabilized by mutualism, reciprocation, and punishment of freeriders (both shirkers and bullies); and second, in the Holocene, where larger, more hierarchical societies required new mechanisms (including group selection and intergroup conflict) to maintain cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a key mechanism for controlling freeriding and maintaining cooperation, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than experimental or quantitative. The paper references experimental public goods games and their findings (e.g., punishment stabilizes cooperation), but only as background for theoretical arguments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and norm enforcement may affect cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. However, it supports the idea that punishment is more likely to stabilize cooperation (and thus efficiency) in small, transparent, repeated-interaction groups, and that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on social structure, group size, and transparency. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as background for mechanism hypotheses rather than as a source of direct predictive evidence.

  findings: The study finds that sustained interagency cooperation in public policy (here, food security in Bogotá) depends on (1) strong sponsorship (e.g., from central government or consensus), (2) stability of staff in the coordination system, and (3) mechanisms to reward or punish collaboration levels. The absence of these factors leads to decreased collaboration. However, the findings are qualitative and do not include quantitative measures of efficiency or payoff.

It is adjacent to the prediction task, as it discusses the importance of reward and punishment mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in real-world, multi-agent institutional settings, and draws on sociobiological theory. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main value is in supporting the general idea that mechanisms for reward and punishment can help sustain cooperation, especially when other supports (like sponsorship or staff stability) are present. It does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Yukalov, V. I., Yukalova, E. P., & Sornette, D. (2013). Utility Rate Equations of Group Population Dynamics in Biological and Social Systems. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The authors develop a system-theoretic, utility-based model for the evolution of group fractions (cooperators, defectors, regulators) in self-organized societies. They show analytically that, under resource constraints, neither defectors nor regulators can exceed about 10% of the population in stable equilibrium. The model demonstrates that cooperation can persist without explicit punishment mechanisms, provided resources are finite. When regulators (punishers) are present, their fraction is also limited by resource costs. The model is applied to real-world data from countries, but only to estimate group fractions and stability, not efficiency or payoff. No direct predictions or measurements of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus are made.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental designs. It offers a theoretical perspective that, in resource-constrained societies, both defectors and regulators (punishers) are limited in equilibrium, and cooperation can be stable even without explicit punishment. However, the model does not map onto the game design dimensions or efficiency outcomes used in experimental PGG research. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may provide indirect, high-level context about the limits of punishment and defection in self-organized systems.

- source: Fagundes, D. (2017). The Social Norms of Waiting in Line. *Law and Social Inquiry-journal of the American Bar Foundation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the stability of waiting lines among strangers is best explained by the human tendency toward strong reciprocity: people are inclined to cooperate when they see others doing so and to punish norm violators, even at personal cost. It draws on public goods game experiments to support this model, noting that people will punish defectors even when it is costly. The paper describes how informal sanctions (verbal objections, shaming, and sometimes violence) deter line-cutting and maintain order. It also discusses cultural and contextual variation in queue norms, exceptions to the rules, and the role of service providers and technology. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoffs, nor does it manipulate or systematically analyze game design dimensions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for the role of punishment and social norms in sustaining cooperation in environments analogous to public goods games, such as physical queues. It supports the idea that informal sanctions can deter defection and promote norm compliance, even among strangers. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may inform mechanistic reasoning about why punishment can sustain cooperation, but it does not provide empirical data or parameter estimates relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

- source: Artavia-Mora, L., Bedi, A. S., & Rieger, M. (2017). Intuitive help and punishment in the field. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that time delay (deliberation) reduces the likelihood of helping strangers and norm violators in the field, with helping rates dropping by 18-27 percentage points depending on the scenario. However, time delay does not significantly affect the rate of direct punishment of norm violators. The study also finds that time delay increases self-reported fear of retaliation for punishing. The design is a one-shot, non-strategic, individual-level field experiment, not a public goods game or its direct variant, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar strategic environments. Its design lacks group interaction, repeated rounds, contribution choices, or payoff structures analogous to PGGs. The main outcomes are binary helping or punishment actions in response to norm violations, not group payoffs or efficiency. While the findings may be contextually informative about the psychological underpinnings of prosocial and punitive behavior, they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. The study is best used as contextual or mechanistic background, not as direct input for efficiency prediction models.

- source: Kaltwasser, L., Hildebrandt, A., Wilhelm, O., & Sommer, W. (2016). Behavioral and neuronal determinants of negative reciprocity in the ultimatum game. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both prosociality (as measured by social value orientation) and assertiveness predict the likelihood of rejecting unfair offers in the ultimatum game, supporting both the strong reciprocity and status defense models of negative reciprocity. Neural data (feedback negativity) show that individuals with higher negative reciprocity have stronger neural responses to proposer fairness. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. The findings are limited to the behavioral and neural correlates of negative reciprocity in the UG, not to efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is an ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and the main outcome is the rejection rate of unfair offers (a form of costly punishment), not group payoff or efficiency. While the study is relevant for understanding individual motivations for costly punishment (negative reciprocity), it does not inform how punishment affects group efficiency or payoff under different game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be weighted strongly for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Vromen, J. (2017). Ultimate and proximate explanations of strong reciprocity. *History and Philosophy of the Life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that advocates of strong reciprocity (SR) treat SR as a proximate mechanism for human cooperation, not as an ultimate explanation. It clarifies misunderstandings in the literature about the explanatory role of SR, especially in relation to public goods experiments with punishment. The paper discusses how gene-culture co-evolutionary theory is used to provide ultimate explanations for the evolution of SR, while neuroeconomics is invoked for proximate explanations. It does not present new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but references existing experimental work to illustrate the existence and behavioral effects of SR.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is useful for understanding the conceptual and explanatory framework surrounding punishment and cooperation, and for clarifying that the presence of punishment in public goods experiments is interpreted as evidence for strong reciprocity as a proximate mechanism. However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative effects on efficiency, payoff, or welfare, and thus offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Clavien, C., & Chapuisat, M. (2013). Altruism across disciplines: one word, multiple meanings. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the term 'altruism' is used inconsistently across disciplines, leading to confusion and overstated claims, especially when transferring findings between fields like experimental economics and evolutionary biology. It argues for a clear taxonomy distinguishing psychological, reproductive, behavioural, and preference altruism. The authors show that much of the experimental economics literature (including public goods games with punishment) is best understood in terms of behavioural or preference altruism, not reproductive altruism. The paper cautions against conflating outcome-based and motivation-based definitions, and against assuming that findings about punishment or cooperation in economic games directly inform evolutionary or moral questions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it is useful for clarifying that many studies in experimental economics (including those on punishment in public goods games) are about behavioural or preference altruism, and that their outcomes (such as increased cooperation or punishment behavior) should not be conflated with broader evolutionary or moral claims. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that careful attention should be paid to the definitions and outcome measures used in empirical studies, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are distinct from measures of cooperation or punishment frequency.

- source: Kim, M., & Sivan, E. W. Z. (2017). Faking It Is Hard to Do: Entrepreneurial Norm Enforcement and Suspicions of Deviance. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when norm enforcement is mandated (i.e., actors are required to respond to deviance), enforcers are perceived as more sincere and less likely to be suspected of ulterior motives, even in high-accusability contexts. In contrast, when enforcement is entrepreneurial (i.e., voluntary and not mandated) and the context is high accusability (where actors have reason to fear being accused of deviance), enforcers are viewed with greater suspicion and are more likely to be seen as having ulterior motives. In low-accusability contexts, entrepreneurial enforcement does not arouse suspicion. The study does not measure or report on efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about social perceptions of norm enforcers, not about behavioral or payoff consequences of punishment. The design is adjacent to PGG-like environments in that it involves norm enforcement and public goods logic, but it is not an economic game and does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational moderators of norm enforcement.

- source: Waldeck, R. (2013). Segregated Cooperation. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with internalized norm adherence (modeled as a private emotional cost for defecting), local interaction structures (small neighborhoods) can lead to persistent, segregated clusters of cooperators, resulting in higher average fitness (payoff) for cooperators compared to defectors. This effect is robust to various simulation parameters (error rate, number of active players, population size) but is highly sensitive to the distribution of emotional adherence and the cost of cooperation. When the basin of attraction for defection is large (e.g., high cost of cooperation or unfavorable emotion distribution), cooperation collapses even in local structures. No explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; all norm enforcement is via internalized emotion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or PD games, as it does not include any explicit punishment mechanism. Instead, it models norm adherence as an internalized emotional cost. The findings are relevant for understanding how local interaction structure and heterogeneity in norm adherence can affect efficiency and cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the efficiency impact of punishment interventions, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in environments with internalized norm enforcement and local interactions.

- source: Panchanathan, K., Frankenhuis, W. E., & Silk, J. B. (2013). The bystander effect in an N-person dictator game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds a strong bystander effect in an N-person dictator game: when there is only one dictator, recipients receive significantly more than when there are two or three dictators. The average transfer as a fraction of the 'equal share' drops from 57% (one dictator) to 36% (two or three dictators). Recipient payoffs are about 50% higher in the one-dictator condition than in the two- or three-dictator conditions. This effect persists even when uncertainty about others' actions is removed (strategy method) and when dictators can communicate and coordinate (bargaining method). The results suggest that most participants transform the situation into a prisoner's dilemma, leading to low transfers in groups. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but discusses the possibility that introducing punishment could alleviate the bystander effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the number of potential contributors in a many-to-one helping game (adjacent to PGG) reduces efficiency/welfare, even when communication or strategy methods are used. The absence of punishment or sanctions means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does establish a robust baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency declines with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar games without punishment, efficiency will be lower as group size increases. It also suggests that communication alone may not be sufficient to restore efficiency. However, since punishment is not present, the paper cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, only to set a control baseline.

- source: Liu, G. F., Lin, C. D., & Xin, Z. Q. (2014). The Effects of Within- and Between-Group Competition on Trust and Trustworthiness among Acquaintances. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both within-group and between-group competition increase trust (investment) among acquaintances in an investment game. However, within-group competition reduces trustworthiness (return ratio) and leads to lower payoffs for trustors but higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trustee payoffs but does not significantly affect trustworthiness or trustor payoffs compared to the non-competition condition. The results suggest that competition type differentially affects efficiency for trustors and trustees, and that within-group competition can undermine overall group efficiency by harming trustor payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, introducing within-group competition can increase trust but reduce trustworthiness, leading to lower payoffs for trustors and higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trust and trustee payoffs but does not harm trustor payoffs. Since the study does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that competitive structures can have asymmetric effects on efficiency depending on the role. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence or absence of competition (and its type) can moderate efficiency outcomes, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs.

- source: McClung, J. S., Placì, S., Bangerter, A., Clément, F., & Bshary, R. (2017). The language of cooperation: shared intentionality drives variation in helping as a function of group membership. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper introduces a joint foraging game (the 'egg hunt') in which pairs of participants can help each other at a cost to themselves, with helping not explicitly instructed. The design manipulates group membership (in-group vs out-group) and the ability to talk (talking vs no talking). Both in-group membership and the ability to talk significantly increased costly helping and overall group success (total screws collected, which determined monetary payoffs). Shared intentionality in conversation was identified as the key psychological mechanism mediating the effect of group membership on helping. Costly helping was costly to the helper but increased total group payoff. There were no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The game is adjacent to public goods or prisoner's dilemma games, but differs in that helping is not explicitly structured and there is no punishment or reward system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling communication (chat) and making group membership salient can increase efficiency (total group payoff) in joint action games where helping is possible but not enforced. The effect is mediated by shared intentionality in communication. While the game is not a standard public goods game and does not include punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games where communication and group identity are manipulated. The absence of punishment means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that communication alone can have a strong positive effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of chat/communication and group identity, but does not provide evidence about punishment, punishment cost, or related dimensions.

- source: Lewis, H. M., Vinicius, L., Strods, J., Mace, R., & Migliano, A. B. (2014). High mobility explains demand sharing and enforced cooperation in egalitarian hunter-gatherers. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The simulation demonstrates that enforced demand sharing (forced equal sharing of food among all camp members) combined with high mobility enables populations to survive in unpredictable environments, maintain high group efficiency (measured by survival, energy surplus, and life expectancy), and buffer against the presence of free riders. In contrast, loner (non-sharing) populations and sedentary (non-mobile) demand-sharing populations are much more likely to collapse, especially under harsh environmental conditions. The model explicitly excludes punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, showing that high efficiency and cooperation can be maintained without punishment, as long as mobility is high. The findings are directly relevant to understanding the efficiency of enforced cooperation in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the effect of adding punishment to a public-goods-like environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as punishment is not present in the model. Instead, it shows that enforced cooperation (demand sharing) combined with high mobility can maintain high efficiency and group survival without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task—predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency—this paper suggests that, in some environments, enforced cooperation and mobility may substitute for punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence on the marginal effect of punishment. The model is adjacent to PGGs, but the absence of a punishment condition means it cannot be used to predict the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Chiang, Y. S. (2015). Good Samaritans in Networks: An Experiment on How Networks Influence Egalitarian Sharing and the Evolution of Inequality. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that voluntary sharing in networks can reduce inequality, but the extent depends on network topology. When networks connect the very rich and very poor (Lattice_Hetero, SF_Negative), local inequality is salient and motivates more redistribution, leading to significant reductions in the Gini coefficient. In other network structures (Full, Lattice_Homo, SF_Positive), inequality is not significantly reduced. The study does not involve public goods provision, punishment, or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency as the ratio of group payoff to the fully cooperative benchmark. The findings are most relevant to how network structure moderates voluntary redistribution and the evolution of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in the sense required for the downstream prediction task. The findings are about voluntary redistribution and inequality in networked settings, which is adjacent but not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The paper may provide some contextual insight into how network structure affects redistribution, but it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Grujic, J., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Traulsen, A., Cuesta, J. A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2014). A comparative analysis of spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments: Conditional cooperation and payoff irrelevance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments on lattices, cooperation rates start high but quickly decline to low, nonzero levels (15-35%). (2) Defectors consistently earn more than cooperators, with a significant negative correlation between cooperation and earnings. (3) The behavioral rule most consistent with the data is moody conditional cooperation, where players' probability to cooperate depends on their own previous action and the number of cooperating neighbors. (4) There is no evidence that spatial structure (network topology, number of neighbors, or system size) promotes higher cooperation or changes the behavioral rule. (5) Payoff-based imitation rules (imitate-the-best, Fermi rule) do not explain the observed behavior. (6) The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions, and does not report group efficiency relative to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates and group payoffs remain low in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games, and that spatial structure alone does not increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper may help establish a baseline for control (no-punishment) efficiency in spatial PD-like environments, but offers no information about the effect of enabling punishment. The behavioral findings (moody conditional cooperation) may inform models of baseline behavior in the absence of incentives.

- source: Molleman, L., Pen, I., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Effects of Conformism on the Cultural Evolution of Social Behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of conformism on the evolution of cooperation depends on the game structure. In social dilemmas, conformism can make cooperation a stable equilibrium if it is already common, but in group-structured populations, conformism hinders the spread of cooperation because it prevents rare cooperative strategies from invading. In evasion games, conformism erodes polymorphic equilibria and leads to fixation of a single strategy. The main outcomes are fixation probabilities and average payoffs at equilibrium, which are related to efficiency. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms, and does not directly model public goods games, but the results are relevant for understanding how social learning biases affect group-level cooperation and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how social learning rules (payoff-based vs. conformist) affect the spread and stability of cooperation and group payoffs in two-strategy games, including social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that mechanisms which stabilize cooperation (such as conformism) may not always increase group efficiency, especially in group-structured populations. However, since punishment is not modeled, and the game structure is not a PGG or its direct variant, this paper should be considered only for background or mechanistic context, not for quantitative prediction.

- source: Horiuchi, S. (2015). Emergence and Collapse of the Norm of Resource Sharing Around Locally Abundant Resources. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that workers' effort (reciprocity) increases with their own wage (direct reciprocity) and, when their own wage is unobservable, also with the coworker's wage (indirect reciprocity via signaling). When both wages are observable, the coworker's wage has no effect on effort, indicating that indirect reciprocity is driven by signaling about one's own treatment, not by altruism toward the coworker. There is no evidence that inequality aversion explains the observed patterns. The main outcome is effort, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent in that it studies reciprocity and information in a gift-exchange game, but the main outcome is effort, not efficiency, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings may inform predictions about the role of information and signaling in reciprocity, but not about punishment or efficiency. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency is limited.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Hoffman, M., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Uncalculating cooperation is used to signal trustworthiness. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that uncalculating cooperation is used as a signal of trustworthiness: participants are more likely to cooperate without calculating costs when their decision process is observable to others, and such behavior leads to greater trust and actual trustworthiness in a subsequent trust game. The findings support a reputation-based account of uncalculating cooperation. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in public goods or similar group cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about reputation and uncalculating cooperation may be of theoretical interest for understanding motivations for cooperation, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Blake, P. R., Rand, D. G., Tingley, D., & Warneken, F. (2015). The shadow of the future promotes cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma for children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Children aged 10-11 cooperate significantly more in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games than in one-shot games, demonstrating sensitivity to the 'shadow of the future' and the potential for direct reciprocity. Conditional cooperation strategies are observed, with girls and children with fewer conduct problems being more forgiving and cooperative. The study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how repeated interaction (direct reciprocity) increases cooperation in dyadic games among children, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Thus, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the prediction task, mainly by illustrating that repeated interaction alone (without punishment) can increase cooperation rates in dyadic settings.

- source: Capraro, V., & Cococcioni, G. (2015). Social setting, intuition and experience in laboratory experiments interact to shape cooperative decision-making. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, among Indian participants (a population presumed to have internalized non-cooperative strategies), promoting intuition (via time pressure) versus deliberation (via time delay) does not affect cooperation rates among inexperienced subjects. However, experienced subjects (those with prior exposure to economic games) are more cooperative than inexperienced ones, but only under time pressure. The main dependent variable is the percentage of endowment transferred in a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a two-person continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma without punishment or reward options. The findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, group payoffs) are low in non-cooperative settings without institutional support or experience, and that experience with economic games can increase cooperation under intuitive conditions. However, since no punishment mechanism is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. It may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation levels in non-cooperative settings.

- source: Romano, A., Balliet, D., Yamagishi, T., & Liu, J. H. (2017). Parochial trust and cooperation across 17 societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people cooperate more with ingroup than outgroup members in a trust game, but reputational concern (common knowledge of group membership) increases cooperation with both ingroup and outgroup members. Men show slightly more ingroup favoritism than women, but social value orientation predicts general cooperation rather than parochialism. There is little cross-societal variation in ingroup favoritism, and this variation is not explained by material security, religiosity, or pathogen stress. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how reputational concerns (a form of indirect social incentive) can increase cooperation, but not for payoff-based or punishment-based interventions. Its design and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, offering only indirect context for how social information and group identity affect cooperation.

- source: Dunning, D., Anderson, J. E., Schlösser, T., Ehlebracht, D., & Fetchenhauer, D. (2014). Trust at Zero Acquaintance: More a Matter of Respect Than Expectation of Reward. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that trust in zero-acquaintance trust games is driven more by internalized moral norms (especially a norm of respect) than by expectations of reward or risk tolerance. Participants report that they 'should' trust more than they 'want' to, and this sense of obligation predicts trust behavior. Making trust decisions public does not increase trust, suggesting the norm is moral rather than social. When the trust game is altered so that the recipient's character is not at stake (e.g., outcome determined by coin flip), trust rates drop. The paper does not study public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, and thus does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that in trust games without punishment, trust rates can be high due to internalized moral norms, even when efficiency or payoff maximization would not predict such behavior. The findings are only indirectly relevant to public goods games, and not at all relevant to punishment or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., Lergetporer, P., & Sutter, M. (2016). Cooperation and discrimination within and across language borders: Evidence from children in a bilingual city. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that children cooperate more with in-group members (same class), less with out-group members of the same language, and least with out-group members of a different language. Both in-group favoritism and language group discrimination are observed, with the latter emerging mainly among older German-speaking children. Cooperation increases with age, and girls cooperate more than boys. Beliefs about partner cooperation are higher for in-group partners and are positively correlated with own cooperation. No punishment, sanctioning, or reward mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods or prisoner's dilemma games. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. However, it provides contextual evidence on baseline cooperation rates and discrimination in the absence of punishment, which may be relevant for understanding control conditions in similar environments. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot inform the treatment effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: Across four lab experiments, participants induced to adopt a Unity moral motive (via framing or subliminal priming) allocated more money to another participant in a one-shot risk-sharing game than those induced to adopt a Proportionality motive. The findings are about the psychological determinants of solidarity behavior (unconditional giving) in risk-sharing contexts.

  findings: The study finds that in one-shot, anonymous ultimatum games, proposer offers vary significantly across villages, but responder behavior is highly uniform: almost all offers are accepted, and responders' self-reported minimum acceptable offers are very low. There is no evidence for cultural conformity or coordinated expectations at the ethno-linguistic group level. The findings suggest that local environmental factors, rather than group-level fairness norms, may drive variation in bargaining behavior. The study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff.

  findings: The paper finds that the equilibrium level of cooperation in a population depends on the distribution of agents' emotional parameters (guilt and especially betrayal). Increasing the number of agents with a lower betrayal factor does not always increase cooperation; there are critical points where the population becomes robust to further increases. The models show that the equilibrium cooperation rate can be predicted from the distribution of emotional types, but the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment. The findings are relevant for understanding how emotional payoffs shape cooperation in social dilemmas, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

Its findings on how emotional payoffs (guilt, betrayal) affect cooperation rates in 2x2 games may be contextually informative for understanding the role of psychological factors in social dilemmas, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency. The paper is best used as background on the impact of non-material payoffs on cooperation, not as quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with limited resources, lower total resource and intermediate temptation to defect can promote higher average cooperation (measured by the average characteristic parameter, CP). The number of dead individuals decreases with increasing resource, and the diversity of strategies is affected by both resource and temptation to defect. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are primarily about the evolution of cooperation and survival under resource constraints.

  findings: The paper finds that dynamically adjusting the intensity of interaction based on individual reputation and evaluation level can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial iterated prisoner's dilemma. The mechanism works by creating positive feedback between reputation and effective neighbors, facilitating the formation and expansion of cooperator clusters. However, the study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

  findings: The study finds that allowing players to break and re-form connections based on their satisfaction with payoffs (partner choice) leads to much higher levels of cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma, and similar effects are observed in the snowdrift and stag-hunt games.   findings: The study finds that increasing the coupling factor between networks generally impedes cooperation in the classical prisoner's dilemma, while the entanglement degree in the quantum game can promote the quantum strategy (Q) and, to some extent, overall cooperation. The coupling factor and entanglement degree interact in complex ways to determine the stationary fractions of strategies, but the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. There is no analysis of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing moderate irrational sentiment (either overestimating or underestimating one's own payoff) can increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest for moderate values of the irrationality parameter θ, with both positive (overconfidence) and negative (self-abasement) values promoting cooperation up to a point. Excessive irrationality in either direction reduces cooperation. The findings are based on simulations of strategy evolution on a lattice, with no explicit payoff or efficiency outcomes reported. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.

- source: Swakman, V., Molleman, L., Ule, A., & Egas, M. (2016). Reputation-based cooperation: empirical evidence for behavioral strategies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that people condition their helping decisions on both first-order (recipient's past helping) and second-order (context of recipient's past helping/defection) information. Donors are especially interested in 2nd-order information about defection, and tend to reward 'justified defection' (denying help to defectors). There is strong individual heterogeneity: some use only first-order information, some use both, and a minority are unconditional cooperators or defectors. The availability of 2nd-order information (free or costly) does not significantly increase overall helping rates compared to the control, though helping rates are numerically higher when 2nd-order information is available. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The game is an iterated indirect helping game with binary helping decisions, and the main outcomes are behavioral (helping rates, information use, strategy classification). While the findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms of reputation-based cooperation, they do not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The only indirect relevance is that richer information about others' behavior can (non-significantly) increase helping rates, but this is not about efficiency or payoff, nor about punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., Smyth, C., Mylona, K., & Niblo, G. A. (2014). Benevolent Characteristics Promote Cooperative Behaviour among Humans. *Plos One*.

  findings: The paper finds that (1) benevolence—defined as unilaterally increasing another's payoff beyond one's own—exists in the population, (2) individuals who are more benevolent are more likely to cooperate in a subsequent one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, and (3) priming participants to think benevolently increases their cooperation rate compared to priming malevolence. The study uses custom BT games and standard two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, but does not include public goods games, punishment, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about benevolence and cooperation may be contextually relevant for understanding individual factors that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

  findings: The study finds that participants are more likely to help ingroup than outgroup members, and more likely to help when decisions are public (i.e., reputational concerns are present).   findings: The paper finds that increasing the weight of other-regarding preferences (alpha) promotes cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with synchronous updating. The study analyzes the micro-dynamics of strategy switching but does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.

  findings: The study finds that the effect of noise (randomness in strategy updating) on cooperation and spatial assortment differs between the spatial prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games.
