Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Capraro, V., & Marcelletti, A. (2014). Do good actions inspire good actions in others?. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that good actions (altruism or benevolence) do not inspire further good actions in anonymous, one-shot economic games. Specifically, being the recipient of a good action (either a split in a Dictator Game or a benevolent act in a Benevolence Game) does not significantly increase the probability of cooperating in a subsequent Prisoner's Dilemma or splitting in a subsequent Dictator Game. The results are robust to exclusion of participants who did not believe the other player was real or failed to provide a valid reason for their choice. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on the lack of behavioral contagion of good actions in one-shot, anonymous settings may be contextually relevant for understanding baseline cooperation or altruism, but not for the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

- source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2013). Cooperation during cultural group formation promotes trust towards members of out-groups. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the degree of cooperation required during group formation (cooperative vs. independent production tasks) causally affects trust and reciprocity in subsequent trust games. Specifically, participants from cooperative production groups (CP) trust and reciprocate with out-group members at similar rates as with in-group members, showing little parochial altruism. In contrast, participants from independent production groups (IP) display significant in-group favouritism and out-group discrimination. These effects persist even after negative experiences (betrayal) in the trust game. The results are explained by differences in beliefs about out-group trustworthiness, shaped by the group formation process. No efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that the cooperative context of group formation can influence trust and reciprocity, but not in a payoff or efficiency framework. It should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Lu, P. (2015). Individual choice and reputation distribution of cooperative behaviors among heterogeneous groups. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the mean of reputation (interpreted as a proxy for cooperation) declines with increasing temptation (b), and is negatively affected by the proportion of elites and positively by the proportion of scoundrels. The diversity (standard deviation) of reputation increases with temptation and is affected in a quadratic manner by the proportion of elites and the local cooperation ratio. However, the paper does not study public goods games, punishment, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. All results are based on simulation of a spatial prisoner's dilemma with three agent types and reputation updating.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings on reputation and cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with heterogeneous agents may be of limited adjacent interest for understanding micro-level cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task.

- source: van Apeldoorn, J., & Schram, A. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity; A Field Experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper provides field evidence that downstream indirect reciprocity operates in an online community: members with a history of helping others are more likely to have their own requests for help accepted. This effect is robust across most subgroups. There is no evidence for upstream indirect reciprocity. The study does not involve a public goods game or any form of punishment or sanctioning, nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and the experimental design is not a PGG or close variant. Its findings on indirect reciprocity may be of theoretical interest for understanding cooperation, but provide no direct or indirect evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Epstein, Z. G. (2014). Risking Your Life without a Second Thought: Intuitive Decision-Making and Extreme Altruism. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who engage in extreme altruism overwhelmingly describe their decision-making as intuitive, fast, and automatic, rather than deliberative or reasoned. This is supported both by human ratings of interview statements and by linguistic analysis of inhibitory language. The results are robust even in cases where the altruist had time to deliberate. The study does not involve any economic games, public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions of interest (e.g., player count, punishment, MPCR, etc.), nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about intuitive decision-making in real-world extreme altruism may be of theoretical interest for understanding the psychology of cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Buckholtz, J. W., Martin, J. W., Treadway, M. T., Jan, K., Zald, D. H., Jones, O., & Marois, R. (2015). From Blame to Punishment: Disrupting Prefrontal Cortex Activity Reveals Norm Enforcement Mechanisms. *Neuron*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC) plays a selective, causal role in punishment decision-making for norm violations. Disrupting DLPFC function via rTMS reduces the amount of punishment assigned to culpable agents, especially for low- to moderate-harm crimes, but does not affect blameworthiness ratings. fMRI results show that DLPFC activity is higher during punishment decisions than during blameworthiness judgments. The study supports an integration-and-selection model in which DLPFC integrates information about harm and culpability to determine punishment. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any economic game with payoffs, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not use such games or report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding the cognitive and neural mechanisms of punishment assignment in norm enforcement, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency or group payoff in economic games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context about punishment, but not for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

- source: Kasper, C., & Mulder, M. B. (2015). Who Helps and Why? Cooperative Networks in Mpimbwe. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that in a rural Tanzanian village, helping behavior is generally low and is best predicted by reciprocity (contingency), with kinship and affinal relationships also playing roles. Wealthier individuals are less likely to participate in reciprocal helping relationships and receive less help. Geographic proximity and age also influence helping patterns. The study discusses the erosion of traditional cooperation networks in the context of increasing market integration and inequality. Witchcraft accusations are mentioned as a form of social sanction, but there is no formal punishment system or experimental manipulation of sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or any formal game, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. The study is observational, not experimental, and focuses on naturalistic helping behavior, not on the effects of introducing or varying punishment mechanisms. Witchcraft accusations are discussed as a form of social pressure, but not in a way that is structurally or functionally equivalent to PGG punishment treatments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some adjacent context about the role of social sanctions in real-world cooperation, but not in a way that is mappable to the target prediction problem.

- source: Exadaktylos, F., Espín, A. M., & Brañas-Garza, P. (2013). Experimental subjects are not different. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both student status and self-selection (volunteering) can have small effects on social preference behaviors, but these effects are generally minor and not economically significant. Self-selected students (the typical lab subject pool) behave very similarly to the broader population in Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. The only significant differences are small and mainly concern strategic behavior and trust/reciprocity among non-students. The results support the external validity of using self-selected students for social preference experiments, but do not address public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any public goods game, punishment, or sanctioning treatment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are only relevant to the representativeness of subject pools in social preference experiments using Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Manesi, Z., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Pollet, T. V. (2015). Butterfly Eyespots: Their Potential Influence on Aesthetic Preferences and Conservation Attitudes. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that minimal eye-like patterns (eyespots) on butterflies increase their perceived attractiveness and elicit more positive attitudes toward their conservation. In some cases, eyespots also increase the likelihood of actual conservation behavior (e.g., donations), though this effect is mixed. The effect of eyespots on conservation attitudes is mediated by increased aesthetic preference. The study does not address economic games, punishment, or group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games, nor does it report payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are not transferable to the prediction task at hand.

- source: Arbia, A., & Carbonnier, G. (2016). Human nature and development aid: IR and the biology of altruism. *Journal of International Relations and Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none

  findings: The study finds that ODA as a share of GNI is persistent over time and increases with donor country wealth and government spending, supporting the idea that altruism may be partially hardwired. There is mixed evidence for the influence of cultural or institutional factors (nurture), such as income inequality and political competition. The analysis is at the country-policy level and does not involve experimental games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.

It does not report on game design dimensions, control or treatment efficiency, or any related laboratory or field experiments. Its findings on macro-level ODA policy and the nature of altruism are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in public-goods-game-like settings.

- source: Cai, W., Huang, X. Q., Wu, S., & Kou, Y. (2015). Dishonest behavior is not affected by an image of watching eyes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that implicit reputation cues (images of watching eyes) do not reduce dishonest behavior in laboratory settings, even when dishonesty leads to higher monetary payoffs or reputational benefits. This null effect was consistent across different tasks and motivational framings. The study also finds that higher moral identity predicts less dishonesty, but the eyes image does not affect this. The pilot experiment replicates the known effect of eyes images on generosity in a dictator game, but the main experiments focus on dishonesty, not cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. The findings about implicit reputation cues and dishonesty may be of tangential interest for understanding the limits of non-punitive interventions, but do not inform the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.
